# The research band

The programme map splits the 137 nodes closest to the alleged programme into seven bands, and this file is the research band: 38 nodes, taken era by era from the earliest to the most recent. Each entry gives what the node is, why the board places it here, why it holds the tier it holds, what it has denied, its documented and testimonial claims with their sources, and the links that tie it to the rest of the board.

Names in full, for this file:

- **UAPTF**: Unidentified Aerial Phenomena Task Force

# THROUGH 1969: Estimate through Blue Book

## National Aeronautics and Space Administration

National Aeronautics and Space Administration is a institution on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1958-07-29; axes C0 A1 D3; 15 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Civil space agency; host of the 2023 UAP Independent Study Team.

### What it is

The National Aeronautics and Space Administration, the U.S. civil space agency. Since 2022 it has been a formal participant in the government's UAP effort through a commissioned Independent Study Team, whose final report it published on 14 September 2023 alongside the appointment of a Director of UAP Research. Since 2026 it has been a contributing agency to the Presidential Unsealing and Reporting System for UAP Encounters, supplying Apollo- and Gemini-era imagery and astronaut accounts.

### Why it is implicated

NASA's role in the concealment architecture is not custody; it is certification. It is the civilian, scientifically credentialed institution that repeatedly states there is no evidence of extraterrestrial origin while holding, by its own design choices, no access to the compartmented material where the question would actually be settled, its Independent Study Team used unclassified data only, and its charter expressly excluded review of past incidents. The sharper fact is a custody claim. In 2025 NASA's Office of Communications told a records researcher that it has no UAP records qualifying for transfer to the National Archives under the FY2024 NDAA because "any agency-related records associated with UAP, came from, and are owned by, the U.S. Department of Defense." That single sentence removes NASA's UAP holdings from the statutory records regime by attributing ownership to the department least likely to release them. Ten months later NASA's own Administrator publicly identified NASA-provided Apollo 12 and Apollo 17 lunar-surface imagery as "some of the most interesting data that NASA has provided as part of the UAP disclosure." Both statements cannot sit comfortably together: an agency with nothing to transfer, holding the most interesting data.

### The longer account

NASA entered the modern UAP file formally in 2022, when it commissioned an outside Independent Study Team to advise on how the agency might contribute. The team's final report was published on 14 September 2023 under NASA release 23-106, chaired by David Spergel, president of the Simons Foundation, with sixteen members. Its overall recommendation was that NASA play a prominent role in the whole-of-government effort by using its open-source resources, technological expertise, data analysis techniques, federal and commercial partnerships, and Earth-observing assets to curate a better dataset for understanding future UAP. The report explicitly is not a review or assessment of previous UAP incidents. That boundary is the point: NASA's charter for the study excluded the historical record, which is where the legacy program question lives.

Spergel described the methodological constraint as a virtue: "Using unclassified data was essential for our team's fact-finding, open-communication collaboration, and for upholding scientific rigor to produce this report for NASA." The team reported that there are currently a limited number of high-quality observations of UAP, which make it impossible to draw firm scientific conclusions about their nature. The same architecture reappears three years later in the UAP Science Advisory Council chaired by Avi Loeb, unclassified data only, chartered to recommend how to get better data, structurally incapable of reaching a custody finding. NASA built the template.

With the report NASA named Mark McInerney its Director of UAP Research. McInerney had previously served as NASA's liaison to the Department of Defense "covering limited UAP activities for the agency", meaning the agency's UAP function had already existed as a DoD liaison billet before it was given a public title. Administrator Bill Nelson framed the role in terms of instrumentation and analytics: the director would apply "artificial intelligence and machine learning to search the skies for anomalies," and "NASA will do this work transparently for the benefit of humanity." McInerney's career runs through NASA's Goddard Space Flight Center, NOAA and the National Hurricane Center since 1996, a NOAA lineage he shares with Tim Gallaudet, the retired admiral now on the 2026 UAP Science Advisory Council.

Then came the records question, and with it the most consequential NASA statement in the public record. The FY2024 NDAA, sections 1841-1842, required federal agencies to identify and transfer UAP records to a new collection at the National Archives, with digital transfers due by 30 September 2025. Record Group 615 was created to hold them. Over the course of 2025 the FAA, the Nuclear Regulatory Commission, the National Security Agency, ODNI and the Office of the Secretary of Defense transferred records into the online catalog. NASA did not. Asked why, NASA's Office of Communications replied: "Per the requirements in Section 1841-1842 of the Fiscal Year 2024 National Defense Authorization Act for handling records related to unidentified anomalous phenomena (UAP), NASA does not have any records that meet the requirements to submit records to the National Archives. Any agency-related records associated with UAP, came from, and are owned by, the U.S. Department of Defense." That is not a denial of possession. It is an assertion of foreign ownership over material NASA holds, a jurisdictional argument that keeps agency UAP records outside a statutory public-release regime by routing their custody back into the Department of War.

The 2026 disclosure wave then produced the contradiction. On 27 May 2026, NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman told Fox News Digital that the newly declassified files show "real unexplained phenomena" but no recovered craft: "What's being surfaced isn't crashed ships or alien bodies, but real unexplained phenomena," and "There's nothing I'm aware of in terms of alien bodies or spaceships." Note the construction, personal awareness, from a political appointee without read-in to the compartments where such material would be held. In the same interview Isaacman said agencies "really didn't take this quite as seriously in the past until President Trump put out the tweet, basically giving an order to government agencies and going out and saying, look, this time you better go through the files, you better start searching your databases and bring things up", a sitting NASA Administrator stating that federal agencies did not conduct records searches for UAP material until a presidential post compelled them.

And on NASA's own contribution: "I would say that some of the most interesting data that NASA has provided as part of the UAP disclosure was taken on the surface of the moon from Apollo 12 and 17. You can't be a moon landing denier and also believe that those photos captured unexplained phenomenon." Fox News reported that the PURSUE material includes astronaut and NASA-related accounts from the Apollo and Gemini eras describing strange lights and unidentified objects observed in space, alongside 2023 infrared footage of an F-16 engaging a diamond-shaped object over Lake Huron and reports from military operations in Iran, Syria, Iraq and Greece. Set against the 2025 Office of Communications statement, NASA has no qualifying records because DoD owns them, the position is that NASA's most interesting UAP data was released through the Department of War's website rather than deposited in the National Archives collection Congress created for exactly that purpose. One route is statutory, permanent and indexed; the other is a rolling, discretionary web publication controlled by the Secretary of War. NASA's material went down the second.

A loose thread worth pulling: on the war.gov PURSUE page, the Release 05 slideshow of 7 August 2026 carries an image described as "A photograph of an unidentified object in low-Earth orbit, taken from Space Shuttle Columbia in 1996," while the underlying file is named DOW-UAP-D098_Film-Analysis-of-Unidentified-Objects_1953.jpg. A 1996 Columbia photograph filed under a 1953 film analysis is either a metadata error or an indexing artifact, and either way it is a checkable discrepancy in the government's own release. The Columbia 1996 imagery in question is almost certainly from STS-75, the tethered satellite mission whose downlink footage has been argued over for thirty years, meaning the 2026 disclosure has quietly placed a long-contested NASA video into an official unresolved-case release.

### What it has denied

NASA's denial operates in three layers, each with an escape hatch. The 2023 Independent Study Team found no evidence of extraterrestrial origin while working exclusively from unclassified data and explicitly excluding past incidents from its charter. The 2025 Office of Communications statement denies possession of transferable records by asserting DoD ownership rather than absence, a custody argument, not a factual one. And Administrator Isaacman's 2026 formulation, 'There's nothing I'm aware of in terms of alien bodies or spaceships,' limits the denial to the personal knowledge of an appointee with no read-in to the compartments in question. Each is technically defensible and none touches the underlying question.

Roles the board assigns it: research, concealment, disclosure, custody.

### Open questions on this node

- What UAP-related records does NASA physically hold that it characterises as DoD-owned, and who at NASA made that ownership determination?
- Was NASA's ownership position coordinated with the Department of Defense or the National Archives, and is there written correspondence recording it?
- Is Mark McInerney still serving as Director of UAP Research under Administrator Isaacman, and has the role been budgeted or staffed?
- Why does PURSUE Release 05 file DOW-UAP-D098 carry a 1953 film-analysis filename while being described as a 1996 Space Shuttle Columbia photograph, and is the underlying imagery from STS-75?
- Where are the original Apollo 12 and Apollo 17 lunar-surface negatives and the associated frame indices, and were any frames withheld from the PURSUE release?
- Did the NASA Independent Study Team request classified access and, if so, who denied it?
- Which NASA Gemini- and Apollo-era astronaut accounts entered PURSUE, through what channel, and were the astronauts or their estates consulted?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 190 of 1057, score 45). 15 connections, 3 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A1/D3.

## Battelle Memorial Institute

Battelle Memorial Institute is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1929; axes C3 A2 D1; 14 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Private nonprofit applied science institute, Columbus, Ohio, est. 1929; operator or co-operator of PNNL, ORNL, INL, NREL, Brookhaven and NBACC.

### What it is

Battelle Memorial Institute is a Columbus, Ohio applied-science nonprofit founded in 1929 that describes itself as the world's largest independent nonprofit applied science organization. From late 1951 it held the classified Air Technical Intelligence Center contract code-named Project STORK, out of which came Project Blue Book Special Report No. 14 (5 May 1955), the largest statistical study of UFO reports ever performed for the U.S. government. Today Battelle manages or co-manages eight U.S. national laboratories, including Los Alamos, Oak Ridge, Idaho, Pacific Northwest and Savannah River.

### Why it is implicated

Battelle is the single institution that sits on both sides of the mid-century split: it produced the numbers that the public debunking campaign was built on, and it is the contract operator of the laboratory system where exotic-materials characterization would actually be performed. The Durant Report's own list of documentary evidence placed before the CIA's Robertson Panel names, as item 3, "Progress Reports of Project STORK (code name for Battelle Memorial Institute contract work supporting ATIC)", meaning a private contractor's classified analysis was the evidentiary substrate for the panel that recommended a national "training and debunking" program. Battelle's own analysis found roughly 22 percent of about 3,200 cases were unknowns, and that the higher the case quality the more likely it was to be unknown; the Air Force publicized the study in October 1955 as proof that flying saucers did not exist and cited a 3 percent unknown rate. Five days before the Robertson Panel convened, a Battelle memorandum dated 9 January 1953, the document Jacques Vallee found in J. Allen Hynek's files in 1967 and calls the "Pentacle memorandum", is reported to have proposed that Battelle and ATIC agree in advance on what could and could not be said to the CIA panel. Battelle is therefore not a neutral statistical vendor; it is the earliest documented instance of the pattern this board tracks, a cleared contractor mediating between what the government knew and what a public-facing scientific body was permitted to see.

### The longer account

The relationship began with an admission of institutional inadequacy. Captain Edward J. Ruppelt, running the Air Force's UFO desk at the Air Technical Intelligence Center at Wright-Patterson, concluded at the end of 1951 that the Air Force lacked the analytic capacity to do anything scientific with the report backlog. He went to Battelle Memorial Institute in Columbus, roughly a hundred and twenty miles away, and the resulting classified support contract was code-named Project STORK. Battelle built a standardized observer data sheet, encoded about thirty characteristics of each sighting onto IBM punched cards, and ran the first machine-assisted statistical analysis of the UFO corpus. It is important to hold the shape of this arrangement in view: the government's public UFO investigation had, from 1952 onward, a classified contractor layer underneath it that the public did not know existed and that Blue Book's own critics could not see.

The Robertson Panel is where that layer surfaces in the documentary record. F.C. Durant's 16 February 1953 memorandum to the CIA's Assistant Director for Scientific Intelligence records that on Thursday morning, 15 January 1953, "The Project STORK support at Battelle Memorial Institute, Columbus, was described by Dr. Hynek." Tab B of the same document lists the STORK progress reports as evidence item 3, sitting alongside the Project GRUDGE and BLUE BOOK status reports, the Holloman Air Force Base sightings summary, Project TWINKLE, and Project POUNCE. The five-scientist panel that would recommend stripping UFOs "of the special status they have been given" was reading Battelle's work product.

The timing of the Pentacle memorandum is the part of this that will not settle. Vallee has stated for decades that in 1967, while organizing Hynek's files, he found a memorandum stamped SECRET dated 9 January 1953, five days before the Robertson Panel's first session, written by Battelle's Howard C. Cross and addressed to Miles Goll at Wright-Patterson, and that it proposed agreement between Project Stork and ATIC on what could and could not be discussed in Washington on 14-16 January. Vallee's papers are now at Rice University's Woodson Research Center as MS 0672, and the finding aid lists a file titled "11. The Robertson Panel and the 'Pentacle Memorandum', January 1953" in Series III. The collection is restricted: Series I and II open on 24 January 2028, Series III case files on 1 January 2031. The Department of Defense has never released the memorandum. Treat the document's existence as archivally attested and its full text as an open question, not a claim.

Special Report No. 14, dated 5 May 1955, is the payoff. Battelle's analysts sorted about 3,200 cases into knowns, unknowns and insufficient information, graded each on a four-point quality scale, and required unanimity among four analysts before a case could be called unknown, an unusually stringent test that biases toward suppressing the unknown count. About 22 percent came back unknown. The quality gradient ran the wrong way for the debunking thesis: roughly 35 percent of excellent-quality cases were unknowns versus about 18 percent of the poorest. Battelle's own summary nevertheless declared it "highly improbable" that any of the reports represented technological developments outside present-day knowledge. When the Air Force released the study in October 1955 under Secretary Donald Quarles, the public framing was that the report scientifically disproved flying saucers, with an unknown rate cited as 3 percent. The report itself was not distributed; the press release was. The cleanest surviving copy of the 312-page report sits in CIA's declassified holdings as CIA-RDP81R00560R000100060001-5, which is itself worth noting, since the document is nominally an Air Force product.

What makes Battelle load-bearing for a recovery-and-exploitation thesis rather than merely for a history of statistical malpractice is what the institute became. Battelle's own site states it manages eight national laboratories for the Departments of Energy and Homeland Security under the government-owned, contractor-operated model, and partners in managing Canadian Nuclear Laboratories. It is the sole LLC member for Pacific Northwest National Laboratory, Idaho National Laboratory (via Battelle Energy Alliance), the National Biodefense Analysis and Countermeasures Center, and Savannah River National Laboratory; an LLC member of UT-Battelle for Oak Ridge; of Brookhaven Science Associates with Stony Brook; and of Triad National Security with the Texas A&M System and the University of California for Los Alamos, which Battelle describes as executing work for the Department of Energy, the Department of War and the Department of Homeland Security. Battelle's stated core competencies are advanced materials, biology and chemistry, and its national security portfolio includes an advanced functional materials line.

The analytic point is a continuity of institutional posture, not a proven chain of custody. The organization that in 1953 was negotiating with ATIC over what the CIA's scientists would be allowed to hear is the same organization that now holds the operating contracts for the laboratories where anomalous materials would plausibly be characterized. No public contract, appropriation line, or DD Form 254 has been produced connecting Battelle or any Battelle-operated laboratory to UAP materials exploitation. That absence is the research target, and Battelle's carve-out-eligible SAP work at those laboratories is exactly the space in which such an absence would be expected.

### What it has denied

Battelle has issued no public statement about Project STORK, the Pentacle memorandum, or any materials-analysis role, and its corporate history pages do not mention the ATIC contract at all. The denial in this case is by omission: the institute's public account of its own past treats 1952-1955 as if the largest statistical study of UFO reports ever performed for the U.S. government was not its work.

Roles the board assigns it: research, concealment, custody.

### Open questions on this node

- The full text of the 9 January 1953 Battelle memorandum has never been released by DoD or Battelle; the only known copy sits in a Rice University collection restricted until 2031. What does the complete document say about what ATIC withheld from the CIA panel?
- What was the contract number, contracting activity and dollar value of Project STORK, and how long did it run after Special Report No. 14 was delivered?
- Battelle reportedly reacted with hostility when Hynek returned in 1967 to ask about the memorandum; no contemporaneous record of that exchange has surfaced.
- Did Battelle perform materials characterization on any recovered debris, at Columbus or at any Battelle-operated national laboratory? No contract, appropriation line, DD Form 254 or facility clearance record establishing this has been produced.
- Which Battelle-operated facilities hold SAP carve-out designations under 32 CFR 117.6, and does DCSA retain or lack industrial security cognizance over them?
- Why does the cleanest surviving copy of an Air Force report reside in CIA's document holdings rather than in the Air Force's own accession to NARA?
- Battelle's own corporate history omits the ATIC work entirely, was that omission a contract requirement, a classification consequence, or an editorial choice?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 35 of 1057, score 90). 15 connections, 6 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C3/A2/D1.

## Robertson Panel

Robertson Panel is a program on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1953; axes C0 A3 D0; 13 connections; in the research band.

In one line: CIA Office of Scientific Intelligence advisory panel, January 1953.

### What it is

The Robertson Panel was a five-member scientific advisory panel convened by the CIA's Office of Scientific Intelligence, meeting 14-18 January 1953, to evaluate whether unidentified flying objects posed a threat to national security. Its proceedings were recorded in a memorandum dated 16 February 1953 from F.C. Durant to the Assistant Director for Scientific Intelligence, known as the Durant Report, with the two-page formal panel report appended as Tab A. It is the founding document of official U.S. UFO information policy.

### Why it is implicated

The Robertson Panel is the moment the U.S. government formally converted a collection problem into a perception-management problem, and it did so in writing. The panel recommended that national security agencies "take immediate steps to strip the Unidentified Flying Objects of the special status they have been given and the aura of mystery they have unfortunately acquired," and Durant's supplement records the panel's concept of an education program with "two major aims: training and 'debunking,'" to be delivered through television, motion pictures and popular articles, with Walt Disney Inc. animated cartoons and broadcaster Arthur Godfrey named as candidate channels. It further recommended that civilian UFO groups "should be watched" for "potentially great influence on mass thinking" and "possible use ... for subversive purposes." The panel's own reasoning turns on hardware: Robertson contrasted the UFO problem with the 1943-1944 CROSSBOW effort, in which "by June 1944 there was material evidence of the existence of 'hardware' obtained from crashed vehicles in Sweden," and concluded that the "absence of any 'hardware'" gave ATIC's problem a "will-of-the-wisp" nature. If hardware existed and was compartmented away from ATIC, the panel's central premise was false and the panel was the instrument by which that falsity was made durable. The CIA then spent 1991 to 1994 telling a FOIA requester it held no records of the panel before finally closing the request.

### The longer account

The Robertson Panel did not originate inside the Air Force. Durant records that "after consideration of the subject of 'unidentified flying objects' at the 4 December meeting of the Intelligence Advisory Committee, the following action was agreed: 'The Director of Central Intelligence will: a. Enlist the services of selected scientists to review and appraise the available evidence in the light of pertinent scientific theories.'" The action was delegated to the Assistant Director for Scientific Intelligence, H. Marshall Chadwell, and the panel was assembled with the cooperation of the Air Technical Intelligence Center. The five members were H.P. Robertson of Caltech, listed in Tab C under field of competency as "Physics, weapons systems"; Luis W. Alvarez of the University of California, "Physics, radar"; Lloyd V. Berkner of Associated Universities, Inc., geophysics; Samuel Goudsmit of Brookhaven National Laboratories, "Atomic structure, statistical problems"; and Thornton Page of the Office of Research Operations, Johns Hopkins University, astronomy and astrophysics. Associate members were J. Allen Hynek of Ohio State and F.C. Durant of Arthur D. Little, Inc. This is a weapons-and-radar panel with a nuclear-physics component, not an atmospheric-phenomena panel.

The panel sat for eight sessions across five days. It viewed the Tremonton, Utah and Great Falls, Montana films, heard Navy Photo Interpretation Laboratory analysts who had spent roughly a thousand professional and sub-professional man-hours plotting the Tremonton frames and concluded the objects were "not reflections because there was no blinking while passing through 60 degrees of arc" and were therefore "self-luminous." The panel rejected that conclusion after watching a short film of seagulls in bright sunlight. It heard Ruppelt on ATIC procedure, Hynek on Battelle's Project STORK, Lt. Col. Frederick Oder on Project TWINKLE, and Dewey Fournet on fifteen months as Pentagon UFO project officer, during which Fournet had eliminated known and probable causes and been left with "extra-terrestrial" as the only remaining explanation in many cases. The panel declined to accept any of Fournet's cases on the grounds that they were "raw, unevaluated reports."

The two-page formal report, Tab A, dated 17 January 1953 and signed by all five members, concluded that the evidence "shows no indication that these phenomena constitute a direct physical threat to national security" and that "there is no residuum of cases which indicates phenomena which are attributable to foreign artifacts capable of hostile acts." The threat it identified was domestic and psychological: continued reporting "does, in these parlous times, result in a threat to the orderly functioning of the protective organs of the body politic," through "the clogging of channels of communication by irrelevant reports, the danger of being led by continued false alarms to ignore real indications of hostile action, and the cultivation of a morbid national psychology in which skillful hostile propaganda could induce hysterical behavior and harmful distrust of duly constituted authority."

Durant's Part II, explicitly labeled "an unofficial supplement to the official Panel Report ... setting forth comments and suggestions of the Panel Members which they believed were inappropriate for inclusion in the formal report", is where the operational content lives. The two-aim education program, the recommended engagement of a mass-psychology consultant (Hadley Cantril of Princeton, author of the study of the Orson Welles panic), the suggestion of an advertising expert, the Jam Handy Co. and Walt Disney Inc. as production channels, the Navy ONR Special Devices Center at Sands Point as an implementing organization, and the estimate that the program would need to run a minimum of one and a half to two years. And the surveillance recommendation regarding the Civilian Flying Saucer Investigators of Los Angeles and the Aerial Phenomena Research Organization.

Two passages deserve the board's attention above the rest. First, under the heading "ARTIFACTS OF EXTRATERRESTRIAL ORIGIN", a standing agenda item, page 10 of the index, Durant records that "none of the members of the Panel were loath to accept that this earth might be visited by extra-terrestrial intelligent beings of some sort, some day," that Goudsmit held such artifacts "are no cause for alarm; rather, they are in the realm of natural phenomena subject to scientific study," and that "this was an attitude in which Dr. Robertson did not concur, as he felt that such artifacts would be of immediate and great concern not only to the U.S. but to all countries." Second, the geographic analysis: the ATIC map of 1952 unexplained sightings "showed clusters in certain strategic areas such as Los Alamos," and "the Panel could find no ready explanation for these clusters."

The document's own release history is evidence. CUFON's Dale Goudie filed FOIA requests in September 1991, November 1991, March 1992 and April 1992. CIA told him the report had been released by the Air Force Office of Public Information on 9 April 1958 and referred him to the Air Force and NARA; NARA told him the Durant Report was in CIA custody. After a search initiated in June 1992, CIA informed him on 6 September 1994 that it was "unable to locate the records requested," and on 28 November 1994 Information and Privacy Coordinator John H. Wright wrote that "we feel it inappropriate to conduct yet another search on your behalf. Therefore, we are unable to assist you further and will not discuss this matter with you further." CUFON documents that multiple non-identical versions of the report, differing in pagination, spacing, typeface and redaction, circulated from CIA over the years, and that no version carries the panel members' signatures.

### What it has denied

The panel's signed conclusion, that there is "no residuum of cases which indicates phenomena which are attributable to foreign artifacts capable of hostile acts" and "no evidence that the phenomena indicates a need for the revision of current scientific concepts", is the template for every subsequent official denial, including AARO's. CIA's own posture is separately documented: it told a FOIA requester for three years that it held no records of a panel it had convened, then declined further discussion in a signed letter of 28 November 1994.

Roles the board assigns it: concealment, oversight, research.

### Open questions on this node

- Robertson drafted the panel report on the evening of Friday 16 January and Chadwell reported the next morning that he had shown the rough draft to the Director of Intelligence, USAF, "whose reaction was favorable." Who else saw the draft before it was finalized, and was the conclusion negotiated?
- Berkner was absent until Friday afternoon yet had "reviewed and approved" the draft report earlier than the other members. How?
- What became of the recommended education program? No implementing directive, contract, or budget line for a training-and-debunking program has been produced.
- Was the surveillance recommendation regarding civilian UFO groups acted on, and by which agency? FBI and Air Force OSI files on APRO and NICAP exist but no document ties them to this recommendation.
- Why did CIA hold multiple non-identical, unsigned versions of a single 1953 memorandum, and where is the signed original?
- The panel treated the absence of hardware as dispositive. If recovered materiel existed under compartmented control in January 1953, who at CIA or ATIC knew, and was the panel deliberately kept below that access line?
- The final page of Part II is redacted after the sentence on expected increase in sightings. What was removed?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 98 of 1057, score 62). 15 connections, 4 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 3 official denials recorded against it. Axes C0/A3/D0.

### The claims on the record

Goudsmit held that such artifacts "if they did exist, are no cause for alarm"; Durant records that "this was an attitude in which Dr. Robertson did not concur, as he felt that such artifacts would be of immediate and great concern not only to the U.S. but to all countries." Fournet, Jr., the Pentagon's UFO project officer for fifteen months, told the panel he had eliminated each known and probable cause of sightings, "leaving him 'extra-terrestrial' as the only one remaining in many cases"; the panel declined to accept any of his cases because they were "raw, unevaluated reports." ## University of Colorado UFO Project (Condon Committee)

University of Colorado UFO Project (Condon Committee) is a program on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1966 to 1969; axes C0 A2 D1; 12 connections; in the research band.

In one line: USAF contract 44620-67-C-0035 (1966-1969).

### What it is

The Condon Committee was the University of Colorado UFO Project, funded by the Air Force Office of Scientific Research from 1966 to 1968 under physicist Edward U. Condon, which produced the report formally titled Scientific Study of Unidentified Flying Objects. Its conclusions, released January 1969, were used by the Air Force as the basis for terminating Project Blue Book on 17 December 1969. It remains the most cited public document on the scientific status of the UFO question.

### Why it is implicated

The Condon Committee is the second and larger instance of the pattern the Robertson Panel established: a nominally independent scientific body whose framing was set before the work began and whose product retired the public investigative apparatus. Robert J. Low's memorandum of 9 August 1966, written to reassure University of Colorado administrators before the contract was signed, states the design explicitly: "The trick would be, I think, to describe the project so that, to the public, it would appear a totally objective study but, to the scientific community, would present the image of a group of non-believers trying their best to be objective but having an almost zero expectation of finding a saucer." The finished report contains an affirmative denial of concealment, "We have no evidence of secrecy concerning UFO reports", offered by a committee that had simultaneously declined to independently evaluate the Air Force's national-security finding, writing "we felt that it was out of our province." A body that will not examine the defense question is not positioned to certify the absence of secrecy about it. The report also recommended against establishing any federal UFO agency, a recommendation that held for fifty-three years until AARO. Buried in Section IV, Chapter 2, the report's own Lakenheath-Bentwaters case assessment concludes "the probability that at least one genuine UFO was involved appears to be fairly high."

### The longer account

The chain of custody runs from the Air Force to itself. In March 1966 an ad hoc committee of the Air Force Scientific Advisory Board chaired by optical physicist Brian O'Brien recommended that the Blue Book program be strengthened, that contracts be negotiated with a few selected universities to provide scientific teams, and that one university or nonprofit coordinate. The Secretary of the Air Force referred the recommendations to the Air Force Office of Scientific Research, which combined recommendations A and C into a single contracting university with subcontracting authority and implemented recommendation B through Air Force Regulation 80-17, reprinted as Appendix B of the eventual report. Harvard, Berkeley, MIT and North Carolina declined. Hynek's Northwestern and McDonald's Arizona were rejected as too committed. Colorado accepted on 6 October 1966. The contract was publicly announced on 7 October 1966 and provided $313,000 for fifteen months, 1 November 1966 to 31 January 1968; total funding later rose above $500,000.

The contract terms are worth reading against what followed. The report states that "the contract provided that the planning, direction and conclusions of the Colorado project were to be conducted wholly independently of the Air Force," that the Air Force was ordered to furnish its records and base support, and that "we were assured that the federal government would withhold no information on the subject." Where sightings involved classified missile launches or classified radar systems, "this fact is merely stated as to do more would involve violation of security on these military subjects." The committee then reports that these reservations "have affected a negligible fraction of the total material." This is an assurance accepted on the word of the contracting party, by a project that had no independent means of verifying either the completeness of what it received or the existence of holdings outside Blue Book.

The Low memorandum predates the contract by two months. Written 9 August 1966 to two university administrators, it reassured them that the study "would be conducted almost entirely by non-believers who, though they couldn't possibly prove a negative result, could and probably would add an impressive body of thick evidence that there is no reality to the observations," before the sentence about the trick. James McDonald learned of it in July 1967 and located a copy in the project's own open files; NICAP severed ties on 30 April 1968; Donald Keyhoe circulated copies; John G. Fuller published "Flying Saucer Fiasco" in Look on 14 May 1968, describing the project as a "$500,000 trick." Condon fired David Saunders and Norman Levine for alleged incompetence; administrative assistant Mary Louise Armstrong resigned; Low resigned in May 1968. Representative J. Edward Roush asked the General Accounting Office to investigate; GAO declined. Condon resigned from the AAAS after Science published on the controversy without his input.

What the committee produced is not a single argument but a document whose front matter contradicts its interior. Condon's six-page Conclusions and Recommendations opens: "Our general conclusion is that nothing has come from the study of UFOs in the past 21 years that has added to scientific knowledge. Careful consideration of the record as it is available to us leads us to conclude that further extensive study of UFOs probably cannot be justified in the expectation that science will be advanced thereby." He recommended against a new federal agency, and on secrecy wrote: "It has been contended that the subject has been shrouded in official secrecy. We conclude otherwise. We have no evidence of secrecy concerning UFO reports. What has been miscalled secrecy has been no more than an intelligent policy of delay in releasing data so that the public does not become confused by premature publication of incomplete studies of reports."

Set that against the adjacent paragraphs. On the defense question Condon wrote: "We felt that it was out of our province to attempt an independent evaluation of this conclusion. We adopted the attitude that, without attempting to assume the defense responsibility which is that of the Air Force, if we came across any evidence whatever that seemed to us to indicate a defense hazard we would call it to the attention of the Air Force at once. We did not find any such evidence. We know of no reason to question the finding of the Air Force." The committee thus deferred to the Air Force on the national-security question and then, three paragraphs later, certified that no secrecy existed. Hynek's later count was that the committee had been unable to explain more than a quarter of the cases it examined. In Case 2 of Section IV, Chapter 2, on the 1956 Lakenheath-Bentwaters radar-visual event, the report states: "although conventional or natural explanations certainly cannot be ruled out, the probability of such seems low in this case and the probability that at least one genuine UFO was involved appears to be fairly high."

The operational effect was immediate and durable. The Air Force had asked the National Academy of Sciences, before the report was finished, to independently assess its scope, methodology and findings; a panel chaired by Yale astronomer Gerald M. Clemence studied it for six weeks and endorsed it, concluding that "no high priority in UFO investigations is warranted by data of the past two decades." On 17 December 1969 the Secretary of the Air Force announced the termination of Project Blue Book, citing the Colorado report, the NAS review, past studies and Air Force experience. The Air Force fact sheet distributed from Wright-Patterson in January 1985 records the closing tally, 12,618 sightings reported 1947 to 1969, of which 701 remain "Unidentified", and appends a categorical denial: "There are not now nor ever have been, any extraterrestrial visitors or equipment on Wright-Patterson Air Force Base." That sentence, on a public affairs fact sheet from the base that housed ATIC and let the Battelle contract, is a dated, attributable claim about the specific installation most often named in recovery accounts. It is evidence about the denier.

### What it has denied

Condon's Section I contains one of the two most consequential official denials in the record: "We have no evidence of secrecy concerning UFO reports." It was issued by a body that had explicitly declined to evaluate the defense question, that acknowledged its access to classified missile and radar material was limited by the contracting agency, and whose project coordinator had described the public framing as a trick two months before the contract was signed. The Air Force's January 1985 Wright-Patterson fact sheet supplies the companion denial about physical custody.

Roles the board assigns it: concealment, oversight, research, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- The report states the Air Force assured the project that no information would be withheld. No document memorializing that assurance, and no accounting of what classified missile or radar material was in fact withheld, has been produced.
- Was the Colorado project given access to anything outside the Blue Book file series, ATIC's Battelle-era holdings, foreign technology division files, or any compartmented program?
- Condon stated in a January 1967 lecture that he thought the government should not study UFOs because the subject was nonsense, "but I'm not supposed to reach that conclusion for another year." Who, if anyone, in the Air Force knew of that statement at the time?
- The GAO declined Rep. Roush's request to investigate the project. On what stated grounds, and does a written GAO declination exist?
- Fifty-nine case studies were published with their locations altered "for legal reasons." The key mapping location-to-case has never been released.
- Low resigned in May 1968; no further contemporaneous account of the August 1966 memo's audience or purpose has surfaced.
- Why did the Air Force commission an NAS review of the report before the report was finished?

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** Condon's conclusion reads: "Our general conclusion is that nothing has come from the study of UFOs in the past 21 years that has added to scientific knowledge. Careful consideration of the record as it is available to us leads us to conclude that further extensive study of UFOs probably cannot be justified in the expectation that science will be advanced thereby." Source: https://files.ncas.org/condon/text/sec-i.htm (Condon Report, Section I)
- **Tier 1 documented.** Robert Low's memorandum of 9 August 1966 states 'the trick would be, I think, to describe the project so that, to the public, it would appear a totally objective study but, to the scientific community, would present the image of a group of nonbelievers.' Source: Low memorandum, 9 Aug 1966, University of Colorado
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Air Force cited the Colorado study and a National Academy of Sciences review as the basis for terminating Project Blue Book on 17 December 1969. Source: USAF Fact Sheet, 'Unidentified Flying Objects and Air Force Project Blue Book'
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Air Force put the answer it wanted to the project in writing three months into the contract. Hippler's letter to Condon of 16 January 1967 continues: 'It will be at least that long before another independent study can be mounted to see if the Air Force can get out from under this program.' It closes by telling Condon that if the contract expires before the groundwork for a recommendation is laid, extending the contract 'would be less costly than another decade of operating Project Blue Book.' Source: Lt Col Robert R. Hippler to Dr Edward U. Condon, 16 January 1967, page 2, https://www.nicap.org/docs/HipplerLetters.pdf

Source: https://www.archives.gov/research/military/air-force/ufos (U.S. Air Force Fact Sheet on UFOs and Project BLUE BOOK, January 1985, reproduced by NARA)
- **Tier 1 documented.** The committee expressly declined to independently evaluate the Air Force's national-security finding: "We felt that it was out of our province to attempt an independent evaluation of this conclusion ... We know of no reason to question the finding of the Air Force that the whole class of UFO reports so far considered does not pose a defense problem." Source: https://files.ncas.org/condon/text/sec-i.htm (Condon Report, Section I)
- **Tier 1 documented.** The contract "provided that the planning, direction and conclusions of the Colorado project were to be conducted wholly independently of the Air Force," and the project recorded that "we were assured that the federal government would withhold no information on the subject, and that all essential information about UFOs could be included in this report." - **Tier 1 documented.** The first AFOSR research contract provided $313,000 for the first fifteen months, 1 November 1966 to 31 January 1968, and was publicly announced on 7 October 1966; total funding later rose above $500,000. - **Tier 1 documented.** The project originated in the March 1966 report of an ad hoc committee of the Air Force Scientific Advisory Board chaired by Brian O'Brien, which recommended contracts with selected universities and a coordinating university or nonprofit; AFOSR combined recommendations A and C into a single contracting university and implemented recommendation B through Air Force Regulation 80-17, reprinted as Appendix B of the final report. - **Tier 1 documented.** The report contains an affirmative denial of concealment: "It has been contended that the subject has been shrouded in official secrecy. We conclude otherwise. ## Força Aérea Brasileira / Ministério da Aeronáutica

Força Aérea Brasileira / Ministério da Aeronáutica is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1941; axes C2 A2 D3; 10 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Brazilian Air Force; ran Operação Prato and the 1986 intercepts; transfers UAP records to the Arquivo Nacional under Portaria 551/GC3 of 9 August 2010.

### What it is

The Forca Aerea Brasileira (FAB), the air arm of the Comando da Aeronautica, formerly the Ministerio da Aeronautica, which existed as a cabinet ministry from 1941 until the Ministry of Defence absorbed it in 1999. It is the Brazilian state organ that holds the air-defence radar picture, controls civil and military air traffic through CINDACTA and COMDABRA/COMAE, and receives pilot and controller reports of unidentified aerial objects. Since 2010 it has operated under a published administrative rule requiring it to register those reports and forward them to a civilian national archive.

### Why it is implicated

The FAB is the board's control case. It is a mid-sized air force inside a country that spent 1964 to 1985 under a military dictatorship, which means it had every institutional reason to behave like the US Air Force did after 1947, and for a period it did: SIOANI in 1969, Operacao Prato in 1977 under a named captain, and an air-defence scramble in 1986 that it initially treated as a security event. What it then did is what makes it load-bearing. It did not build a permanent denial apparatus around the subject; it built a filing pipeline out of it. Portaria do Comando da Aeronautica no 551/GC3 of 9 August 2010 does not classify unidentified aerial phenomena reports, it routes them to the Arquivo Nacional for public consultation, and the FAB says on the record that everything it holds from 1952 to 2025 has gone there. Every structural argument the US posture rests on (national security, sources and methods, the danger of public panic, the need to protect radar performance data) was equally available to a Cold War military government in Brasilia, and Brasilia declined to use it.

### The longer account

The Brazilian Air Force arrives on this board not as a suspected participant in a legacy recovery programme but as the measuring stick against which the US posture is read. Its value is that it controls for almost every variable the US concealment argument depends on. Brazil had an air force with a genuine air-defence mission, a real radar network, supersonic interceptors, a military intelligence service (the SNI), and, for twenty-one years, a military government answerable to nobody. If the argument for permanent secrecy about anomalous aerial objects were driven by the intrinsic sensitivity of the subject, the FAB should look like the most closed institution on the map. Instead it is the most open, and the openness is administrative rather than rhetorical, which is what makes it hard to dismiss.

The institutional history runs in three phases. The first is investigative. In 1969 the FAB stood up the Sistema de Investigacao de Objetos Aereos Nao Identificados, SIOANI, inside the 4a Zona Aerea in Sao Paulo, the command later renamed IV COMAR. SIOANI ran for roughly three years before ceasing operations quietly in 1972, and left behind a documentary residue that is now in the Arquivo Nacional. The second and best known phase is Operacao Prato, the field operation the FAB mounted in Para in 1977 and 1978 under Capitao Uyrange Hollanda, which put uniformed personnel into riverine villages with cameras and orders to observe. The third is the 19 May 1986 night, when CINDACTA and civil radar produced returns the FAB could not reconcile, five fighters were launched, and the Minister of Aeronautics stood in front of the press four days later and said the Air Force had no technical explanation.

What happened after 1986 is the part that matters most for comparison. The FAB did not sustain a public debunking programme. It did not issue a terminal report closing the file the way Project Blue Book was closed in 1969 with the Condon Committee as its warrant. Its posture, as characterised in the Brazilian Senate's own record, was discretion without denial: reticent when unasked, non-denying when asked. Then, in stages between 2007 and 2010, it converted the material into an archival stream. From 2007 the Aeronautica began sending its ufological papers to the Arquivo Nacional in Brasilia. In April 2009 CENDOC, the Air Force's documentation and history centre, sent across the first tranche relating to the Para operation. In August 2010 the Comando da Aeronautica published Portaria no 551/GC3 in the Diario Oficial da Uniao, formalising the pipeline: reports of aerial phenomena received by the aerospace operations command are received, registered, catalogued and forwarded to the Arquivo Nacional, where they are made available for consultation.

That rule is the single most consequential artefact in this dossier, because it inverts the default. Under 551/GC3 the resting state of a Brazilian military UAP report is public. There is no analytic gate, no adjudication of credibility before release, no requirement that the object be resolved before the paperwork moves. A LATAM captain reporting lights over Guarulhos in September 2025 and a Londrina tower controller reporting an intermittent radar-and-visual target in November 2025 both end up as scanned forms a citizen can pull down. In August 2026 the FAB restated the position in a written note to CNN Brasil: all documents, videos, photographs and reports available within the Comando da Aeronautica concerning unidentified aerial phenomena for the period 1952 to 2025 have already been transferred to the Arquivo Nacional, where they are in the public domain.

The limits are real and should be stated. The transfer was one service only. The Senate record is explicit that from 2007 it was the Aeronautica, and not the Army and not the Navy, that sent its papers to the Arquivo Nacional, and a 2013 approach to the Ministry of Defence by Brazilian researchers was aimed specifically at prying loose the Army and Navy holdings. So Brazil is not a case of total disclosure. It is a case of one service unilaterally choosing custody-with-publication while its sister services did not, which is itself an argument that the choice was institutional rather than physically compelled by the nature of the material. Nothing in the FAB record establishes retrieval of a craft or reverse-engineering activity, and nothing here should be read as claiming otherwise. What it establishes is narrower and sharper: a military that scrambled interceptors against unresolved radar contacts, said so at a podium, and then put the file in a civilian archive did not find that any of those three acts destabilised the state. The US argument that it cannot do the first two, let alone the third, is therefore not a claim about the subject matter. It is a claim about the US institution.

### What it has denied

The FAB does not deny; it defers to the archive. Its standing public answer, restated in a written note in August 2026, is that everything it holds from 1952 to 2025 is already in the Arquivo Nacional and in the public domain, which converts every substantive question into a records query rather than a security refusal. That posture is itself the artefact: an air force that treats the subject as a filing matter has decided the subject is not a secret.

Roles the board assigns it: research, custody, disclosure, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- The full text of Portaria no 551/GC3 of 9 August 2010 as published in the Diario Oficial da Uniao has not been retrieved directly; the date and content are attested by the FAB's own note and by the Senate record, not yet by the DOU page itself.
- The Senate record spells the 2010 FAB commander as 'Junichi Saito'; the commander of the Aeronautica in that period is conventionally rendered Juniti Saito. The discrepancy has not been resolved against a primary FAB or DOU source.
- The FAB says it has transferred everything through 2025, but the Arquivo Nacional's own description of the fund gives the most recent occurrence as 2016. Whether this reflects a lag in accessioning, a separate accrual series, or a genuine gap is unresolved.
- No document has been located establishing why the Aeronautica transferred and the Exercito and Marinha did not, or what the Army and Navy holdings contain.
- Nothing in the FAB record located here speaks to material recovery, custody of hardware, or reverse-engineering. Absence of such records in an otherwise open service is itself a datum but is not evidence either way.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 101 of 1057, score 61). 11 connections, 4 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C2/A2/D3.

- **Tier 1 documented.** DOU RECORD FOUND (by citation, not by page image). Source: Senado Federal, RQS 193/2022, justificacao, https://legis.senado.leg.br/sdleg-getter/documento?dm=9089831&disposition=inline ; Agencia Senado, 24 June 2022, https://www12.senado.leg.br/noticias/materias/2022/06/24/em-homenagem-no-plenario-ufologos-cobram-liberacao-de-documentos-sobre-ovnis
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Senate record characterises the Aeronautica's historic posture as discreet but non-denying: 'A Aeronautica sempre foi discreta quanto a estes e a outros fatos, mas nunca os negou quando inquirida.' Source: Senado Federal, RQS 193/2022, justificacao, https://legis.senado.leg.br/sdleg-getter/documento?dm=9089831&disposition=inline
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Senate record names Operacao Prato (1977), the creation of SIOANI in 1969 at the IV Comando Aereo Regional in Sao Paulo, and the 1986 'Noite Oficial dos UFOs no Brasil' as official Brazilian government UFO research programmes conducted through the Aeronautica. Source: Senado Federal, RQS 193/2022, justificacao, https://legis.senado.leg.br/sdleg-getter/documento?dm=9089831&disposition=inline
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Senate record states that in April 2013, at the invitation of then Foreign Minister Celso Amorim, Brazilian researchers were received at the Ministry of Defence before the commanders of the armed forces to press for full opening of the ufological documents, specifically those of the Army and the Navy. Source: Senado Federal, RQS 193/2022, justificacao, https://legis.senado.leg.br/sdleg-getter/documento?dm=9089831&disposition=inline
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Senate record states that the FAB's own web portal has made available a link dedicated exclusively to the subject of unidentified flying objects. Source: Senado Federal, RQS 193/2022, justificacao, https://legis.senado.leg.br/sdleg-getter/documento?dm=9089831&disposition=inline
- **Tier 1 documented.** The same Senate record states that in August 2010 the then commander of the FAB, identified in the document as brigadeiro Junichi Saito, determined by an instrument published in the Diario Oficial da Uniao that all OVNI cases registered across the force's installations be concentrated at COMDABRA for onward transmission to the Arquivo Nacional. Source: Senado Federal, RQS 193/2022, justificacao, https://legis.senado.leg.br/sdleg-getter/documento?dm=9089831&disposition=inline
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** A LATAM captain reported to Brazilian authorities on 1 September 2025 near Guarulhos (SP) seeing two to three strong balls of light, white, red and yellow, moving irregularly, and reported associated dry turbulence; the report form is in the Arquivo Nacional release. Source: CNN Brasil, 12 August 2026, reproducing the Arquivo Nacional form, https://www.cnnbrasil.com.br/ciencia/pilotos-e-controladores-relataram-18-casos-de-ovnis-no-brasil-em-2025/
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** Air Force note to BBC Brasil: all available UFO material has been sent to the Arquivo Nacional and the force 'não dispõe de profissionais especializados para realizar investigações científicas ou emitir parecer a respeito deste tipo de fenômeno aéreo'. Source: BBC Brasil, 14 January 2022
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** FAB documents released through the Arquivo Nacional record at least 18 unidentified-object cases reported in Brazil during 2025 by pilots, air traffic controllers and other aviation-sector personnel, with Parana (5 cases, concentrated at Londrina) and Ceara (4) leading. Source: CNN Brasil, 'Pilotos e controladores relataram 18 casos de OVNIs no Brasil em 2025', 12 August 2026, https://www.cnnbrasil.com.br/ciencia/pilotos-e-controladores-relataram-18-casos-de-ovnis-no-brasil-em-2025/
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** FAB's current stated coverage runs to 2025, contradicting the Arquivo Nacional's 2018 description. Official Air Force note: 'A Força Aérea Brasileira (FAB) informa que todos os documentos, vídeos, fotografias, relatos, entre outros, disponíveis, no âmbito do Comando da Aeronáutica, sobre fenômenos aéreos não identificados, no período de 1952 a 2025, já foram transferidos para o Arquivo Nacional, onde são de domínio público.' The same note cites Portaria 551/GC3 and names COMAE (not COMDABRA) as the receiving command, reflecting the post-2017 reorganisation. Source: Nota da Força Aérea Brasileira, quoted verbatim in CNN Brasil, 12 August 2026 (https://www.cnnbrasil.com.br/ciencia/pilotos-e-controladores-relataram-18-casos-de-ovnis-no-brasil-em-2025/)
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** On 10 August 2010 the Air Force created rules for pilots in case of contact with UFOs. Source: EFE/Terra, 'FAB cria normas para pilotos em caso de contato com ovnis', 10 August 2010
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** Portaria do Comando da Aeronautica no 551/GC3, dated 9 August 2010, governs the forwarding of reports of aerial phenomena received by the Comando de Operacoes Aeroespaciais (COMAE) to the Arquivo Nacional; under it the Comando da Aeronautica receives, registers, catalogues and forwards the occurrences to that body, where they are made available for consultation. Source: Nota da Forca Aerea reproduced verbatim in CNN Brasil, 12 August 2026, https://www.cnnbrasil.com.br/ciencia/pilotos-e-controladores-relataram-18-casos-de-ovnis-no-brasil-em-2025/
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** SIOANI, the Sistema de Investigacao de Objetos Aereos Nao Identificados, was created in 1969 within the FAB's 4a Zona Aerea in Sao Paulo (later IV COMAR) and ceased operations in 1972. Source: Revista Questao de Ciencia, 'A ditadura a caca de extraterrestres', 30 March 2024, https://www.revistaquestaodeciencia.com.br/apocalipse-now/2024/03/30/ditadura-caca-de-extraterrestres ; SIOANI bulletin scans, Internet Archive 'Brazilian UFO Files', https://archive.org/stream/BrazilianUFOFiles/1969%20Boletim%20SIOANI%20Conteu%CC%81do%201969_BOLETIM_SIOANI_CONTEUDO_djvu.txt
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** The Forca Aerea Brasileira stated in an official written note in August 2026 that all documents, videos, photographs and reports held within the Comando da Aeronautica on unidentified aerial phenomena for the period 1952 to 2025 have already been transferred to the Arquivo Nacional and are in the public domain. Source: Nota da Forca Aerea reproduced verbatim in CNN Brasil, 'Pilotos e controladores relataram 18 casos de OVNIs no Brasil em 2025', 12 August 2026 (updated 14 August 2026), https://www.cnnbrasil.com.br/ciencia/pilotos-e-controladores-relataram-18-casos-de-ovnis-no-brasil-em-2025/

### Its strongest connections

- Força Aérea Brasileira / Ministério da Aeronáutica runs out to **AARO FY2025 Consolidated Annual Report on UAP** (document, board tier A), a institutional link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: COMPARISON: AARO's Foreign Partner Engagement section names no Latin American partner at all. COMPARISON EDGE, and a sourced negative. Section V.A of AARO's FY2025 Consolidated Annual Report is two sentences in full: 'During the reporting period, AARO continued to engage with international partners to exchange information and best practices for UAP reporting, analysis, and response. AARO's deputy director presented a mission briefing to the Defense Geospatial Intelligence conference in London, United Kingdom.' No foreign office, no foreign air force and no foreign archive is named anywhere in the document except that one London conference. Brazil, the FAB and the Arquivo Nacional, the largest declassified military UAP holding in the Americas, do not appear. The task asked what the section actually names: it names one conference in London and nothing else. Sources: All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office, 'Fiscal Year 2025 Consolidated Annual Report on Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena', document 26-P-0880, section V.A 'Foreign Partner Engagement', https://www.aaro.mil/Portals/136/PDFs/FY25%20UAP%20Annual%20Report/AARO_FY2025_Consolidated_Annual_Report_on_UAP.pdf; Relatório de Ocorrências (Arquivo Nacional), http://imagem.sian.an.gov.br/acervo/derivadas/BR_DFANBSB_ARX/0/0/0249/BR_DFANBSB_ARX_0_0_0249_d0001de0001.pdf, dated 1986-06-02.
- Força Aérea Brasileira / Ministério da Aeronáutica runs out to **Operação Prato (Operation Saucer)** (program, board tier A), a programmatic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: The Brazilian Air Force mounted Operação Prato as its own field operation. Operação Prato was run by the Força Aérea Brasileira out of the Belém air base in Pará, with the SNI, after mayors in the Colares area formally asked for help. The Brazilian Senate's own Requerimento 193/2022 lists it among the official Aeronáutica UAP programmes: 'Desde 1954, o Governo Brasileiro, por meio de sua Aeronáutica, já criou vários programas oficiais de pesquisas dos OVNIs, como em 1977, quando ocorreu a chamada Operação Prato'. Bob Pratt's file records that the operation was staffed by 'Captain Uyrangê Hollanda and six sergeants' and was shut down after four months by Brigadier Protázio Lopes de Oliveira, the base commander in Belém. This is the base institutional fact the whole Brazilian cluster hangs from and it was previously unwired. ## Project MKULTRA

Project MKULTRA is a program on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1953-04-13 to 1964; axes C0 A3 D2; 9 connections; in the research band.

In one line: CIA umbrella project for research and development of materials to control human behavior; authorized by DCI Dulles 13 April 1953, records destroyed January 1973.

### What it is

The CIA's umbrella project for research and development of materials to control human behavior, authorized by Director of Central Intelligence Allen W. Dulles in April 1953 and run out of the Technical Services Division through 149 numbered subprojects at universities, foundations, hospitals and prisons. Its normal procedures for project approval, funding and accounting were waived. Its records were destroyed in January 1973 on a verbal order, and seven boxes of finance folders survived only because they had been misfiled to the Retired Records Center in 1970.

### Why it is implicated

It is the documented American precedent for the whole of this board's argument about compartmented programmes and their records: a waived-procedure compartment, an Inspector General finding that the practice was to keep no records of the planning and approval of test programmes, a destruction order that two Senate committees could not defeat, and a single Freedom of Information Act request that did. The board's own MKULTRA hearing node argued from this programme for two months without carrying it.

### The longer account

The argument for putting MKULTRA on a board about a legacy recovery programme is not that the two are the same subject. It is that MKULTRA is the case where every mechanism this board alleges was later proven on the government's own paper, and can therefore be used as a calibration rather than as an analogy. The Inspector General's survey of 26 July 1963 states the purpose in the Agency's own words and states, in the same document, that normal procedures for project approval, funding and accounting were waived and that present practice was to maintain no records of the planning and approval of test programs. That is a compartment designed to leave no trail, described by its own auditor. Ten years later the trail that did exist was destroyed: Technical Services Division personnel burned the files in January 1973 on Sidney Gottlieb's verbal order, itself carrying out a verbal order from DCI Helms, and both men later testified to it. The Rockefeller Commission and the Church Committee investigated the programme in 1975 without the finance file, because the 1975 searches covered the records of every branch thought likely to hold MKULTRA papers and not the Budget and Fiscal Section's retired holdings. What broke it open was administrative accident plus a statute. One branch had sent its finance folders to the Retired Records Center in 1970 in a departure from normal procedure the Agency could not later explain, and in 1977 an employee working John Marks's Freedom of Information Act request found them. The Senate's own opening statement puts the lesson plainly: one individual, through a Freedom of Information request, accomplished what two US Senate committees could not. Everything on this board about custody, about destruction orders and about what survives them has a documented instance here.

Roles the board assigns it: concealment, research, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether any of the 149 subproject files identifies its researchers: the released Subproject 43 file excises every occurrence of the researcher's name and university.
- What the 33 subprojects unrelated to behavioural work in the recovered boxes actually cover.

## Lockheed Martin Advanced Development Programs (Skunk Works)

Lockheed Martin Advanced Development Programs (Skunk Works) is a institution on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1943; axes C3 A3 D0; 8 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Unacknowledged-program aeronautics organisation, Burbank then Palmdale.

### What it is

Lockheed Martin Advanced Development Programs, universally known as Skunk Works: the unacknowledged-program aeronautics organisation founded by Clarence 'Kelly' Johnson in 1943 at Burbank, California, and now resident at Air Force Plant 42, Palmdale, a government-owned contractor-operated facility. Ben Rich directed it from 1975 to 1991. Its programme list is the working definition of the black aircraft: the U-2, the A-12 and SR-71, the F-117, Sea Shadow, DarkStar and the RQ-170 Sentinel.

### Why it is implicated

Skunk Works is the operational proof of the board's central structural claim: that a private corporation can hold, fly and conceal hardware from everyone without a need to know, including senior officers in the chain of command, for years at a time. AARO's own Historical Record Report uses Skunk Works aircraft to explain a class of UAP reports, and in doing so concedes the point -- the organisation routinely fields flying objects whose existence the government denies while they are flying. As of the 1986 GAO pass it also carries a documented custody failure: the companion report to GAO's testimony of 24 July 1986 names the Lockheed/Burbank plant as the site where special-access document control deteriorated, and says the deterioration might not have happened had the Defense Investigative Service been permitted to inspect the contract as it inspected Lockheed's others.

### The longer account

The reason Skunk Works belongs at programme level on this board is not that anyone has documented anomalous material inside it. It is that Skunk Works settles an argument the board otherwise has to make by inference. The standing objection to every legacy-programme account is that a secret of this size could not be kept -- too many people, too many decades, too many contractors. Skunk Works is the counter-example the United States government publishes about itself: the RQ-170 flew operationally for years before Lockheed acknowledged it existed, and the acknowledgment came after a loss on foreign soil rather than on a schedule anyone chose. The concealment worked until physics interrupted it.

What the 1986 GAO material adds is the failure mode. Concealment of that quality is not free, and the price is paid in accountability: 1,460 discrepancies in a 40,000-item special-access inventory at one company, 11 still unresolved, discovered because a congressional subcommittee asked rather than because the security system surfaced it. GAO's own explanation of why nobody caught it earlier is the sentence this board keeps returning to -- special access contracts are 'carved out of the Defense Investigative Service's periodic inspection program', by a 1965 decision, and by 1983 there were probably several thousand of them.

The organisation therefore sits on this board twice over: as the demonstration that unacknowledged hardware can be held indefinitely, and as the documented case where holding it meant nobody could say where the paper went.

### What it has denied

Skunk Works does not deny; it declines to confirm, and the record shows the decline holding for years at a time and then ending on someone else's timetable. The RQ-170 is the canonical instance: operational, photographed, and unacknowledged until it was lost abroad. The 1986 GAO exchange is the same posture at the paperwork layer -- GAO was granted access on condition that it not name the programme, the component or the service, so the public record of the failure exists with the subject of the failure redacted out of it. The board treats that as a documented behaviour rather than an inference, because in both cases the concealment and its terms are described in a government document.

Roles the board assigns it: concealment, custody, research.

### Open questions on this node

- GAO/NSIAD-88-2BR, 'Information Security: Actions Taken To Improve Lockheed's Special Access Document Accountability', is the follow-up that would say whether anything changed after 1986. It appeared in a search result only and has not been opened; nothing on this board is cited to it.
- The 1986 testimony and its companion report never name the special access programme itself, by agreement with DoD. Whether that programme has since been acknowledged, and under what name, is not established from any document read here.
- Ben Rich's post-retirement remarks about technologies 'locked up in black projects' remain the most quoted and least documented artefact in this field. No primary recording or contemporaneous transcript has been located, and the board carries no Tier 1 or Tier 2 claim resting on them.

## Air Technical Intelligence Center

Air Technical Intelligence Center is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1951; axes C3 A3 D0; 7 connections; in the research band.

In one line: USAF technical intelligence centre, Wright-Patterson AFB.

### What it is

The Air Technical Intelligence Center, formed in 1951 out of Air Materiel Command's T-2 intelligence organisation at Wright Field and resident at Wright-Patterson Air Force Base, Ohio. It was the United States' centre for the exploitation of foreign aerospace technology, the shop that processed the Operation Paperclip haul and took captured airframes apart. Every official American UFO project of the legacy era was hosted here: SIGN from 1948, GRUDGE from 1949 and Blue Book from 1952. It issued Project Blue Book Special Report No. 14 on 5 May 1955 under Project 10073, Study No. 102-EL-55/2-79, and both editions of AFR 200-2 name it as the Air Force activity responsible for conducting analysis of information and evidence. It became the Foreign Technology Division in 1961 and later the National Air and Space Intelligence Center, which is still at the base.

### Why it is implicated

The implicating fact is the assignment, not an allegation. The United States did not send unidentified flying objects to a science agency, to an academic body or to an air-defence command; it sent them to the organisation whose standing function was to answer one question about recovered hardware, which is what it is and how it works. That placement is usually reported as an administrative detail and is the structural point of this whole domain. The board also carries a Tier 2 custody link on the same ground: Howard McCoy ran AMC intelligence from 1945 to 1951, the organisation that received and exploited recovered foreign materiel and that hosted Project SIGN, so any 1947 recovery would have been processed at this address. And ATIC's own 1947-1951 T-2 intelligence files have never been subject to a comprehensive declassification review.

### The longer account

Read the regulation rather than the folklore and the placement is explicit. AFR 200-2 of 26 August 1953, paragraph 4a, says that 'The Air Technical Intelligence Center (ATIC), Wright-Patterson Air Force Base, Ohio, has been designated the Air Force activity responsible for conducting analysis of information and evidence.' The 1954 edition keeps the same terminal position, taking reports from base level through the Air Defense Command and the 4602d Air Intelligence Service Squadron and ending at ATIC.

One correction this board has had to make to its own corpus belongs here, because the mistake is easy and it repeats. The claim that AFR 200-2 routed reports to ATIC rather than to Blue Book is a category error. Project Blue Book was a project inside ATIC at Wright-Patterson; naming ATIC is naming Blue Book's host, and the 1953 regulation does not mention Blue Book at all in either edition.

The second thing the record establishes is that ATIC's analytical work was not confined to reports of things other people saw. TR-AC-47, the joint report on Project Silver Bug of 15 February 1955, carries the Air Technical Intelligence Center imprint on its title page, and its own conclusion asks for direct liaison between WADC and ATIC personnel. That is the same organisation, in the same year it published the largest statistical study of UFO reports the United States has ever produced, working with the Air Force's development command on a circular-planform aircraft.

### What it has denied

ATIC is not recorded on this board issuing a denial of its own, and its D axis is 0. Its posture is structural rather than spoken: the regulation that named it the analysis authority is the same instrument that governed what could be said publicly, so the centre held the evidence and the parent service held the microphone. That division is worth stating plainly, because it means an absence of ATIC denials is not an absence of denial. The statements this board grades as directed denials in the legacy era are the Air Force's, and they are carried on the usaf node.

Roles the board assigns it: custody, research, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- ATIC's 1947-1951 T-2 intelligence files have never been subject to a comprehensive declassification review. Nothing on this board establishes what is in them.
- The 1954 edition of AFR 200-2 is carried here from a transcription at cufon.org rather than from a government scan. The 1953 edition has a four-page image scan behind it; the 1954 one does not.
- The board holds no document establishing what ATIC did with any recovered materiel, only that it was the organisation designated to receive and analyse evidence. The custody link through McCoy is graded Tier 2 for that reason.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 148 of 1057, score 50). 8 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C3/A3/D0.
