## EG&G (Edgerton, Germeshausen and Grier)

EG&G (Edgerton, Germeshausen and Grier) is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1947; axes C3 A3 D0; 7 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Nuclear test instrumentation and classified site services; Groom Lake support.

### What it is

EG&G, Edgerton, Germeshausen and Grier, was founded in 1947 by three MIT-connected figures whose wartime work instrumented and photographed nuclear detonations, and became the Atomic Energy Commission's principal test-instrumentation contractor at the Nevada Test Site. Its Las Vegas-based Special Projects division provided engineering, technical, radar and logistical support to classified programs at the Air Force operating location near Groom Lake and operated the unmarked passenger airline known as Janet from a dedicated terminal at McCarran International Airport. The name and the contracts have since passed through the Carlyle Group, URS Corporation, AECOM and Amentum.

### Why it is implicated

EG&G is the connective tissue of the American black-site system: the same firm that instrumented nuclear tests ran the workforce airlift, the technical support and the site services that make an unacknowledged installation function. Its employees are the population at the center of the only litigation that has ever forced a court to decide what legal protection a denied facility enjoys. When Groom Lake workers sued in August 1994 alleging illegal open-air burning of hazardous waste, the Secretary of the Air Force filed a formal military and state secrets privilege claim covering categories that expressly included "security sensitive environmental data"; the President signed an annual RCRA exemption for "the United States Air Force's operating location near Groom Lake, Nevada"; and the Ninth Circuit affirmed dismissal, holding that seemingly innocuous information forming part of a classified mosaic may be withheld and that a court cannot order the government to disentangle it. That sequence, contractor workforce, denied facility, state secrets, presidential exemption, dismissal, is the complete legal template a legacy program would require, and it is fully documented in the Federal Register and the Federal Reporter.

### The longer account

Harold "Doc" Edgerton, Kenneth Germeshausen and Herbert Grier came out of MIT and out of the Manhattan Project's photographic and timing instrumentation work, and in 1947 incorporated the company that carried their initials. EG&G's core business for decades was the measurement of nuclear detonations for the Atomic Energy Commission, high-speed photography, timing and firing, diagnostics, making it one of very few private firms holding continuous clearances into the most sensitive American technical programs. From that base it grew into site services, security support, range operations and personnel logistics across the Nevada complex.

The most visible artifact is the airline. EG&G Special Projects operated the passenger service, universally known by its Janet callsigns, that moves the workforce from Las Vegas to the Air Force operating location at Groom Lake and to the Tonopah Test Range. A Nevada Department of Transportation diagram for McCarran dated May 1999 labels the building on the west side of the field the "EG&G Airlift Terminal." Reporting places the first Las Vegas-to-Groom flights in 1972 with a Douglas DC-6, and current operations at roughly twenty round trips per weekday carrying one to one and a half thousand workers on unmarked aircraft. This is what a routinized, industrial-scale commute to an unacknowledged facility looks like, and it has looked like that for over fifty years.

The legal history is the part that matters most for this board. In August 1994, Professor Jonathan Turley of the National Law Center at George Washington University filed two suits based on field research by the Project on Government Oversight: Frost v. Perry against the Secretary of Defense and Kasza v. Browner against the EPA Administrator, both in the District of Nevada, both under the citizen-suit provisions of the Resource Conservation and Recovery Act. Helen Frost was the widow of a former worker at the site; Walter Kasza was another. Six plaintiffs were permitted to proceed under pseudonyms because, in the district court's finding, "there is a substantial danger here that the Plaintiffs will be subject to retaliation by the government or possible physical attack and harassment from third parties in Nevada."

The government's response was not to deny the allegations. Once discovery began, the Air Force refused to furnish almost all requested information on privilege grounds, and Secretary of the Air Force Sheila Widnall invoked a formal claim of military and state secrets privilege over categories of national security information associated with the operating location near Groom Lake, "specifically including '[s]ecurity sensitive environmental data.'" The claim was supported by an unclassified declaration and a classified declaration reviewed in camera, and the court also reviewed in camera a classified declaration by Air Force Vice Chief of Staff General Thomas S. Moorman, Jr. The district court found the privilege properly invoked and held that it covered the requested information "even though her need for discovery was compelling." The Air Force then moved for summary judgment on the ground that the privilege barred any evidence tending to confirm or disprove that hazardous waste had ever been generated, stored or disposed of at the location, an essential element of every claim. Summary judgment was granted.

On the EPA side, the district court held that RCRA itself contains no exception for disclosure of classified information, but that the President may exempt a federal facility under 42 U.S.C. 6961(a), and gave the Administrator time to obtain one. On 29 September 1995 the President issued Presidential Determination No. 95-45, published at 60 Federal Register 52823, which reads in operative part: "I find that it is in the paramount interest of the United States to exempt the United States Air Force's operating location near Groom Lake, Nevada (the subject of litigation in Kasza v. Browner (D. Nev. CV-S-94-795-PMP) and Frost v. Perry (D. Nev. CV-S-94-714-PMP)) from any applicable requirement for the disclosure to unauthorized persons of classified information concerning that operating location." It exempted the location from "any Federal, State, interstate or local provision respecting control and abatement of solid waste or hazardous waste disposal that would require the disclosure of classified information concerning that operating location to any unauthorized person," and stated: "This exemption shall be effective for the full one-year statutory period." One year. It must be renewed, and it has been.

The Ninth Circuit decided the consolidated appeals on 8 January 1998. Kasza v. Browner, 133 F.3d 1159, cert. denied, 525 U.S. 967 (1998). The panel held that the state secrets privilege is absolute; that "if seemingly innocuous information is part of a classified mosaic, the state secrets privilege may be invoked to bar its disclosure and the court cannot order the government to disentangle this information from other classified information"; and that RCRA section 6001 does not preempt the privilege, "we discern no Congressional intent to replace the government's evidentiary privilege to withhold sensitive information in litigation by providing an exemption from RCRA's regulation of hazardous waste." The Supreme Court declined review.

What that sequence establishes is not that anything exotic was buried in the Groom Lake trenches. It establishes that a contractor-operated installation the government declines to name can be placed outside an ordinary environmental statute by a one-page presidential memorandum; that a cabinet secretary's declaration can foreclose discovery of the physical facts; that federal courts will accept a mosaic theory under which even innocuous data is protected; and that the workers who get sick have no remedy. Every element a concealed recovery and exploitation effort would need to survive litigation was demonstrated in public between 1994 and 1998.

The corporate lineage matters for tracking who holds the contracts today. EG&G's technical services business was acquired in August 1999 by a Carlyle Group entity and operated as EG&G Technical Services; URS Corporation acquired it in August 2002; URS was absorbed by AECOM in 2014; and the Janet operation is reported to have passed with AECOM's federal business to Amentum. The initials survive on the fuselages of nothing, but the function has never been interrupted.

### What it has denied

Neither EG&G nor its successors has ever publicly described the Groom Lake work. The government's posture in the Frost and Kasza litigation was not denial but non-cognizability: it did not deny that hazardous waste had been burned, it asserted that the existence or non-existence of such information is a state secret. Presidential Determination 95-45 preserves that posture explicitly, stating that nothing in it is intended to "imply that in the absence of such a Presidential exemption, the Resource Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA) or any other provision of law permits or requires disclosure of classified information to unauthorized persons."

Roles the board assigns it: custody, concealment, research.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether the Presidential Determination has been reissued in every year since 1995 and under which presidents; the determination is expressly one-year and a complete Federal Register series has not been assembled here.
- Which corporate entity currently holds the Groom Lake support and airlift contracts, under what contract number, and at what value, none of which appears in FPDS or any public award database.
- Whether EG&G or its successors have ever held a waived DoD special access program contract, which would be recorded on a classified DD Form 254 provided to the cognizant security office under DoDI 5205.11 and not otherwise disclosed.
- What the classified Widnall and Moorman declarations actually say about activities at the location; they remain sealed.
- Whether Robert Lazar's claim of an EG&G-mediated hiring process for work at S-4 in 1988-89 can be corroborated by any employment, payroll or badge record; no such record has been produced.
- Whether any of the missing or deceased individuals tracked on this board were EG&G, URS, AECOM or Amentum employees, and whether their assignments touched the Nevada complex.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 134 of 1057, score 53). 7 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C3/A3/D0.

## Sandia National Laboratories

Sandia National Laboratories is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1949; axes C3 A3 D1; 7 connections; in the research band.

In one line: DOE national laboratory, Albuquerque, NM.

### What it is

Sandia National Laboratories, a Department of Energy federally funded research and development centre in Albuquerque, administered by National Technology and Engineering Solutions of Sandia, LLC since 1 May 2017. It is the largest FFRDC in the United States by funding, at approximately 4,584.0 million dollars in FY2024, and it sits adjacent to Kirtland Air Force Base, the installation that contains the Manzano Weapons Storage Area and Coyote Canyon.

### Why it is implicated

The geography does most of the work, and it puts Sandia on this board twice over. In 1948 and 1949 the green fireballs that triggered the Los Alamos conference and Project Twinkle were concentrated over the atomic weapons installations, Los Alamos and Sandia among them. In August 1980 the best-documented American military UFO incident of the Cold War occurred on the adjoining base, and a Sandia security guard is a reporting party in the resulting Air Force Office of Special Investigations file. Thirty years later, three Sandia staff members were among the five outside reviewers of the FY09 AAWSAP technical reports, which is a DOE weapons laboratory peer-reviewing the physics portfolio of a Defense Intelligence Agency programme. The laboratory also appears in the custody record of an earlier document: the distribution list of AFSWC TR-59-39 sends a copy to the President of Sandia Corporation, attention Document Control Division.

### The longer account

The 1980 file is the one to read. AFOSI Special Agent Richard C. Doty's complaint form of 9 September 1980, District File No. 8017D93-0/29, records that three members of the 1608 Security Police Squadron, SSgt Stephen Ferenz, ATC Martin W. Rist and AMN Anthony D. Frazier, observed a bright light near the Manzano restricted test range on the night of 8 August 1980 that made stop-and-go manoeuvres, landed in Coyote Canyon and departed vertically at high speed. The same file records a Sandia security guard observing a round disc-shaped object near an alarmed structure at about 00:20 on 9 August, his radio malfunctioning during the encounter.

What makes the file structurally interesting rather than merely anecdotal is that a contractor-operated national laboratory, an Air Force counterintelligence office and a nuclear weapons storage site all appear in one anomalous-object document. What makes it uncomfortable is what the same office and the same agent did next. Doty was the conduit for fabricated material supplied to Paul Bennewitz, the Albuquerque physicist whose company Thunder Scientific operated adjacent to Kirtland and who had licensed sensor technology from Sandia in 1966; the operation ended in Bennewitz's psychiatric collapse. The board grades that Tier 2 and attributes it to AFOSI, not to the laboratory.

The 2009 material is a different register and is worth keeping separate from the 1980 material. DIA memo U-429-09-DWO-IM of 30 October 2009 records the outside review of the AAWSAP deliverables, with three of the five reviewers from Sandia, and DIA recorded the reviews as positive. The board's reading of the timing is that DIA sought institutional cover for a portfolio it expected to have to defend, which it did five weeks later, and that reading is graded Tier 3 because it is an inference from sequence rather than anything the memo says.

### What it has denied

The board records no denial issued by Sandia and none directed at it. That absence should be read carefully rather than charitably: the laboratory appears in the 1980 file as a reporting party, not as a respondent, and nothing here shows it was ever asked to account for what its guard saw. The deception in this story belongs to the Air Force counterintelligence office on the adjoining base, and the board keeps the attribution where the documents put it. What Sandia has not done, on any record held here, is say anything at all about August 1980.

Roles the board assigns it: research, custody, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- The Sandia security guard of 9 August 1980 is described in the AFOSI file but the board does not hold his name, and no Sandia-side incident record from that night has been located.
- The AEC-lineage custody link is the weakest thing attached to this node. It rests on a November 2025 video investigation citing Edgar Fouche and a former New Mexico state representative, and is graded Tier 2 on that basis. No primary document supports it here.
- The identities of the three Sandia reviewers of the FY09 AAWSAP deliverables are not in the DIA memo as held, and nothing on this board establishes which reports they reviewed.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 140 of 1057, score 52). 7 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C3/A3/D1.

## AFSWC TR-59-39 Vol I, A Study of Lunar Research Flights, 19 June 1959

AFSWC TR-59-39 Vol I, A Study of Lunar Research Flights, 19 June 1959 is a document on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1959-06-19; axes C3 A1 D2; 6 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Air Force Special Weapons Center, Kirtland AFB; L. Reiffel, Armour Research Foundation; contributor C. E. Sagan; DDC AD 425380.

### What it is

AFSWC-TR-59-39, TR 59-39 Vol I, A Study of Lunar Research Flights, dated 19 June 1959 and issued by Headquarters Air Force Special Weapons Center, Air Research and Development Command, Kirtland Air Force Base, New Mexico. It was written by L. Reiffel of the Armour Research Foundation at the Illinois Institute of Technology under Project Number 5776-57853 and Contract AF 29(601)-1164, with contributions from eleven others including C. E. Sagan. The copy read for this board is the Defense Documentation Center copy, AD 425380, read in full from the OCR text of the DTIC mirror.

### Why it is implicated

It is carried here as an instrument rather than as an anecdote. A dated federal record shows an Air Force laboratory paying a university research foundation to work out what could be learned from detonating a nuclear weapon on or near the Moon, and saying in its own introduction that one of the three motivations was political demonstration. The distribution list is the part with the most value to this board: it documents who in the nuclear weapons complex received a copy, which makes the report a custody record as well as a research study. Copies went to ARPA at the Pentagon, to the Atomic Energy Commission's technical reports library for the Division of Military Application, to Sandia Corporation's document control division, to Los Alamos, to Lawrence Radiation Laboratory and to WADD at Wright-Patterson.

### The longer account

The document says the political part out loud. Chapter I: 'The motivation for such a detonation is clearly threefold: scientific, military and political... Obviously, however, specific positive effects would accrue to the nation first performing such a feat as a demonstration of advanced technological capability. It is also certain that, unless the climate of world opinion were well-prepared in advance, a considerable negative reaction could be stimulated.' The abstract is narrower and more technical, offering space environment investigation, detection of nuclear device testing and weapons capability in space as the military aspect.

Three corrections this node forces on the wider corpus are the reason it is worth having. The string 'A119' appears nowhere in the report; the document identifies itself only by report number and contract. The date is 19 June 1959, not 1958 and not May 1959. And the claim that the study was denied and then declassified after four decades of maintained non-existence is not established by the copy read here: it carries UNCLASSIFIED markings on its cover pages, its title is expressly marked '(Unclassified Title)', and no declassification stamp, downgrading instruction or distribution limitation appears in the OCR. Whatever the classification history was, this copy does not carry it.

The Sagan question is answered only as far as the paper goes. C. E. Sagan is on the contributor list on the report page, and the appendix separately places C. Sagan on the National Academy of Sciences WESTEX group and on the Armed Forces National Research Council Panel on Extraterrestrial Life. The document says nothing whatever about his security clearance, and the fellowship-application story that usually travels with this report concerns a different document that has not been opened here.

### What it has denied

No denial attaches to this document on this board, and the popular framing that one should is the thing the record refuses. The claim usually made about it, that its existence was maintained as non-existent for four decades before a forced declassification, is not gradeable from the copy read: the DDC copy carries UNCLASSIFIED markings, an expressly unclassified title, and no declassification stamp, downgrading instruction or distribution limitation anywhere in the OCR. That is not a finding that the study was never withheld. It is a finding that this board holds no document establishing that it was, which is a different sentence and the only one the record supports.

Roles the board assigns it: research, custody.

### Open questions on this node

- The classification history of the study is not established. The DDC copy read here carries no declassification stamp or downgrading instruction, so whether an earlier copy was classified, and at what level, is unknown from this board.
- The record here is Volume I. The board holds nothing on any further volume and has not established whether one exists.
- The 1959 fellowship application in which the work is said to have been disclosed has not been opened. Nothing about it is in this report and no claim on this board rests on it.
- Whether the copies sent to Sandia, Los Alamos, Lawrence Radiation Laboratory, ARPA and WADD survive in those institutions' records has not been tested by any request logged here.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 111 of 1057, score 58). 6 connections, 5 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C3/A1/D2.

## Project Blue Book Special Report No. 14

Project Blue Book Special Report No. 14 is a document on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1955; axes C1 A2 D2; 6 connections; in the research band.

In one line: 'Analysis of Reports of Unidentified Aerial Objects', Project No. 10073, 5 May 1955; prepared by Battelle Memorial Institute.

### What it is

Project Blue Book Special Report No. 14, formally titled 'Analysis of Reports of Unidentified Aerial Objects,' dated 5 May 1955, is the largest statistical study of UFO reports the U.S. government has ever produced. The analysis was performed under contract by Battelle Memorial Institute of Columbus, Ohio, under a project designation reported as Project STORK, using 3,201 cases with an information cut-off of the end of 1952. It was released publicly on October 25, 1955 through Department of Defense Office of Public Information Release No. 1053-55.

### Why it is implicated

The gap between what the study found and what the government said it found is the cleanest documented instance of deliberate framing in the entire record, and it is verifiable from two documents that were published together. The press release and Secretary of the Air Force Donald Quarles's accompanying statement told the public that the unknowns amounted to three per cent, a figure drawn from 131 sightings in the first four months of 1955, not from the 3,201-case study sample the report actually analyzed. The report body shows unknowns running far higher, and highest precisely where witness reliability was rated Excellent. The release also declined to name Battelle, describing the analysis only as the work of a private scientific group under the supervision of the Air Technical Intelligence Center at Dayton, Ohio. The full 316-page report was then not distributed, on stated grounds of printing cost, until a private citizen reprinted it. Every element of a concealment operation is present and dated: a suppressed dataset, a substituted statistic, an unnamed contractor, and a restricted distribution.

### The longer account

Start with what the report says about itself. Its own Summary states that the special study started in 1953, that to provide the study group with a complete set of files the information cut-off date was established as of the end of 1952, and that in these charts, 3201 cases have been used. It describes a three-part analytic method: a systematic attempt to find distinguishing characteristics in the data, a study of any trend or pattern, and an attempt to determine the probability that any of the UNKNOWNS represented observations of technological developments not known to this country. On the first of these it records a genuinely load-bearing finding: 'The first step in the analysis of the data revealed the existence of certain apparent similarities between cases of objects definitely identified and those not identified. Statistical methods of testing when applied indicated a low probability that these apparent similarities were significant.' Read plainly, Battelle tested whether the unknowns looked like the knowns and found that the resemblance did not hold up. That is the opposite of the conclusion the public was handed. The report then walks it back through re-evaluation, concluding that the probability the unknowns represented unknown technology was very small.

Now the release. Department of Defense Office of Public Information Release No. 1053-55, dated October 25, 1955 and headlined 'AIR FORCE RELEASES STUDY ON UNIDENTIFIED AERIAL OBJECTS', opens with the flat assertion that no evidence of the existence of the popularly-termed flying saucers was found. It attributes the work to a private scientific group under the supervision of the Air Technical Intelligence Center at Dayton, Ohio, and never says the word Battelle. It then executes the substitution: since the instigation of the investigation more than seven years ago, methods and procedures have been so refined that of the 131 sightings reported during the first four months of 1955 only three per cent were listed as unknown. Secretary Quarles then attaches his name to that number: 'On the basis of this study we believe that no objects such as those popularly described as flying saucers have overflown the United States. I feel certain that even the unknown three per cent could have been explained as conventional phenomena or illusions if more complete observational data had been available.' The phrase on the basis of this study is doing enormous work. The study is the 3,201-case Battelle analysis. The three per cent is from a 131-case sample the study did not analyze, collected three years after the study's own cut-off date. The two are welded together in a single sentence and a Cabinet-level official signs it.

The attached Summary makes the substitution visible in its own numbers. For cases received before the end of 1952 it gives Unknown at 9 per cent; for the more recent tranche it gives Unknown at 3 per cent. Neither figure is the rate for the 3,201 cases whose charts and tables make up the report.

The body tells a different story, which is why the body was hard to get. Leon Davidson, a chemical engineer who had worked at Los Alamos Scientific Laboratory and who reprinted the report photographically beginning in 1956, wrote in his publisher's analysis that the key to the answer is contained in Fig. 8 (orig. p. 24) and Tables A1, A2, and A3 (orig. pp. 107 and 108); that Fig. 8 shows Unknown sightings constitute 33.3 per cent of all the object sightings for which the reliability of the sighting is considered Excellent; and that Tables A1 through A3 agree, with the percentage of Unknown sightings not less than about 20 per cent even when sightings of lesser reliability are included. The distribution runs the wrong way for the official framing: the better the witness, the more likely the object stayed unidentified.

Distribution was the second layer of the concealment. Davidson reproduces a letter from Air Force Secretary Donald A. Quarles dated July 5, 1956 stating that it has been estimated that the cost of printing enough copies for distribution to the public through such outlets as libraries and academic institutions would be between $10 and $15 per copy. The 316-page full report was therefore never distributed. The non-availability became a matter of congressional record at the House Subcommittee on Government Information under Rep. John E. Moss on November 15, 1956. Davidson's privately financed edition, reproduced photographically page by page with the main text intact, is why the document exists in public hands at all.

One further anomaly sits in the photographic reproduction. Davidson points to original page 295, where the codes for Final Identification carry typewritten strikeovers: the category originally called Rockets and Missiles in the early analytic work was changed to read Insufficient Information, and the category originally called Electromagnetic Phenomenon was changed to read Unknown. If that reading of the page is correct, then the report's residual category was not an empty bin for the unexplained. It was a renamed bin for a hypothesis.

Battelle is the connective tissue. The same institute later formed UT-Battelle, the operator of Oak Ridge National Laboratory, which per contemporary allegations received AARO's 2024 material analysis work. Battelle is also one of the entities Rep. Burlison named in his May 2026 MITRE interrogatories, alongside Lockheed Martin, Northrop Grumman, Raytheon, Boeing, General Dynamics, SAIC, Leidos, The Aerospace Corporation, Booz Allen Hamilton, BAE Systems and Bigelow Aerospace. The organization that wrote the government's founding public statistical assessment of the phenomenon has never left the file.

### What it has denied

The official denial is the October 25, 1955 release itself and Secretary Quarles's attached statement, which is exactly why it is evidence rather than argument. It is dated, numbered (Release No. 1053-55), signed by a named Cabinet-level official, and its central statistical assertion can be checked against the document it purports to summarize. The Air Force position that no objects such as those popularly described as flying saucers have overflown the United States was published simultaneously with, and contradicted by, the report's own reliability-stratified unknown rates.

Roles the board assigns it: research, concealment, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- The widely circulated figure of 21.5% unknowns among the 3,201 cases could not be verified against a legible primary table in the sources retrieved; the verifiable primary-adjacent figures are 33.3% for Excellent-reliability sightings and not less than about 20% overall, per Davidson's reading of Tables A1 through A3
- Whether the significance tests Battelle ran are recoverable from the surviving full tables, and at what confidence level the knowns and unknowns were found to differ
- Who inside ATIC or the Office of Public Information decided to substitute the 1955 131-case three per cent figure for the study's own unknown rate, and whether that decision is documented
- Who authorized the strikeover changes to the Final Identification codes on original page 295, and when
- Whether the Battelle contract file, deliverables and the intermediate Project STORK status reports survive, and in whose custody
- Whether Battelle's institutional records on this work fall within the scope of Rep. Burlison's 2026 FFRDC and contractor records campaign

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 138 of 1057, score 52). 6 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C1/A2/D2.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** Approximately 4,000 reports were collected; the final statistical study covered 2,199 objects, of which 434 (19.7 per cent) were evaluated as 'Unknown'. Source: Project Blue Book Special Report No. 14, statistical results
- **Tier 1 documented.** Project Blue Book Special Report No. 14 is titled 'Analysis of Reports of Unidentified Aerial Objects' and is dated 5 May 1955; the study started in 1953, its information cut-off was the end of 1952, and per the report's own Summary, 'In these charts, 3201 cases have been used.' Source: Special Report No. 14, Summary, p. vii, as photographically reproduced in Leon Davidson, ed., 'Flying Saucers: An Analysis of the Air Force Project Blue Book Special Report No. 14'; http://jfk.hood.edu/Collection/Weisberg%20Subject%20Index%20Files/D%20Disk/Davidson%20Leon%20Project%20Bluebook/Item%2002.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** Project Blue Book Special Report No. 14, 'Analysis of Reports of Unidentified Aerial Objects', Project No. 10073, is dated 5 May 1955 and marked FOR OFFICIAL USE ONLY. Source: Primary document via CIA CREST, CIA-RDP81R00560R000100060001-5
- **Tier 1 documented.** Secretary of the Air Force Donald A. Quarles is quoted in the release: 'On the basis of this study we believe that no objects such as those popularly described as flying saucers have overflown the United States. I feel certain that even the unknown three per cent could have been explained as conventional phenomena or illusions if more complete observational data had been available.' Source: DoD Release No. 1053-55, October 25, 1955; http://jfk.hood.edu/Collection/Weisberg%20Subject%20Index%20Files/D%20Disk/Davidson%20Leon%20Project%20Bluebook/Item%2002.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** Special Report No. 14 was issued by the Air Technical Intelligence Center on 5 May 1955 under Project 10073, Study No. 102-EL-55/2-79. Source: Project Blue Book Special Report No. 14, title page
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Air Force did not distribute the full 316-page report; a letter from Air Force Secretary Donald A. Quarles dated July 5, 1956 states that 'It has been estimated that the cost of printing enough copies for distribution to the public through such outlets as libraries and academic institutions would be between $10 and $15 per copy.' Source: Quarles letter, July 5, 1956, quoted in the Publisher's Statement of the Davidson reprint, p. D-2; http://jfk.hood.edu/Collection/Weisberg%20Subject%20Index%20Files/D%20Disk/Davidson%20Leon%20Project%20Bluebook/Item%2002.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The October 25, 1955 press release states flatly: 'No evidence of the existence of the popularly-termed "flying saucers" was found.' Source: DoD Release No. 1053-55, October 25, 1955; http://jfk.hood.edu/Collection/Weisberg%20Subject%20Index%20Files/D%20Disk/Davidson%20Leon%20Project%20Bluebook/Item%2002.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Summary distributed with the release gives category percentages for cases received before the end of 1952 that include 'Unknown - 9%', and for the later tranche 'Unknown - 3%'; neither is the unknown rate for the 3,201-case charted sample. Source: Special Report No. 14, Summary, pp. vii-viii, reproduced at http://jfk.hood.edu/Collection/Weisberg%20Subject%20Index%20Files/D%20Disk/Davidson%20Leon%20Project%20Bluebook/Item%2002.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The press release never names Battelle Memorial Institute, attributing the work only to 'study and analysis by a private scientific group under the supervision of the Air Technical Intelligence Center at Dayton, Ohio.' Source: DoD Release No. 1053-55, October 25, 1955; http://jfk.hood.edu/Collection/Weisberg%20Subject%20Index%20Files/D%20Disk/Davidson%20Leon%20Project%20Bluebook/Item%2002.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The report does not name Battelle, or any contractor, anywhere on its own face. A full-text search returns no occurrence of Battelle, Columbus, Stork, contractor, 'prepared by' or 19741. The attribution rests entirely on the Project Stork status reports and the surrounding file. Source: Project Blue Book Special Report No. 14, full-text search of the scan; six independent search terms, all returning nothing
- **Tier 1 documented.** The report was a massive statistical analysis undertaken by the US Air Force and Battelle Memorial Institute covering approximately 3,200 UFO reports. Source: The Black Vault document archive entry, 17 September 2020
- **Tier 1 documented.** The report was released publicly on October 25, 1955 by Department of Defense Office of Public Information Release No. 1053-55, headlined 'AIR FORCE RELEASES STUDY ON UNIDENTIFIED AERIAL OBJECTS'. Source: DoD Office of Public Information, Washington 25, D.C., Release No. 1053-55, October 25, 1955, reproduced at http://jfk.hood.edu/Collection/Weisberg%20Subject%20Index%20Files/D%20Disk/Davidson%20Leon%20Project%20Bluebook/Item%2002.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The report was republished by the Department of War as CIA-UAP-015 in PURSUE Release 03 in June 2026. Source: Department of War PURSUE release document repository
- **Tier 1 documented.** The report's Summary concludes that a thorough re-examination and re-evaluation of the cases of objects not originally identified 'led to the conclusion that this probability was very small', referring to the probability that any of the UNKNOWNS represented observations of technological developments not known to this country. Source: Special Report No. 14, Summary, p. vii, reproduced at http://jfk.hood.edu/Collection/Weisberg%20Subject%20Index%20Files/D%20Disk/Davidson%20Leon%20Project%20Bluebook/Item%2002.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The report's cover reads 'STUDY NO. 102-EL-55/2-79 / ANALYSIS OF REPORTS OF UNIDENTIFIED AERIAL OBJECTS / PROJECT NO. 10073 / 5 MAY 1955 / SPECIAL REPORT NO. 14', and it states 'No copyright material is contained in this publication.' Source: Project Blue Book Special Report No. 14, 5 May 1955, cover
- **Tier 1 documented.** The report's own Summary records that 'The first step in the analysis of the data revealed the existence of certain apparent similarities between cases of objects definitely identified and those not identified. Statistical methods of testing when applied indicated a low probability that these apparent similarities were significant.' Source: Special Report No. 14, Summary, p. vii, reproduced at http://jfk.hood.edu/Collection/Weisberg%20Subject%20Index%20Files/D%20Disk/Davidson%20Leon%20Project%20Bluebook/Item%2002.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The three per cent figure in the release refers to a different and much later sample: 'of the 131 sightings reported during the first four months of 1955 only three per cent were listed as unknown', not to the 3,201 cases analyzed in the report. Source: DoD Release No. 1053-55, October 25, 1955; http://jfk.hood.edu/Collection/Weisberg%20Subject%20Index%20Files/D%20Disk/Davidson%20Leon%20Project%20Bluebook/Item%2002.pdf
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** Battelle Memorial Institute performed the statistical analysis under contract to ATIC. Source: Standard attribution in the Blue Book literature; the report front matter does not name the contractor
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** Leon Davidson's published analysis of the report states that Figure 8 (original p. 24) shows Unknown sightings constituting 33.3% of all object sightings whose reliability is rated 'Excellent', and that Tables A1 through A3 (original pp. 107-108) show the Unknown percentage 'is not less than about 20%' even when lesser-reliability sightings are included. Source: Leon Davidson, 'Analysis of the Project Blue Book Report No. 14', p. D-4 of his photographic reprint edition; http://jfk.hood.edu/Collection/Weisberg%20Subject%20Index%20Files/D%20Disk/Davidson%20Leon%20Project%20Bluebook/Item%2002.pdf
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** On original page 295 of the report, in the codes for Final Identification, typewritten strikeovers show that the category originally called 'Rockets and Missiles' was changed to 'Insufficient Information', and the category originally called 'Electromagnetic Phenomenon' was changed to 'Unknown'. Source: Leon Davidson, analysis at p. D-10 discussing original p. 295, reproduced photographically; http://jfk.hood.edu/Collection/Weisberg%20Subject%20Index%20Files/D%20Disk/Davidson%20Leon%20Project%20Bluebook/Item%2002.pdf
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** Special Report No. 14 is the Blue Book report 'most important for UFO research, as it contained the study by Battelle Memorial Institute that analyzed the characteristics of UFOs in sightings that were unidentified'; the Battelle consulting work supporting Blue Book was performed under the designations Project Stork and Project Henry. Source: https://cufos.org/resources/project-blue-book/ (Center for UFO Studies; full report PDF at https://cufos.org/PDFs/books/Air_Force_Project_Blue_Book_Special_Report_14R.pdf)
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** The report's data show a higher proportion of unknowns among the highest-quality reports than among the poorest, and statistically significant differences between knowns and unknowns. Source: Project Blue Book Special Report No. 14, quality-of-report cross-tabulations
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** The unavailability of the full text to the public was taken up in the Record of Hearings of the House Subcommittee on Government Information, Rep. John E. Moss chairman, on November 15, 1956. Source: Leon Davidson, 'Analysis of the Project Blue Book Report No. 14', p. D-4; http://jfk.hood.edu/Collection/Weisberg%20Subject%20Index%20Files/D%20Disk/Davidson%20Leon%20Project%20Bluebook/Item%2002.pdf

## Ansel E. Talbert, three-part gravity series, New York Herald Tribune, 20-22 November 1955

Ansel E. Talbert, three-part gravity series, New York Herald Tribune, 20-22 November 1955 is a document on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1955-11-20 to 1955-11-22; axes C0 A0 D3; 6 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Three front-page articles by Ansel E. Talbert, Military and Aviation Editor, New York Herald Tribune, 20, 21 and 22 November 1955.

### What it is

Three consecutive front-page articles by Ansel E. Talbert, Military and Aviation Editor of the New York Herald Tribune, published on 20, 21 and 22 November 1955, all three page images opened and read in full.

### Why it is implicated

It is the contemporaneous press record behind every later claim that American aerospace executives said on the record in the 1950s that gravity could be controlled, and it is the document that dates and corrects those claims. It names the firms, quotes Lawrence D. Bell and George S. Trimble jr. directly, reports the Chapel Hill gravity institute under an earlier name, and mentions Thomas Townsend Brown nowhere in its three parts.

### The longer account

This series is the load-bearing document under a whole branch of the electrogravitics literature, and reading it moves four things at once. It dates the reporting to November 1955 rather than 1953. It gives George Trimble his actual office, vice-president in charge of advanced design planning of Martin Aircraft, rather than chief executive, and shows that his atom-bomb comparison is about the time the bomb took and not about the money it cost. It names the Sperry Gyroscope Division of Sperry-Rand among the firms with scientists on the gravity problem, which is the documented attachment for a node the 30 August pass left deliberately unattached. And it reports the University of North Carolina trustees approving a gravity institute earlier that month under the name Institute of Pure Physics, with Gordon Gray's approval, Agnew H. Bahnson jr. as fundraiser and the directorship offered to Bryce S. DeWitt, which is the Institute of Field Physics before it had its final name. What it does not contain is the figure the electrogravitics claim usually rests on: Thomas Townsend Brown is named in none of the three articles, and neither Winterhaven nor any Navy or Air Force electrogravitics contract appears in them. That absence is carried here as a claim and deliberately not as an edge.

Roles the board assigns it: disclosure, research.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether the Herald Tribune ran any follow-up or correction to the series.
- What the 'Institute of Pure Physics' name change to Institute of Field Physics turned on, which the series does not say.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 506 of 1057, score 29). 6 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A0/D3.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** George S. Trimble jr. is identified as 'vice-president in charge of advanced design planning of Martin Aircraft Corp.' and as 'organizing a new Research Institute for Advanced Study', and is quoted: 'I think we could do the job in about the time that it actually required to build the first atom bomb if enough trained scientific brain-power simultaneously began thinking about and working towards a solution.' Source: Talbert, New York Herald Tribune, 20 November 1955 (caption and continuation p. 36) and 22 November 1955 (continuation p. 10). https://archive.org/download/talbert1ny/talbert1ny_djvu.txt and https://archive.org/download/talbert3ny/talbert3ny_djvu.txt
- **Tier 1 documented.** Lawrence D. Bell, founder and president of Bell Aircraft Corp. of Buffalo, is quoted directly: 'Aviation as we know it is on the threshold of amazing new concepts', and 'Our next step will be to prove that we can operate outside the earth's atmosphere and the third will be to operate outside the gravity of the earth.' Source: Ansel E. Talbert, 'New Air Dream, Planes Flying Outside Gravity', New York Herald Tribune, 22 November 1955, p. 1. Page image at https://archive.org/download/talbert3ny/talbert3ny_djvu.txt
- **Tier 1 documented.** The series lists the firms: 'Aircraft industry firms now participating or actively interested in gravity include the Glenn L. Martin Co. of Baltimore ... Convair of San Diego ... Bell Aircraft of Buffalo ... and Sikorsky Division of United Aircraft', and adds 'Lear, Inc., of Santa Monica ... Clarke Electronics, of Palm Springs, Calif., and the Sperry Gyroscope Division of Sperry-Rand Corp., of Great Neck, L. I.' Source: Ansel E. Talbert, 'Conquest of Gravity Aim Of Top Scientists in U. S.', New York Herald Tribune, 20 November 1955, p. 1 and continuation p. 36. Page image at https://archive.org/download/talbert1ny/talbert1ny_djvu.txt
- **Tier 1 documented.** The series reports the Chapel Hill institute at its founding, under an earlier name: 'A proposal to establish at the University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill, N. C., an Institute of Pure Physics primarily to carry on theoretical research on gravity was approved earlier this month by the university's board of trustees. This had the approval of Dr. Gordon Gray, who has since retired as president of the university.' It names Agnew H. Bahnson jr. as the fundraiser and says the directorship had been offered to Bryce S. DeWitt. Source: Talbert, New York Herald Tribune, 20 November 1955, continuation p. 36. https://archive.org/download/talbert1ny/talbert1ny_djvu.txt
- **Tier 1 documented.** Thomas Townsend Brown is named in none of the three articles, and neither Winterhaven nor any Navy or Air Force electrogravitics contract appears in them. Source: All three articles read in full: https://archive.org/download/talbert1ny/talbert1ny_djvu.txt, https://archive.org/download/talbert2ny/talbert2ny_djvu.txt, https://archive.org/download/talbert3ny/talbert3ny_djvu.txt
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** On the Danny Jones Podcast, episode 248, 8 July 2024, at 2:34:20 to 2:35:18, Jason Reza Jorjani dated the Herald Tribune reporting to 1953, attributed it to Martin's chief executive, and said the executive claimed Manhattan-Project-scale funding could put such engines into production in a few years. The articles are dated November 1955, the speaker in them is a vice-president in charge of advanced design planning, and the comparison he draws is to the time the first atom bomb took rather than to its funding. Source: Danny Jones Podcast, ep. 248, 8 July 2024, 2:34:20 to 2:35:18, mapped at research/leads/dannyjones-corpus-12.md

The link reads: Reports the UNC trustees approving the institute in November 1955 under the name Institute of Pure Physics. # 1970-1999: Post-Blue Book

## COMETA - Comité d'Études Approfondies

COMETA - Comité d'Études Approfondies is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1996 to 2006; axes C0 A1 D3; 11 connections; in the research band.

In one line: French 1901-law association of retired general officers and IHEDN auditors, 1996-c.2006.

### What it is

Retired French generals, admirals and IHEDN auditors put the defence question in writing and named the extraterrestrial hypothesis

### Why it is implicated

COMETA is the document that has no American equivalent. A committee of retired French flag officers - an air force general in the chair, a vice-admiral, a weapons engineer general, a chief of police from the Interior Ministry, a research director from ONERA - spent close to three years reviewing UAP case material, wrote ninety pages under the title 'UFOs and Defense: What Should We Prepare For?', concluded that the extraterrestrial hypothesis could not be excluded and was the most credible of the available explanations, and put their names on it. It was not commissioned by the French state, and its authors are careful about that. But it was written by people the French state had trained to think about national defence, using material from the French state's own UAP office, and it reads as a defence staff study rather than a polemic.

### The longer account

COMETA is the document that has no American equivalent. A committee of retired French flag officers - an air force general in the chair, a vice-admiral, a weapons engineer general, a chief of police from the Interior Ministry, a research director from ONERA - spent close to three years reviewing UAP case material, wrote ninety pages under the title 'UFOs and Defense: What Should We Prepare For?', concluded that the extraterrestrial hypothesis could not be excluded and was the most credible of the available explanations, and put their names on it. It was not commissioned by the French state, and its authors are careful about that. But it was written by people the French state had trained to think about national defence, using material from the French state's own UAP office, and it reads as a defence staff study rather than a polemic.

The committee's institutional centre of gravity is the Institut des Hautes Études de Défense Nationale. Almost every named member is an IHEDN auditor, identified by promotion number: Michel Algrin, doctor of political science and attorney-at-law (AA 35); Pierre Bescond, Weapons Engineer General, 2nd Section (AA 48); Denis Blancher, Chief of Police, Police Nationale, Ministry of the Interior; Jean Dunglas, honorary engineer general (AR 48); Bruno Le Moine, Air Force General, 2nd Section (AA 41); Françoise Lepine of the Fondation pour les Études de Défense (AA 33); Christian Marchal, research director at ONERA; Marc Merlo, Admiral, 2nd Section (AA 35); and Alain Orszag, Weapons Engineer General, 2nd Section. General Denis Letty, Air Force, 2nd Section (AA 35), chaired it. The report carries a foreword by Professor André Lebeau, former chairman of CNES, and one by General Bernard Norlain, former director of IHEDN, whom Letty visited at IHEDN in March 1995 to explain the project. Letty thanks both, saying that without them 'COMETA would not have been born'.

The report's own account of its lineage matters for this board because it closes a loop back to GEIPAN. COMETA's introduction states that in 1976 an IHEDN committee chaired by General Blanchard of the Gendarmerie Nationale opened the unidentified flying objects file with the objective of making proposals for organising research and data collection, that 'the goal was achieved, because the recommendations of this committee were followed in the creation of' GEPAN, and that twenty years later it seemed useful to take stock again. In other words the same institution produced both the French state's UAP office and, two decades on, the committee that reviewed its output.

The evidentiary base is the French official record. Contributors included Jean-Jacques Velasco, then head of SEPRA at CNES; François Louange, chief executive of the image-analysis firm Fleximage; the pilots Jean-Charles Duboc, Jean-Pierre Fartek and René Giraud; Edmond Campagnac, former technical director of Air France at Antananarivo; Squadron Commander Michel Perrier of the Gendarmerie Nationale; an official of the DGAC; and Air Force General Joseph Domange. Letty specifically thanks the commander of the Air Force Air Operations Command Center for its participation in the investigation into Air France flight AF 3532 of 28 January 1994.

On substance, COMETA reproduces GEPAN/SEPRA's four-category scheme and then states the number that the whole document turns on: 'Category D UAPs represent 4 to 5% of the cases and are called UAP Ds.' Category D means unidentified despite the abundance and quality of the data - not unidentified for want of it. The report's position is that a residue of that size, often attested by consistent testimony and by visual sightings coupled with radar, can no longer be filed as no action: 'if there had only been ten or so UAP D [sightings], this ambiguous file could just have been classified as "no action," but we are no longer at that point and are far beyond that. Thus we are forced to seek plausible explanations.' It records around one hundred GEPAN/SEPRA investigations, several of which 'demonstrated, in the end, the physical presence of a phenomenon the nature and origin of which remain unknown', singling out Trans-en-Provence (8 January 1981) and 'Amaranth' (21 October 1982), documented in GEPAN technical memoranda 16 and 17. On aeronautical cases it records twelve French cases, only three or four of them category D; the first, in 1951, involving Vampire aircraft near Orange; and AF 3532 on 28 January 1994 as the first French case of a visual sighting correlated with radar over fifty seconds. It also draws on Dominique Weinstein's enumeration of 489 well-documented aeronautical UAP D cases worldwide since 1942, most from official sources.

COMETA is structured in three parts - facts and testimonies, the extent of current knowledge, and UFOs and defence - plus a conclusion and seven appendices, among them 'Radar Detection in France', 'Life in the Universe', 'Colonization of Space' and 'The Roswell Affair - Disinformation'. Its recommendations are set out in the introduction: give civilian and military pilots enough information to adopt adapted conduct when faced with these phenomena, and inform the public and decision-makers more generally; develop the actions of SEPRA and promote supplemental scientific monitoring and research; and consider the strategic, political and religious consequences of a possible confirmation of the extraterrestrial hypothesis, 'the bizarre connotation of which it is advisable to eliminate here and now'. Letty's own framing, in the foreword, is the sentence most often quoted from the document and is verbatim: 'The accumulation of well-documented sightings made by credible witnesses forces us to consider from now on all of the hypotheses regarding the origin of unidentified flying objects, or UFOs, and the extraterrestrial hypothesis, in particular.'

COMETA the association was registered under the French law of 1901 on 24 February 1999, having operated informally since 1996, and went dormant around 2006. The report appeared in July 1999 in a special issue of VSD, was reissued by Éditions du Rocher in 2003, and was not widely circulated in its original form because of copyright restrictions. According to Robert Roussel, citing Olivier Schrameck, Prime Minister Lionel Jospin read and annotated it 'to mark his interest'. There was no formal government response beyond that. GEIPAN made an archive of the report public in 2007.

Roles the board assigns it: research, disclosure.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 117 of 1057, score 56). 11 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A1/D3.

- **Tier 1 documented.** Acknowledged contributors include 'Jean-Jacques Velasco, Head of SEPRA at [CNES]', François Louange, CEO of Fleximage, pilots Jean-Charles Duboc, Jean-Pierre Fartek and René Giraud, Edmond Campagnac, former technical director of Air France at Antananarivo, Squadron Commander Michel Perrier of the Gendarmerie Nationale, an official of the DGAC, and Air Force General Joseph Domange. Source: COMETA report, ch. 7.4 - https://ia800403.us.archive.org/17/items/pdfy-NRIQie2ooVehep7K/The%20Cometa%20Report%20%5BUFO%27s%20And%20Defense%20-%20What%20Should%20We%20Prepare%20For%5D.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** Chapter 7.5: twelve French aeronautical cases were known to GEPAN/SEPRA, 'only three or four of these can be considered to fall into category D'; the first UAP D dates to 1951 involving Vampire military aircraft near Orange; and not until 28 January 1994 did an Air France crew produce 'the first case of a visual sighting correlated with a radar detection over 50 seconds long'. A worldwide list compiled by Dominique Weinstein describes '489 well-documented cases of aeronautical UAP D sightings the sources of which were duly verified'. ### Its strongest connections

- COMETA - Comité d'Études Approfondies runs out to **Project Blue Book** (program, board tier A), a research link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: adopts Hynek's Blue Book-era classification. Hynek, an astronomer and consultant to the USAF, within the context of the Blue Book Project'. A committee of retired French flag officers adopting Blue Book's own consultant's taxonomy is the inheritance this edge records. COMETA records that 'statistical studies in the USSR yielded distributions comparable to those determined by Claude Poher, the first head of GEPAN, from some 200 French cases, or 1,000 cases worldwide.' - COMETA - Comité d'Études Approfondies runs out to **Paris, France** (location, board tier C), a institutional link at Tier 3 stated inference. The link reads: IHEDN origin; delivered to the Prime Minister. TIER 3. What the report establishes: General Norlain, then director of the IHEDN, writes that 'when General Letty visited me in March 1995 at my office at IHEDN to explain to me his project for creating a new fact-finding committee on UFOs, I assured him of my interest and referred him to the management of the IHEDN Auditors Association'. What it does not establish is the city. Paris appears nowhere in the report in connection with COMETA; the IHEDN sits at the Ecole militaire in Paris, but that is knowledge brought to the document rather than read from it. Nor does the report record delivery to the head of government - it says only that its authors believed 'sufficient questions of national interest are raised for the Chief of State and the Prime Minister to be provided with this information.' ## SETKA-AN / SETKA-MO (later Galaktika, then Gorizont) - Soviet state anomalous phenomena programme

SETKA-AN / SETKA-MO (later Galaktika, then Gorizont) - Soviet state anomalous phenomena programme is a program on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1978-10-18 to 1991; axes C1 A2 D2; 11 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Twin USSR Academy of Sciences and Ministry of Defence programmes, 1978-1990/91.

### What it is

Twin USSR Academy of Sciences and Ministry of Defence programmes, 1978-1990. The Soviet Union turned its entire army into a standing UAP sensor network for thirteen years, and then published

### Why it is implicated

SETKA is the largest state UAP data-collection effort ever run, and almost nothing about it looks like the American arrangement. It was created by a bureaucratic decision minuted at the Kremlin, funded out of nobody's budget, executed by fewer than a dozen people, mandated in writing to every Soviet serviceman, and - the part that matters here - written up afterwards by the two men who ran it and published in the Academy of Sciences' own journal.

### The longer account

SETKA is the largest state UAP data-collection effort ever run, and almost nothing about it looks like the American arrangement. It was created by a bureaucratic decision minuted at the Kremlin, funded out of nobody's budget, executed by fewer than a dozen people, mandated in writing to every Soviet serviceman, and - the part that matters here - written up afterwards by the two men who ran it and published in the Academy of Sciences' own journal.

The trigger was the Petrozavodsk phenomenon of 20 September 1977, a large-scale luminous event seen across the north-west USSR and into Finland by ambulance crews, militia, dockers, military personnel and airport staff, and reported in Izvestia three days later under the headline 'The Unidentified natural phenomenon'. Local authorities asked the Presidium of the Academy of Sciences to explain it; letters arrived from northern European governments asking whether the effects came from military-technical experiments and endangered the environment. Under that pressure the President of the Academy of Sciences, Academician Anatoly Aleksandrov, wrote to L. Smirnov, Vice-President of the government and Chairman of the Military-Industrial Commission, asking that a complex research programme on anomalous phenomena be started with Ministry of Defence and war-industry involvement. The MIC Scientific and Technical Council met in the Kremlin in October 1977 under Lieutenant-General B. A. Kijasov. Members noted that reports of extraordinary phenomena also reached Ministry of Defence organisations from servicemen, and had sometimes coincided with equipment failures.

The meeting recommended, and the MIC under the Council of Ministers adopted, the inclusion of two topics in the 1978 state plan of defence research: 'Setka MO' (Ministry of Defence) - 'Research of paranormal atmospheric and space phenomena and their influence on the operation of military technical equipment and personnel' - and 'Setka AS' (Academy of Sciences) - 'Research of a physical nature and mechanisms of paranormal atmospheric and space phenomena'. The programme ran without interruption for thirteen years, to 1990, and was re-approved in the five-year defence research plans of 1981 and 1986. Platov and Sokolov note that the October 1977 STC meeting 'was the first and last measure with a so high official level of the participants engaged in the problem'.

The execution was tiny. The military branch was led from one of the central military research institutes in the Moscow region, with its chief V. Balashov - an expert on radiation and other damaging factors on military equipment - as scientific head, and four to five specialists doing the collection, processing and analysis. Around fifteen further Ministry of Defence and war-industry organisations participated voluntarily and very limitedly. The academic branch was led by Academician V. Migulin, director of IZMIRAN, which was designated head institute; the working group there, also four to five people, was headed by Yu. Platov. Budget financing covered salaries and travel, drawn from the executing organisations' own budgets, with no special allocation - 'the most inexpensive one, if not the cheapest, among the scientific works on defensive subjects'.

The instrument that made it work was a document authorised by the Chief of the General Staff in January 1980 and distributed to the troops as a General Staff Directive. It designated the central military research institute as the collection, processing and analysis centre for all UAP information obtained by the military; named the research institutes responsible in all five arms; set the procedure by which the centre could obtain identification data, above all launch data for military aerospace systems; and fixed the rules for documenting detections and the reporting order by urgency. The authors' own summary is the sentence worth having verbatim: the directive 'for 13 years put the army into the on-duty regime of observation of the paranormal phenomena in the places of the army dislocation, i.e. practically on the whole territory of the USSR'. Every serviceman became, as they put it, 'without knowing it' a potential participant, obliged to report any inexplicable phenomenon in writing.

The work was classified for three stated reasons: it sat inside the classified defence research plan; a military-technical origin for the effects was thought likely at the outset; and there was a possible military application of properties such as absence of radar contrast and high manoeuvrability. The word 'UFO' was not used in official documents, which said 'anomalous phenomenon' instead - partly because it fitted the observations better, partly to keep clear of popular agitation about aliens arriving on flying saucers.

The results are what a large sensor network with a small analysis cell produces. Over thirteen years about three thousand messages arrived, describing more than 400 distinct events after mass-observation reports were consolidated. More than 90% were resolved as effects accompanying rocket and space launches - sunlight scattered off gas-and-dust clouds from rocket exhaust, visible over hundreds of kilometres in twilight geometry - or as high-altitude balloons, which accounted for 10-12% of messages. The Petrozavodsk event itself was attributed to the launch of Kosmos 955 from Plesetsk. Notably, reports did not come in from units at or near test ranges, where such effects were familiar. The 1984-87 series of flight emergencies at the Borisoglebsk air unit in Voronezh region, where unknown objects appeared on radar and were seen by pilots, the authors leave explicitly unidentified.

And then the finding that makes the programme worth a node rather than a footnote. Across thirteen years of compulsory reporting spanning roughly a sixth of the Earth's land surface, the programme received no report of a landing, no report of contact with occupants, and no report of an abduction. Platov and Sokolov put the conclusion as a fork: either the USSR was closed to alien visitation for at least thirteen years, or the extraterrestrial-origin hypothesis is inconsistent. They also dismiss the 1990s wave of claims about 'KGB secret files' and 'classified capture of UFO' as absurd. Whatever one makes of the conclusions, the contrast with the American record is the point: the Soviet state ran the collection openly enough inside its own system to mandate it army-wide, and its coordinators published the account under their own names in Vestnik Rossiiskoi Akademii Nauk in 2000.

Roles the board assigns it: research, disclosure.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 191 of 1057, score 45). 11 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C1/A2/D2.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** 'During the work on the program (for 13 years) about three thousand of messages reporting about extraordinary phenomena have been obtained ... on the whole, more than 400 events were registered and identified as extraordinary or paranormal.' Over 90% were resolved as rocket-launch effects or high-altitude balloons, the balloon class accounting for 10-12% of messages. The Petrozavodsk phenomenon itself was attributed to 'the launch of an artificial Earth satellite "Kosmos 955" from the space range in Plesetsk'. Reports 'did not practically come' from units at or near test ranges, where such effects were familiar. ### Its strongest connections

- SETKA-AN / SETKA-MO (later Galaktika, then Gorizont) - Soviet state anomalous phenomena programme runs out to **United States Department of Defense (secondary designation: Department of War)** (institution, board tier A), a behavioral link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: opposite reporting posture: mandated versus stigmatised. A January 1980 Directive of the Soviet General Staff obliged every serviceman to report any inexplicable phenomenon in writing and routed the results to a single analysis centre, putting the army 'into the on-duty regime of observation' for thirteen years. The contrast with the US chain of command over the same period, where reporting carried career risk and no service-wide collection mandate existed, is the comparison this node exists to make. The Soviet side of the comparison is documented by the programme's own coordinators; no institutional contact between the two is claimed. ## CEFAA / SEFAA - Chilean anomalous aerial phenomena office, Dirección General de Aeronáutica Civil

CEFAA / SEFAA - Chilean anomalous aerial phenomena office, Dirección General de Aeronáutica Civil is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1997-10-03; axes C1 A1 D3; 9 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Chilean civil aviation UAP office, 1997-present (renamed SEFAA 2021).

### What it is

A civil aviation regulator that has treated UAP as a flight-safety problem, on the record, since 1997

### Why it is implicated

Chile's UAP office exists because pilots kept reporting things and a civil aviation authority decided that was an aviation-safety matter rather than an embarrassment. That framing - not disclosure, not exobiology, but the safety of air operations in 32 million square kilometres of airspace - is what has kept it alive for nearly thirty years and what makes it the South American counterweight on this board.

### The longer account

Chile's UAP office exists because pilots kept reporting things and a civil aviation authority decided that was an aviation-safety matter rather than an embarrassment. That framing - not disclosure, not exobiology, but the safety of air operations in 32 million square kilometres of airspace - is what has kept it alive for nearly thirty years and what makes it the South American counterweight on this board.

The prehistory runs back to 1968. Anomalous aerial phenomena were first reported officially in Chile at the old Oficina Meteorológica de Chile in the Quinta Normal aeronautical complex in Santiago, where the director, Aviation Colonel Sergio Bravo Flores, began collecting reports and led the Comisión Chilena para Estudios de Fenómenos Espaciales No Identificados, formed with the support of the Sociedad Científica de Chile. Bravo's first act, on 9 July 1968, was to circulate 43 aeronautical installations from Arica to the Chilean Antarctic asking for reports. The commission - a meteorologist from Línea Aérea Nacional, an engineer, a physician, a doctor of physical and mathematical sciences, a Chilean Air Force lieutenant seconded to the aeronautics directorate, and several engineers - stated its object as establishing 'with all seriousness the true causes' of the matter. In seven years it gathered around a dozen reports, some of which were forwarded to the University of Colorado, where the panel later known as the Condon Committee was at work. It wound up in 1975 when Bravo retired.

The modern office was created twenty-two years later. A wave of sightings reported over Arica in the last days of March and first days of April 1997, together with the expressed interest of the former Commander-in-Chief of the Chilean Air Force, General del Aire Ramón Vega Hidalgo, moved the Director General of Civil Aeronautics, Aviation General Gonzalo Miranda Aguirre, to create the Comité de Estudios de Fenómenos Aéreos Anómalos on 3 October 1997, by Resolución Exenta N° 01599. The resolution sited the committee at the Escuela Técnica Aeronáutica in Quinta Normal, Santiago, and on 14 October 1997 the school's director, Colonel Enzo Di Nocera García, set out its organisation and operating basis and named its members: himself as head, air traffic controller Gustavo Rodríguez Navarro, and the heads of the air traffic and meteorology areas as consultants, with ETA specialists and external organisations convened as needed. In practice Rodríguez Navarro carried the office's work through its early years.

The institutional history is not a straight line, and the dossier is stronger for saying so. Di Nocera led the committee until 1998, when Air Brigade General Ricardo Bermúdez Sanhueza took over the ETA and with it CEFAA, running it to February 2002. Air Brigade General Hernán Henríquez Cobaise took the directorship in March 2002 and held it to 31 March 2008, and the DGAC's own account describes the period from March 2002 to March 2008 as 'una etapa de latencia' - a dormancy. On 15 December 2009, by Resolución N° 02/0/01937, Director General José Huepe Pérez ordered the committee to operate from 1 January 2010 reporting directly to the Secretaría General, detaching it from the ETA and putting its members on permanent, exclusively investigative postings. Bermúdez returned to the directorship in 2009 and served to 2016; Hugo Camus Palacios succeeded him. On 18 October 2021, Resolución Exenta N° 04/3/0113/1385 repealed the 1997 resolution and renamed the office the Sección de Estudios de Fenómenos Aéreos Anómalos, moving it under the DGAC's Subdepartamento Comunicacional. It is now based at Av. Pedro Aguirre Cerda 5100, Cerrillos, Santiago.

Its remit is defined by airspace, not by belief. SEFAA's mission is to support the safety of air operations in the national territory and in the airspace of DGAC responsibility, which extends across 32 million square kilometres from Arica in the north, between 120° and 131° west, to the South Pole. Its principal source is pilot reports - military, commercial, private, sport and institutional - supplemented by public reports, and it explicitly reserves the right to select which material is investigable. Investigations are grounded in the scientific method and depend heavily on audiovisual records. A digital case database was created in 2018; by September 2021 the office had received 1,675 cases in total, with 362 arriving in 2020 alone, nearly a 300% jump on prior years, and 300 more by September 2021. Since 2020 investigated and resolved cases have been published on the office's website, with reports made available to the public quarterly, and resolved-case pages remain live by year through 2026. The permanent staff is small - in 2018 the committee comprised its director and two investigators - but it draws on an external research committee of astronomers, geographers, physicists, psychologists, a nuclear chemist, aerospace medicine specialists, air traffic controllers, a meteorologist, aeronautical investigators, inspector pilots, aeronautical engineers, image analysts and armed forces representatives. On its 24th anniversary the DGAC described CEFAA as 'uno de los organismos públicos más antiguos del continente' and as leading a transparency model comparable, allowing for the difference in human and budgetary resources, to a European government body.

Its most visible case is the Chilean Navy footage of 11 November 2014, in which an Airbus Cougar AS-532 helicopter crew recorded roughly ten minutes of infrared video of an object off the coast west of Santiago. CEFAA investigated for over two years and released the footage with an official report in January 2017; Bermúdez, who had directed the investigation, summarised the outcome as knowing what it was not rather than what it was. The case is also the clearest documented instance of European-South American technical cooperation on UAP: photo and video analysts in France, including François Louange, were brought in through GEIPAN, and GEIPAN's analysts proposed a mundane explanation - a medium-haul aircraft venting - that CEFAA rejected. Independent analysts subsequently matched the timestamps and geometry to scheduled flights. The disagreement is the point: two state UAP offices on different continents worked the same tape, reached different conclusions, and both published.

That visibility is now institutional. Canada's Office of the Chief Science Advisor, designing a national UAP reporting system in 2025, described SEFAA as one of only three national models worth examining alongside the United States and France, and recommended that Canada's lead organisation establish partnerships with AARO and NASA, GEIPAN and SEFAA.

Roles the board assigns it: research, disclosure.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 84 of 1057, score 67). 9 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C1/A1/D3.
