The link reads: produced at Whitehall, released via TNA at Kew. Both ends of the Condign story sit in London: the MoD secretariat that handled UFO material was at Whitehall, and the MoD's historical UFO files went to The National Archives at Kew under a five-year transfer programme whose releases run from August 2009 through 2013. The guide describes the transfer programme but does not date the reporting desk's closure, so that detail is not asserted here. The link reads: 1952 recommendation to acquire the technology prefigures Condign's redacted 'Related Military Applications'. Smith's 1952 report recommends 'acquisition of as much as possible of this technology'; Condign's own paragraph 7, 'Related Military Applications', is redacted in full under the defence exemption. Reasoning: both are government documents whose exploitation sections are the point at which the record becomes either explicit or withheld. Sources: LAC - Project Magnet page, https://web.archive.org/web/2006/http://www.collectionscanada.ca/ufo/002029-1400.01-e.html, dated 2005-08-11; Condign Executive Summary, https://archive.org/download/condign-vol-2-1-258/uap_vol1_pgs87to99_ch4to5_djvu.txt, dated 2006-05-15.

The link reads: key recommendation executed nine years later. Condign's single key recommendation was that DI55 should no longer be required to monitor UAP reports and that the database should not be maintained; the desk that fed it closed in 2009 on the same stated ground. ## Arquivo Nacional (Brazil) - UFO/UAP collection

Arquivo Nacional (Brazil) - UFO/UAP collection is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2009; axes C1 A0 D3; 8 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Arquivo Nacional (Brazil), Fundo Objeto Voador Não Identificado – OVNI, reference BR DFANBSB ARX, 743 registros, produced by the Comando da Aeronáutica.

### What it is

The Fundo Objeto Voador Nao Identificado (OVNI), reference code BR DFANBSB ARX, is a discrete archival collection held by Brazil's Arquivo Nacional, the national archives, and produced by the Comando da Aeronautica. It contains reports, occurrence questionnaires, correspondence, photographs, drawings, video, audio and press clippings concerning objects observed in Brazilian skies. It is open to the public and searchable online through SIAN, the Arquivo Nacional's information system.

### Why it is implicated

This is the physical answer to the question the US disclosure fight keeps asking in the abstract: what does it actually look like when a state moves its military UAP holdings into civilian custody and publishes them. It looks like a reference code, a finding aid, a login page and a holding count. The archive is the endpoint of the pipeline the FAB built through Portaria 551/GC3, and its existence dismantles the premise that this material is intrinsically unreleasable, because a NATO-adjacent air force with a real air defence mission released it and continues to accession into it annually. It is also honest about its own limits, and the board should be too: the collection covers one service, the Aeronautica, and not the Army or the Navy, and its holding count is reported differently by the archive and by the Senate. Those limits do not weaken the comparison. They sharpen it, because even a partial, imperfect, single-service release is more than the United States has produced in seventy-nine years.

### The longer account

Brazil's national archive holds the world's most accessible body of military records on unidentified aerial objects, and it holds them under an ordinary archival reference code with an ordinary finding aid, which is precisely what makes it remarkable. The Arquivo Nacional describes the collection plainly on its own website: the Fundo Objeto Voador Nao Identificado, BR DFANBSB ARX, produced by the Comando da Aeronautica do Brasil and held in the archive's custody, composed of 743 registros comprising reports, occurrence questionnaires, correspondence, photographs, drawings, videos, audio and clippings from newspapers and magazines about objects seen in Brazilian skies over the years. The earliest occurrence in the fund dates from 1952 and, per the archive's own description, the most recent from 2016. Access runs through SIAN, the Sistema de Informacoes do Arquivo Nacional, and a curated image set is published on the archive's Pinterest account, which is not a detail one expects from a body of military intelligence records.

The holding count is contested and the board should present both figures rather than pick one. The Arquivo Nacional says 743 registros. The Brazilian Senate's own record, in the justification to RQS 193/2022, says that searching the term OVNI alone returns 137 records but that entering the specific reference code BR DFANBSB ARX returns 758 documents. These are almost certainly counting different things, registros as archival units versus digitised items as retrieved by a code search, and the gap may also reflect accruals between the two dates. Neither figure is wrong and neither is authoritative for the other's purpose. Both are cited here. Layered on top, the ufologist A. J. Gevaerd told the Senate plenary in June 2022 that the Brazilian government and the Aeronautica had released more than 20,000 pages, which is a page count rather than an item count and is not in conflict with either.

The collection's provenance runs through the FAB's documentation and archival machinery rather than through litigation or leak. From 2007 the Aeronautica began sending its papers to the archive. In April 2009 CENDOC forwarded the first Operacao Prato material. In August 2010 Portaria no 551/GC3 formalised the flow, making the archive the default destination for reports received through the aerospace operations command. The consequence is that the fund is not a historical dump frozen at a cut-off date but a live series. In August 2026 Brazilian media reporting off documents released through the archive identified at least eighteen unidentified-object cases reported in Brazil during 2025 by pilots, controllers and other aviation personnel, with five clustered at Londrina in Parana and four in Ceara, including a LATAM captain's report near Guarulhos on 1 September 2025 and a Londrina tower controller's 23 November 2025 report of a white, round object that appeared and disappeared on the traffic display. A citizen can read the form.

The archive is not an advocate, and its framing is worth cataloguing precisely because it is not. Its public page carries an explicit caveat that OVNI does not necessarily mean flying saucers or extraterrestrials, and that the term designates any flying object whose origin was not immediately identified, which may include satellites, drones, balloons or natural phenomena. On this board that sentence is an artefact rather than a rebuttal: it is the institutional hedge a records body attaches when it publishes material a defence ministry produced, and its presence tells you the archive expected the material to be read as more than administrative residue. The important thing is that the hedge is attached to published records rather than used as a reason not to publish.

The boundary of the collection is the sharpest single fact in this dossier. The Senate record states without qualification that from 2007 it was the Aeronautica, and not the Exercito and not the Marinha, that sent its papers. Brazilian researchers went to the Ministry of Defence in April 2013, at the invitation of then Foreign Minister Celso Amorim and before the service commanders, specifically to press for the Army and Navy holdings. Those holdings have not been released. So the Fundo OVNI is a one-service archive, which means Brazil's disclosure is real but partial, and means the comparison with the United States should be drawn carefully: Brazil is not a state that opened everything, it is a state where one armed service unilaterally chose publication as its default and the other two did not. That variance is the most useful thing about it, because it isolates the decision. The material did not force the outcome. The institution chose it. And in the United States, no service has chosen it, at any point, in any decade, for any category of this material.

### What it has denied

The archive itself does not deny; it disclaims. Its public description states that OVNI does not necessarily mean flying saucers or extraterrestrials and that the label covers satellites, drones, balloons and natural phenomena. That is a records body inoculating itself against the reading its own holdings invite, and it is catalogued here as an institutional hedge attached to published material rather than as a reason for withholding it.

Roles the board assigns it: custody, disclosure, research.

### Open questions on this node

- The 743 versus 758 discrepancy has not been reconciled. The two figures come from different bodies, are counting differently (archival registros versus documents returned by a reference-code search), and are separated in time. No authoritative reconciliation exists in the sources located.
- The Arquivo Nacional gives 2016 as the fund's most recent occurrence while the FAB states transfers run through 2025 and 2025 reports are demonstrably being released. Whether the archive page is simply out of date, or whether post-2016 accruals sit under a separate series, is unresolved.
- The Army and Navy holdings referred to in the Senate record and sought at the April 2013 Ministry of Defence meeting have not been released and their extent is unknown.
- Whether any material was withheld, redacted or reclassified during the transfers from CENDOC and GSI is not addressed in any located source.
- Whether the fund contains any material relating to physical residue, recovered objects or materials analysis has not been established from the finding aid.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 64 of 1057, score 73). 10 connections, 6 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C1/A0/D3.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** Accession-by-accession structure is documented: a 2011 parliamentary requirement refers to 'o terceiro recolhimento do CENDOC (FAB) ao Arquivo Nacional (COREG)', citing 'envelope nº 2 do terceiro recolhimento', referring to the year 1981; and records that the Minister of Defence had stated CENDOC 'encerraria no dia 31 de agosto de 2010 a remessa de todo o seu acervo à Coordenação Regional do Arquivo Nacional no Distrito Federal (COREG)'. Source: Câmara dos Deputados, RIC 679/2011, justificação and item 2.4, 22 June 2011, referencing RIC 4470/2009 answered by Minister Nelson Jobim via Ofício 4225-GM/Aspar-MD of 20 April 2010
- **Tier 1 documented.** CONTRADICTION RESOLVED - the 2016 date is a stale description, not a fund closing date. The Arquivo Nacional's own statement - 'Ele é composto de 743 registros' and 'A primeira ocorrência do Fundo Objeto Voador Não Identificado data de 1952 e a mais recente de 2016' - appears on an institutional news item carrying the byline 'Publicado em 13/09/2018 18h25 Atualizado em 13/09/2018 18h27'. It is a snapshot as of September 2018 that has never been revised, not a description of an accession that ended. Source: Arquivo Nacional, 'Conheça o fundo sobre OVNIs do Arquivo Nacional', published 13/09/2018 (https://www.gov.br/arquivonacional/pt-br/canais_atendimento/imprensa/noticias/conheca-o-fundo-sobre-ovnis-do-arquivo-nacional)
- **Tier 1 documented.** HOLDING-COUNT DISCREPANCY RESOLVED - 743 and 758 count different things at different dates by different methods. 743 is the Arquivo Nacional's own 2018 description of the fund's registros. 758 is a SIAN search-hit count reported in 2022: 'Atualmente, no Arquivo Nacional, quando se pesquisa apenas o termo OVNI, se encontram 137 registros, mas se colocarmos na pesquisa um código de referência específico (BR DFANBSB ARX), encontraremos 758 documentos.' The same sentence yields 137 for a free-text search, proving these are query results, not holdings inventories. Neither number is a stable figure for an accruing fund. Source: Senado Federal, RQS 193/2022, justificação; and Arquivo Nacional news item 13/09/2018

- **Tier 1 documented.** The 2016 endpoint was still being repeated by COMAER in 2022 litigation, showing the lag is institutional and not confined to the Arquivo Nacional: 'a coleção filmográfica, iconográfica, sonora e textual referente aos anos de 1952 e 2016, produzida pelo COMAER, está disponível para consulta no acervo do Arquivo Nacional.' - **Tier 1 documented.** The Arquivo Nacional attaches an explicit caveat to its description of the fund, stating that OVNI does not necessarily signify the existence of flying saucers or extraterrestrials and that the term designates any flying object whose origin was not immediately identified, which may include satellites, drones, balloons or natural phenomena. Source: Arquivo Nacional, 'Conheca o fundo sobre OVNIs do Arquivo Nacional', https://www.gov.br/arquivonacional/pt-br/canais_atendimento/imprensa/noticias/conheca-o-fundo-sobre-ovnis-do-arquivo-nacional
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Arquivo Nacional holds the Fundo Objeto Voador Nao Identificado (OVNI) under reference code BR DFANBSB ARX, produced by the Comando da Aeronautica do Brasil. A further 2 claims sit on this node on the board and are not reproduced here.

### Its strongest connections

## Project Condign Volume 3 - Potential for Exploitation of UAP-Associated Effects / UAP Work in Other Countries

Project Condign Volume 3 - Potential for Exploitation of UAP-Associated Effects / UAP Work in Other Countries is a document on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 2000 to 2006-05-15; axes C1 A2 D3; 7 connections; in the research band.

In one line: UK SECRET UK EYES ONLY volume; the international-liaison chapter.

### What it is

Volume 3 of the UK Ministry of Defence Defence Intelligence Staff study "Unidentified Aerial Phenomena in the UK Air Defence Region", issued as Scientific & Technical Memorandum 55/2/00 by DI55 within DI(ST) from the Old War Office Building, Whitehall, and titled on its own contents page "MISCELLANEOUS RELATED STUDIES". Its preface is dated February 2000 and states plainly: "This is the third volume of a three-part report on the information held in DI55 on the subject of UAP." Every page is header- and footer-stamped UK SECRET / UK EYES ONLY, and the volume was released to the public on 15 May 2006 under the Freedom of Information Act 2000 with redactions under sections 26, 27 and 40.

### Why it is implicated

Volume 3 is the volume where a NATO ally's defence intelligence staff wrote down, in a classified assessment, the two things the United States has spent seventy years insisting do not exist: a chapter titled "POTENTIAL FOR EXPLOITATION OF UAP-ASSOCIATED EFFECTS" and a chapter titled "UAP WORK IN OTHER COUNTRIES". Chapter 3 opens by stating that one of the reasons for analysing UAP reports at all "was that of identifying any phenomena which could be robustly generated and provide a military advantage" - and the fourteen lines that follow that sentence are blacked out under Section 26 (Defence), as is the entire body of the page listing what DI55 called "three possible related potential applications". Chapter 4 then catalogues what other states were doing, naming Russian institutions, scientists, cosmonauts, laboratories and a research centre by name, and confirming in the Executive Summary that "DI55 does not have access to any other Government's classified reports, as there is no formal intelligence exchange on this topic." That last sentence is the load-bearing one for the board: the UK's own defence intelligence organisation, an ally inside the closest intelligence-sharing arrangement on earth, records that it was locked out of everyone else's UAP files - including, by necessary implication, Washington's. And the countries DI55 named as having staff active on the subject were redacted not under Defence but under Section 27, International Relations.

### The longer account

The Condign report is usually reduced to a single soundbite - the plasma hypothesis - and Volume 3 is the volume that shows why that reduction is a mistake. Volume 1 carried the statistical analysis and the public-facing conclusions. Volume 2 carried the working papers. Volume 3 carried the material the study's author explicitly said "could not be placed in the RESTRICTED Executive Summary". Its own preface concedes the reason it is stamped SECRET is narrow and technical - it "contains performance values of the UKADR radars" - but then adds that the volume is "also a convenient place-holder for several other related findings" arising "out of earlier DI55 interest in any reliable information connected with this topic and focusing upon the potential technologies involved and their possible future military uses." That is an intelligence organisation stating on paper that it had a standing interest in the technology question.

Chapter 3 is the exploitation chapter. Its first paragraph states the purpose without hedging: to identify "any phenomena which could be robustly generated and provide a military advantage." What follows is redacted. Paragraph 2, "Exotic Vehicles", is remarkable for what a defence intelligence analyst was willing to commit to a classified page in 2000. It records that "the possibility of some sort of exotic vehicle, either terrestrial or from space could not be ruled out until all available evidence had been examined", that "the possibility of technologies beyond the scope of human engineering and conception could not initially be ruled out", and - critically - that had extra-terrestrial technology been proven, "then (possibly) recommendations could be made as to the way ahead." It goes further, sketching what would be required: propulsion "by the use of anti-matter, gravitational or torsion fields or of particle phenomena and fields or waves which are not currently known in science or technology", and reported performance that "appears to imply the negation of inertia". The chapter's stated conclusion is negative - "there is no indication that craft of extra-terrestrial origin exist" - but the surrounding architecture is that of a reverse-engineering feasibility study, and the section headed POTENTIAL APPLICATIONS, which announces "three possible related potential applications", has its entire page body struck out under Section 26 and remains struck out today.

Chapter 4 is the foreign-programmes chapter, and it is where the board's Soviet thread anchors. Paragraph 1 states: "It is clear that the Former Soviet Union has taken the study of UAPs seriously and that this may well be related to a programme of understanding and exploiting certain UAP-related natural phenomena, such as plasmas. A 'UFO' Commission was created under the USSR Union of Scientific and Engineering Societies in 1989, together in 1990 with an Inter-Industry Ufology Research Centre (SOYUZUFOSENTR) to co-ordinate records. This Centre had the rights of an Institute under the USSR Academy of Sciences, and was headed by V.G. AZAHAZHA (or AZHAZHA) an eminent researcher and former naval submariner." The paragraph goes on to record a ten-week school whose terms of reference were to study "ufology as a science of unidentified flying objects and their inter-relations with the universe and, in particular, with the biotechnosphere of the earth".

The chapter keeps naming things. Paragraph 2 identifies V.V. Alexandrov and cosmonaut Y.N. Glazov of the Cosmonaut Training Centre and the Scientific Production Association (Geophysics), and a supporting laboratory at Akhtubisk on the Volga. Paragraph 3, "Plasma Research", records that two Russian scientific papers summarised at Annex A appear to show "20-30 scientists have pursued research for the prevention of air target detection" - plasma stealth - and everything after that sentence is redacted under Section 26. Paragraph 5, "Near Field Effects", reports 1991 Russian soil sampling at alleged landing sites where "in all cases a local field developed over a period of three hours" and "impairment of human senses were reportedly observed in every person studied who had been 'in the field'". The DI55 analyst's own bracketed comment is the most telling line in the volume: "It is possible, and confirmed from some other data that russian scientists may have discovered some new field types."

The Executive Summary makes the intelligence posture explicit. Under "Foreign Military Interest", paragraph 9 records that UAP "are by no means confined to UK airspace", that "Other NATO nations, China, Russia and elsewhere frequently report the most extreme and puzzling incidents in the open press", and that DI55 had no access to any other government's classified reports. The identities of the countries known "to have at least one member of staff active in this area" are withheld under Section 27 - the international-relations exemption, not the defence one. Paragraph 10 adds that "Several governments have also been sufficiently concerned to set up Commissions and Institutes to examine the phenomena." The bullets on the following page state that Russian, Former Soviet Republic and Chinese authorities "have made a co-ordinated effort to understand the UAP topic", that "Several aircraft have been destroyed and at least four pilots have been killed 'chasing UFOs'", and that "Russian investigators have measured (or at least detected) 'fields', which are reported to have caused human effects when they are located close to the phenomena."

The redaction fight that followed is itself part of the record. The MoD's internal-review correspondence, held at The National Archives as DEFE 24/2090/1 and digitised, shows Clarke and Anthony challenging specific Volume 3 withholdings paragraph by paragraph, including Chapter 4 page 3 paragraph 12, where the reviewer notes that "information regarding DIS, the CIA desk officer and DI51 must remain redacted" while conceding that the Society for Scientific Exploration statement in the same sentence was already public. A CIA desk officer appears in the UK's classified UAP chapter on foreign programmes, and his existence is still redacted. That is the shape of the concealment, drawn from the inside.

### What it has denied

The MoD framed the whole exercise defensively: its own release note says the data "was studied principally to ascertain whether there is any evidence of a threat to the UK, and secondly, should the opportunity arise, to identify any potential military technologies of interest" - burying the exploitation objective in a subordinate clause. Volume 3 Chapter 3 paragraph 7 supplies the quotable disclaimer, "there is no indication that craft of extra-terrestrial origin exist", while the paragraphs enumerating the exploitation applications on the same and following pages remain blacked out under Section 26 two decades later.

Roles the board assigns it: research, concealment, disclosure, oversight.

### Open questions on this node

- What are the "three possible related potential applications" identified in Chapter 3 paragraph 8? The entire page body and the following page are still withheld under Section 26.
- Which countries are named in Executive Summary paragraph 9 as having staff active on UAP? Withheld under Section 27, meaning disclosure was judged to damage international relations rather than defence.
- Who is the CIA desk officer referenced in Volume 3 Chapter 4 page 3 paragraph 12, and what was DI51's role? Both remain redacted after internal review.
- The released 48-page Volume 3 file examined here ends mid-Chapter 4; the contents page lists further sections on Former Soviet Union Aircraft Incidents, an FSU/Russian Experimental Vehicle, and OTHER NATIONAL ACTIVITY covering CHINA, SPAIN and USA & CANADA. The USA & Canada section was not reached in this pass and its contents are unverified here.
- What are the two Russian scientific papers summarised at Volume 3 Annex A, and are they identifiable in the open literature?
- The Volume 3 preface is dated February 2000 while the report as a whole is commonly described as completed in December 2000; the discrepancy between the volume's own date stamp and the cited completion date is unresolved.
- Condign's stated conclusion is that no extra-terrestrial craft exist, yet Chapter 3 is structured as a feasibility study for exploiting effects the same chapter says cannot be replicated. Whether that structure reflects a genuine analytic dead end or a compartment the author could not write into an at-most-SECRET volume is not settled by the released text.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 175 of 1057, score 46). 7 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C1/A2/D3.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** Chapter 3 paragraph 2 states that "the possibility of some sort of exotic vehicle, either terrestrial or from space could not be ruled out until all available evidence had been examined", that "the possibility of technologies beyond the scope of human engineering and conception could not initially be ruled out", and that if extra-terrestrial technology were proven "then (possibly) recommendations could be made as to the way ahead"; paragraph 3 lists candidate technologies including "propulsion by the use of anti-matter, gravitational or torsion fields", and paragraph 2 states the reported performance "appears to imply the negation of inertia". Source: Condign Volume 3, Chapter 3, paragraphs 2-3, released PDF p.35. https://ufotransparency.com/files/intl-uk-project-condign-volume-3-project-condign-vol3-pp1-48
- **Tier 1 documented.** Chapter 3's POTENTIAL APPLICATIONS section, paragraph 8, states "the radar detection aspects have shown that there are three possible related potential applications" and the entire body of that page is withheld under Section 26; the following page (released PDF p.38) is withheld in full under Section 26 as well. Source: Condign Volume 3, Chapter 3, paragraph 8, released PDF pp.37-38. https://ufotransparency.com/files/intl-uk-project-condign-volume-3-project-condign-vol3-pp1-48
- **Tier 1 documented.** Chapter 4 paragraph 2 names V.V. ALEXANDROV (Senior Project Engineer) and Y.N. GLAZOV (astronaut, Hero of the Soviet Union, and Deputy Head of the Cosmonaut Training Centre in charge of science and testing) of the Test Centre (Tsenatr Podgotovki Kosmonatov) at the Scientific Production Association (Geophysics), and places a supporting laboratory at AKHTUBISK on the Volga. Source: Condign Volume 3, Chapter 4, paragraph 2, released PDF p.39. https://ufotransparency.com/files/intl-uk-project-condign-volume-3-project-condign-vol3-pp1-48
- **Tier 1 documented.** Chapter 4 paragraph 3 states that Annex A summarises "two russian scientific papers where, it appears, 20-30 scientists have pursued research for the prevention of air target detection", and the remainder of the paragraph is withheld under FOIA Section 26 (Defence). Source: Condign Volume 3, Chapter 4, paragraph 3, released PDF p.39. https://ufotransparency.com/files/intl-uk-project-condign-volume-3-project-condign-vol3-pp1-48
- **Tier 1 documented, allegation shaped wording as stored.** Chapter 4 paragraph 5 ("Near Field Effects") records 1991 Russian soil sampling at alleged landing sites where "In all cases a local field developed over a period of three hours" and "Impairment of human senses were reportedly observed in every person studied who had been 'in the field'"; the DI55 analyst's bracketed comment on the following page states "It is possible, and confirmed from some other data that russian scientists may have discovered some new field types." Source: Condign Volume 3, Chapter 4, paragraphs 5-6, released PDF pp.39-40. https://ufotransparency.com/files/intl-uk-project-condign-volume-3-project-condign-vol3-pp1-48

- **Tier 1 documented.** Volume 3, Chapter 4, para 8 quotes Sokolov on Soviet aircraft incidents: 'We had 40 cases where pilots encountered UFOs. Initially they were commanded to chase, then shoot. ### Its strongest connections

- Project Condign Volume 3 - Potential for Exploitation of UAP-Associated Effects / UAP Work in Other Countries comes in from **Unidentified Aerial Phenomena in the UK Air Defence Region (DIS Scientific & Technical Memorandum 55/2/00)** (document, board tier A), a custody link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: Volume 3 holds the material excluded from the RESTRICTED summary. Volume 3's own opening states that it 'primarily deals with sensitive matters associated with the UAP study which could not be placed in the RESTRICTED Executive Summary'. - Project Condign Volume 3 - Potential for Exploitation of UAP-Associated Effects / UAP Work in Other Countries runs out to **SETKA-AN / SETKA-MO (later Galaktika, then Gorizont) - Soviet state anomalous phenomena programme** (program, board tier B), a investigative link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: assessed the Soviet programme as exploitation-driven. Condign Volume 3 chapter 4 states that Soviet study of UAP 'may well be related to a programme of understanding and exploiting certain UAP-related natural phenomena, such as plasmas'. Sources: Condign Volume 3, https://archive.org/download/condign-vol-2-1-258/Condign_Vol_3_1-48_djvu.txt, dated 2006-05-15.
- Project Condign Volume 3 - Potential for Exploitation of UAP-Associated Effects / UAP Work in Other Countries comes in from **Belgian Air Force F-16 intercept and the 1989-90 Belgian wave** (event, board tier B), a research link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: Belgian imagery inside the Condign study, in Volume 2 rather than Volume 3. Volume 2's figure list carries a video frame captioned Belgium 31 March 1990 and a comparative shape figure for the UK, Russia and Belgium. Volume 3 chapter 4's country sections are the Former Soviet Union, China, Spain, and the United States and Canada, and do not cover Belgium. Sources: Project Condign Volume 2 OCR text, https://archive.org/download/condign-vol-2-1-258/Condign_Vol_2_1-258_djvu.txt, dated 2000-01-01.
- Project Condign Volume 3 - Potential for Exploitation of UAP-Associated Effects / UAP Work in Other Countries runs out to **United Kingdom Ministry of Defence** (institution, board tier B), a denial link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: records a flat denial of any US/Canada exchange, then redacts four lines. Volume 3 para 12: 'There is no intelligence exchange or access or collaboration of any sort on the topic of UFOs' - followed by approximately four redacted lines. Sources: Condign Volume 3, https://archive.org/download/condign-vol-2-1-258/Condign_Vol_3_1-48_djvu.txt, dated 2006-05-15.
- Project Condign Volume 3 - Potential for Exploitation of UAP-Associated Effects / UAP Work in Other Countries runs out to **SOYUZUFOTSENTR - USSR Inter-Industry Ufology Research Centre** (institution, board tier C), a investigative link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: Condign's Former Soviet Union chapter names, dates and assesses SOYUZUFOTSENTR. This is the reason the node exists at all. SOYUZUFOTSENTR reaches this board not through Russian ufology literature but through a UK SECRET UK EYES ONLY Defence Intelligence Staff assessment, which places the centre inside a paragraph opening 'It[s] clear that the Former Soviet Union has taken the study of UAPs seriously and th[a]t this may well be related to a programme of understanding and exploiting certain UAP-related natural phenomena, such as plasmas.' The same chapter runs on to Popovich's 1990 All Union Ufology Association at the Air Force Academy, General of Aviation Igor Maltsev's 1990 release of pilot reports, Vsevolod Troitsky of the Russian Academy of Sciences chairing a Commission for the Study of Anomalous Propagation and Phenomena, and Sokolov's account of Soviet aircraft incidents. Condign treats the public centre and the state effort as one national picture. Verified line by line against the released OCR. Sources: Project Condign Volume 3, Chapter 4, paras 1-8 - released MoD copy, OCR text, https://archive.org/download/condign-vol-2-1-258/Condign_Vol_3_1-48_djvu.txt, dated 2006-05-15.
- Project Condign Volume 3 - Potential for Exploitation of UAP-Associated Effects / UAP Work in Other Countries runs out to **Vladimir Georgievich Azhazha** (person, board tier C), a investigative link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: names Azhazha as head of SOYUZUFOTSENTR. Condign identifies 'V.G. AZAHAZHA (or AZHAZHA) an eminent researcher and former naval submariner' as head of the 1990 inter-industry ufology research centre. Sources: Condign Volume 3, https://archive.org/download/condign-vol-2-1-258/Condign_Vol_3_1-48_djvu.txt, dated 2006-05-15.

## US Patent 10,144,532 - Craft using an inertial mass reduction device

US Patent 10,144,532 - Craft using an inertial mass reduction device is a document on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2016-04-28 to 2023-01-09; axes C2 A1 D0; 6 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Application 15/141270, filed 28 April 2016, granted 4 December 2018; assignee the Secretary of the Navy.

### What it is

United States Patent 10,144,532, 'Craft using an inertial mass reduction device', application 15/141,270, filed 28 April 2016 from the Naval Air Warfare Center Aircraft Division and granted 4 December 2018, assigned to 'The United States of America as represented by the Secretary of the Navy' under Navy docket PAX 205. The examiner was Philip J. Bonzell, supervised by Timothy Collins; the application moved from Art Unit 3644 to Art Unit 3642 mid-prosecution without changing examiner. The patent expired on 9 January 2023 for non-payment of the first maintenance fee under 37 CFR 1.362.

### Why it is implicated

It is the control case for the patents tab. A Navy-assigned propulsion filing that did issue is what makes an absence elsewhere in the patent record a legal artefact of the Invention Secrecy Act rather than evidence that nothing exists. It is also the filing that produced the public controversy, and the file wrapper contradicts the story most often told about it. The secondary accounts say the application was rejected and then granted after the Naval Aviation Enterprise's chief technology officer asserted operability and invoked Chinese development of comparable technology. The wrapper shows the examiner refusing that argument in writing, and shows the grant arriving through a formal appeal rather than a letter.

### The longer account

The prosecution record is unusually explicit for a document of this kind, and it is worth following in order. Non-final rejection 28 November 2017. A telephonic examiner interview on 17 January 2018 at which, in the record's words, 'no agreement was reached'. Final rejection 30 March 2018. A response after final on 24 May 2018 carrying a declaration under 37 C.F.R. 1.132 signed by Dr James Sheehy on 11 May 2018. Notice of Appeal 21 June 2018, on form PTO/AIA/33, an 800 dollar fee against deposit account 600508. Advisory Action 11 July 2018 refusing to allow. Appeal Brief 21 August 2018, re-filing the same 11 May declaration in its Evidence Appendix rather than a second one. Reasons for Allowance 24 October 2018, Notice of Allowance 31 October, grant 4 December. The examiner allowed the application before the Patent Trial and Appeal Board ever ruled.

The examiner quantified his objection rather than gesturing at it. Polarising a quantum vacuum as claimed 'would take 10^9 teslas and 10^18V/m', which 'are not feasible with current technology', and: 'The largest magnetic field ever created is 10^3 teslas and a neutron star is 10^6 teslas so how are you using a microwave emitter that produces a magnetic field that is three orders of magnitude greater than a neutron star?' The same passage repeats verbatim in the final rejection. He also rejected the physical premise in terms, writing that there is no such thing as a repulsive electromagnetic energy field and that electromagnetism can be repulsive in certain circumstances but there is no blanket field that repels everything.

On the argument the secondary accounts credit with winning the patent, the wrapper is unambiguous: 'With respect to third argument that China is already investing significantly in this area, this has nothing to do with what is patentable. If anyone else filed this Application they would receive the same rejection.' Peer review fared no better. On the AIAA and SAE presentations the examiner wrote that presentation and committee review 'does not equate to the device being patentable', and that 'Presenting math at a conference doesn't prove anything physical.'

What was eventually granted is narrow. Claim 1 is the only independent claim and recites an inner resonant cavity wall, an outer resonant cavity wall and microwave emitters creating high frequency electromagnetic waves that vibrate the outer wall in an accelerated mode and create a local polarized vacuum outside it. There are four claims in total. Five years later the Navy stopped paying for it.

### What it has denied

The denial on this node runs in the unusual direction. The Navy did not deny anything about this filing; a federal examiner denied, on the record and in quantified terms, that the claimed effect is physically achievable, and did so twice before the applicant appealed. That is the only adjudicated technical opinion this board holds on any of the Pais filings, and it is negative. Two further things follow, and both matter for how the node should be read. The allowance that eventually issued came from the same examiner rather than from the Patent Trial and Appeal Board, so no appellate body has ever endorsed the disclosure. And the board's own edge from this patent to the five observables is graded Tier 2 and describes what the briefing claims the device would deliver, not a capability anyone has demonstrated.

Roles the board assigns it: research, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether the Navy's failure to pay the first maintenance fee was a decision or an oversight is not established. Nothing on this board explains the expiry.
- Whether the Notice of Appeal was ever docketed at the Patent Trial and Appeal Board is not established. The examiner allowed the application first, so no PTAB decision exists to read.
- The node's own description still says the correspondence asserting operability has not been obtained for this board, while its claims cite the Sheehy letter of 15 December 2017 and the declaration of 11 May 2018 as transcribed under research/patents/wrapper/. The description is behind the claims and should be reconciled.
- No test data, experimental result or hardware associated with the device has been located. The board holds the paper record of the filing and nothing else.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 87 of 1057, score 66). 6 connections, 4 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C2/A1/D0.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** Claim 1 is the only independent claim and recites an inner resonant cavity wall, an outer resonant cavity wall and microwave emitters; the emitters create high frequency electromagnetic waves throughout the resonant cavity, causing the outer wall to vibrate in an accelerated mode and create a local polarized vacuum outside it. There are four claims in total. Source: USPTO file wrapper, application 15/141,270 (PAX 205), Appeal Brief under 37 C.F.R. 41.37 faxed 20 August 2018; notes at research/patents/wrapper/appeal-brief-2018-08-20.txt
- **Tier 1 documented.** Peer review was argued and expressly refused. On AIAA 2017-5343 and SAE 2017-01-2040 the examiner wrote that 'just because a concept was presented at an AIAA forum and that a SAE committee board, peer reviewed the concept does not equate to the device being patentable', and on the mathematics: 'Presenting math at a conference doesn't prove anything physical. It like saying that you can math out tachyons by playing with relativistic equations but that doesn't "prove" that they exist nor can they then be used to create a physical object that can be patented.' Source: USPTO file wrapper, application 15/141,270, Final Rejection mailed 30 March 2018 (Paper No. 20180327), examiner Philip J. Bonzell; transcribed at research/patents/wrapper/final-rejection-2018-03-30.txt

The examiner's section 6 replies to exactly three arguments, in the order the letter makes them: that lasers evolved beyond what was first possible, that Pais 'is funded and working on experiments', and that China is investing. Those are paragraphs 1, 2-4 and 5 of the Sheehy letter. No separate January 2018 declaration appears anywhere in the wrapper. There is no such thing as a "repulsive EM energy field". Electromagnetism can be repulsive in certain circumstances, but there is no blanket field that repels everything.' # 2017-2026: Post-disclosure

## AARO FY2025 Consolidated Annual Report on UAP

AARO FY2025 Consolidated Annual Report on UAP is a document on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2026; axes C1 A3 D3; 18 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Released 21 July 2026, months after the statutory cycle.

### What it is

The Fiscal Year 2025 Consolidated Annual Report on Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena is the fourteen-page statutory report submitted by the All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office under 50 U.S.C. § 3373(k), covering UAP events from 2 June 2024 to 30 May 2025, bearing the clearance number 26-P-0880 and issued under the letterhead "Department of War." It was publicly released on 21 July 2026, months after the annual reporting cycle Congress contemplated. It records 319 new reports, 370 total resolutions, and no unexplained residue attributed to advanced foreign or non-human capability.

### Why it is implicated

This is the current, dated instrument of official denial, and it is therefore the most useful single artifact on the board. Its flat assertion, "To date, no evidence suggests that a USG or private entity has ever captured or exploited UAP-derived materials", is immediately followed by the statement that AARO "is currently developing a formalized process for handling UAP-derived materials by an appropriate line organization, should such materials ever come into the possession of the USG," a process AARO says draws on established procedures governing the recovery of foreign materiel. An office that has found nothing is building a custody chain for it. The report also claims an access authority that collides directly with standing DoD regulation: AARO states it is authorized to receive UAP information "regardless of any restrictive access controls, special access controls, or compartmented access programs," while DoDD 5205.07 §3.2.g simultaneously forbids any DoD contractor employee from providing SAP material to Congress without SAPCO approval and 32 CFR 117.6 removes DCSA visibility from carved-out programs entirely. Claiming access is not the same as exercising it. And the report is internally inconsistent on its headline finding: §IV.C states AARO resolved 44 events as satellite flaring during the reporting period while §VI states the new capability "enabled analysts to resolve 238 reported UAP as satellite flaring."

### The longer account

The arithmetic is the place to start, because the report's own numbers do not reconcile cleanly and the discrepancy runs through its most-promoted achievement. AARO received 319 reports between 2 June 2024 and 30 May 2025: 284 events inside the window and 35 from 2010 to 2024 not previously reported. Of the 319, AARO resolved 114 and additionally resolved 256 cases from prior periods, for 370 resolutions in the reporting period. Case holdings totaled 1,870 as of 30 May 2025. By domain, 274 were airborne, 44 space, one maritime. Of the 319, 191 were transferred to the "active archive", the category for cases with insufficient data to assess whether performance characteristics are consistent with natural phenomena or exceed the known state of the art. Nine reports were identified as meriting further analysis by intelligence community and science-and-technology partners. Two case files were merged.

Now the flaring numbers. Section III.A states that all 44 space-domain reports were assessed as satellite flaring, of which 42 came from FAA civilian pilot reports and two from ground-based U.S. Space Command sensors, and notes, pointedly, that "none of AARO's space-domain case assessments originated from space-based sensors." Section IV.C states: "AARO resolved 44 UAP-related events as attributable to satellite flaring during the reporting period." Section VI states: "This capability, consisting of advanced three-dimensional modeling and simulation applications, enabled analysts to resolve 238 reported UAP as satellite flaring." The executive summary uses 238. Gillibrand's press release uses 238. The 238 figure only coheres if it spans the 256 previously reported cases as well as the current-period 44, which the report never states and which would mean the office's flagship analytic advance was applied overwhelmingly to a backlog rather than to new reporting. A downstream analyst should treat the 238/44 gap as an unresolved presentation problem in a fourteen-page document.

The substantive denials come in Section IV.G and Section IV.H. On materials: "To date, no evidence suggests that a USG or private entity has ever captured or exploited UAP-derived materials. AARO is currently developing a formalized process for handling UAP-derived materials by an appropriate line organization, should such materials ever come into the possession of the USG. AARO's development process draws on established USG capabilities and operating procedures governing the recovery of foreign materiel." On health: "To date, AARO has not received a UAP report in which a reporter described sustaining adverse health effects associated with their experience." Both are negative claims about AARO's own holdings, not about the world. The report never asserts that no such program or materials exist; it asserts that no evidence of them has reached this office.

The access claim in Section V.C is the report's strongest institutional assertion and its most testable: "AARO is authorized by law to receive all UAP-related information, including any classified national security information involving military or intelligence related activities, at any level of classification, regardless of any restrictive access controls, special access controls, or compartmented access programs. Generally, no restrictions, including non-disclosure agreements, prevent AARO from receiving UAP-related information, regardless of the organizational affiliation of the original classification authority within the DoW, the IC, or any other USG department or agency." Note the hedge: "Generally." Note also what the sentence does not say, it describes a right to receive, not an exercised capability to compel, and it does not state that AARO has audited any carved-out special access program. Against that, 262 individuals used the secure reporting mechanism in the period; AARO determined 255 were out of scope and identified seven for follow-on interview. A 97 percent out-of-scope rate is either an accurate measure of noise or a measure of a scoping filter, and the report gives no criteria by which to tell.

Two passages cut against the report's own framing. Section IV.E: "AARO received and analyzed several reports describing UAP in or near national security sites or critical infrastructure. Some narrative reports were suggestive of phenomena whose performance characteristics exceed the known state-of-the-art within a given domain. No technical data accompanied these reports. If validated, the attendant phenomena underlying these reports may represent a currently unmitigated threat vector." And Section III.A, the sole maritime case: a report from U.S. Navy assets operating off the coast of Virginia describing "approximately 100 airborne UAP and two, likely uncrewed, surface systems," which AARO says it is actively investigating. An office reporting no flight-safety concerns and no anomalous residue is simultaneously reporting an unmitigated threat vector and a hundred-object maritime event under investigation.

Section IV.F is the nuclear line. AARO received 50 reports from the Administrator for Nuclear Security and the Chairman of the Nuclear Regulatory Commission regarding unmanned aerial system incidents near U.S. nuclear infrastructure, weapons and launch sites, a 177.8 percent increase over the 18 in the FY2024 report. "None of these incidents were reported as UAP." Forty-seven of the 50 occurred between 1700 and 0500 local; five persisted at least an hour, the longest about three hours; 33 involved a single system, 17 involved between two and seven. The categorical exclusion of all 50 from UAP status is a classification decision made by the reporting agencies, not an analytic finding by AARO, and the report does not say who made it.

On process: the report was released 21 July 2026 under a statute requiring submission "not later than 180 days after December 23, 2022, and annually thereafter for four years." The FY2024 report was published in November 2024 and documented 757 reports against FY2025's 319. Senator Kirsten Gillibrand, who created AARO in the FY2022 NDAA and secured the FY2024 NDAA declassification language, issued a same-day statement calling the report "another crucial step forward" and AARO "the most comprehensive government effort on UAPs to date," and framed the arithmetic as 319 received, 114 explained, "leaving 205 unresolved." The report's own foreign-engagement section names exactly one activity for the year: AARO's deputy director presented a mission briefing to the Defense Geospatial Intelligence conference in London.

### What it has denied

The report issues two categorical denials and one implicit one. On materials: "To date, no evidence suggests that a USG or private entity has ever captured or exploited UAP-derived materials." On health: "To date, AARO has not received a UAP report in which a reporter described sustaining adverse health effects." On adversary technology: "None of the cases AARO resolved indicate advanced foreign adversarial capabilities or breakthrough technologies in any domain." Each is scoped to what has reached AARO rather than to what exists, and each is undercut within the same document by the unmitigated-threat-vector language, the hundred-object Virginia maritime case, and the fact that AARO is building a materials custody process for materials it says do not exist.

Roles the board assigns it: disclosure, concealment, oversight, research.

### Open questions on this node

- Why does §IV.C state 44 satellite-flaring resolutions for the reporting period while §VI and the Executive Summary state 238? The report never reconciles the figures or states which case population the 238 spans.
- Who decided that all 50 nuclear-adjacent UAS incidents were "not reported as UAP", the reporting agencies or AARO, and against what criteria?
- What are the scoping criteria by which 255 of 262 first-hand claimants were ruled out of scope? None are published.
- AARO says it is building a formalized process for handling UAP-derived materials "by an appropriate line organization." Which organization, under what authority, and with what facility?
- AARO claims access regardless of special access controls. Has AARO ever invoked that authority against a specific carved-out SAP, and if so, was access granted or refused?
- The February 2025 Joint Staff UAP GENADMIN remains classified. What does it direct, and does it reach contractor personnel?
- The Virginia maritime case describes approximately 100 airborne UAP and two likely uncrewed surface systems. What sensors recorded it, and what is the current status of the investigation?
- Two reports described electronic or avionic interference near operational aircraft; AARO says it has "not yet rendered a determination." What is the determination timeline, and which airframes were involved?
- The report was released roughly eight months later than the prior year's. What accounts for the delay, and was the report submitted to Congress on time even if released late?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 17 of 1057, score 110). 19 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 6 official denials recorded against it. Axes C1/A3/D3.

## Unidentified Aerial Phenomena Task Force (UAPTF)

Unidentified Aerial Phenomena Task Force (UAPTF) is a program on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2020 to 2022; axes C2 A3 D2; 16 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Navy/ONI task force 2020-2022.

### What it is

The Unidentified Aerial Phenomena Task Force (UAPTF) was a Department of Defense body approved by Deputy Secretary of Defense David L. Norquist on 4 August 2020 and announced publicly on 14 August 2020, led by the Department of the Navy under the cognizance of the Office of the Under Secretary of Defense for Intelligence and Security. Its stated mission was to detect, analyze and catalog UAP that could potentially pose a threat to U.S. national security. It produced the dataset behind the June 2021 ODNI preliminary assessment and was superseded in November 2021 by AOIMSG and in 2022 by AARO.

### Why it is implicated

The UAPTF is where the modern disclosure fight acquired its documents and its witnesses. David Grusch served as the National Reconnaissance Office representative to the UAPTF, and it was from inside that role that he says he was briefed on, and then denied access to, a multi-decade crash retrieval and reverse-engineering program. That is the specific institutional pathway by which a legacy program's existence leaked into a body that Congress could subpoena. The task force also demonstrates the concealment mechanism in miniature: a Navy-led, OUSD(I&S)-supervised body with an unclassified public face and a classified annex, stood up only after decades of the same phenomena being handled through channels Congress was not shown. Within fifteen months of its public announcement it was dissolved and its functions moved twice, each time into a differently named organization with different reporting lines. Reorganization is a documented concealment technique on this board, and the UAPTF-to-AOIMSG-to-AARO chain is the cleanest recent example of it.

### The longer account

The establishment is on the record and unambiguous. The Navy's own press release of 14 August 2020 states: "On Aug. 4, 2020, Deputy Secretary of Defense David L. Norquist approved the establishment of an Unidentified Aerial Phenomena (UAP) Task Force (UAPTF). The Department of the Navy, under the cognizance of the Office of the Under Secretary of Defense for Intelligence and Security, will lead the UAPTF." The mission statement is "to detect, analyze and catalog UAPs that could potentially pose a threat to U.S. national security." Note what is absent: any reference to materials, recoveries, or prior programs. The public charter is a sensor-and-safety charter.

What the UAPTF actually produced was the dataset in the ODNI preliminary assessment delivered 25 June 2021, prepared in response to a reporting provision attached to the Intelligence Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2021. That nine-page unclassified assessment, with a classified annex, covers 144 reports from U.S. government sources between November 2004 and March 2021, states that most reports came from Navy and other military aviators, and identifies a small number of cases where UAP "appeared to remain stationary in winds aloft, move against the wind, maneuver abruptly, or move at considerable speed, without discernable means of propulsion." The assessment explicitly declines to attribute the phenomena and cites limited, inconsistent reporting as the principal analytic constraint. It is the government's own written acknowledgment that a body of unexplained aerospace observations exists inside its own sensor record.

The personnel question is where the task force becomes load-bearing for the program argument. David Grusch, an Air Force veteran and intelligence officer, served as the NRO representative to the UAPTF from 2019 to 2021 and then as his agency's co-lead for UAP and trans-medium object analysis at the National Geospatial-Intelligence Agency from 2021 to 2023. In 2022 he filed a complaint with the Intelligence Community Inspector General that was found credible and urgent for reporting to the congressional intelligence committees. On 26 July 2023, under oath before the House Oversight Subcommittee on National Security, the Border, and Foreign Affairs, Grusch testified that in the course of his official duties he was informed of a multi-decade crash retrieval and reverse-engineering program to which he was denied access, and that recoveries included non-human biologics. Whatever one concludes about the substance, the provenance is institutional: the claim entered the public record through a person whose job was to represent a national intelligence agency to a DoD task force.

The dissolution is as instructive as the founding. On 23 November 2021 Deputy Secretary of Defense Kathleen Hicks, in coordination with the Director of National Intelligence, directed the Under Secretary of Defense for Intelligence and Security to establish the Airborne Object Identification and Management Synchronization Group as the successor to the Navy's UAPTF, with oversight from an Airborne Object Identification and Management Executive Council drawn from DoD and the intelligence community. Ronald Moultrie was the USD(I&S) of record. AOIMSG itself lasted months before being reconstituted in July 2022 as the All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office. Three names, three charters, three sets of reporting relationships, in under two years, for the same subject matter. Each transition reset what Congress had been briefed on and who owed the briefing.

The naming precision matters here in the same way it matters for AATIP. The UAPTF was a Navy-led DoD task force with an OUSD(I&S) reporting chain. It was not a special access program, it did not have acquisition authority, and nothing in its public charter gives it custody of anything. That is precisely Grusch's point: he says the compartmented activity he learned about was adjacent to, and walled off from, the body Congress had been told was handling the subject. The UAPTF is therefore best read on this board not as the program but as the sensor that detected the program from inside the building.

Who matters by name: Norquist (approving authority), Hicks (dissolving authority), Moultrie (USD(I&S)), Grusch (NRO representative), and the ODNI and Navy officials who signed out the June 2021 assessment. What the record shows is a legitimate, publicly chartered analytic body producing a public product that concedes unexplained observations. What the record does not show is any UAPTF authority over, or access to, materials, and the absence of that authority in a body specifically created to be the government's answer on UAP is the anomaly worth pulling on.

### What it has denied

DoD has never denied the UAPTF existed; it published its own establishment. The denial operates one level down, at AARO, which states in its 2024 Historical Record Report Volume 1 that it has found no evidence of any U.S. reverse-engineering program and that interviewees, including those with UAPTF-adjacent access, 'likely' misinterpreted sensitive but conventional programs because 'none of the interviewees had firsthand knowledge.' The denial is an assertion about the mental states of named witnesses rather than a document production.

Roles the board assigns it: oversight, concealment, research, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- The 4 August 2020 Norquist approval memorandum itself has not been released in full. What does the charter say beyond the press release summary?
- Who led the UAPTF day to day, and what was the size and composition of its staff? DoD has never published a roster.
- What is in the classified annex to the 25 June 2021 ODNI assessment, and has it been provided to the House Oversight Committee?
- Was there an ONI-run UAP task force predating the 4 August 2020 formalization, and if so under what authority did it operate?
- Grusch says he was briefed on a program while serving as NRO representative to the UAPTF. Who briefed him, and does the UAPTF hold a record of that briefing?
- What happened to UAPTF holdings during the two reorganizations, and were any records destroyed or reclassified in transit?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 74 of 1057, score 69). 19 connections, 6 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C2/A3/D2.

## The Galileo Project

The Galileo Project is a program on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 2021; axes C1 A0 D3; 9 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Harvard-based unclassified UAP and interstellar-object instrumentation programme, founded July 2021.

### What it is

The Galileo Project for the Systematic Scientific Search for Evidence of Extraterrestrial Technological Artifacts is a Harvard-hosted, cross-institutional research programme launched in July 2021 and co-founded by Avi Loeb, the Frank B. Baird Jr. Professor of Science at Harvard, and Frank Laukien, chairman, president and CEO of the scientific instrument manufacturer Bruker Corporation. It operates three branches: the study of interstellar objects near Earth, the search for unidentified aerial phenomena using multimodal ground-based observatories, and the study of interstellar meteors. It is funded entirely by private donation.

### Why it is implicated

Galileo is the control experiment for this entire board. Every version of the legacy-program hypothesis and every official denial of it reduce to claims about physical evidence held under classification. The only structural way to break that is an independent, uncleared, publishing instrument capable of producing calibrated data on the same phenomena - data that is not subject to prepublication classification review because it was never collected on government money or on government sensors. Galileo is the first serious institutional attempt to build one, and its funding structure is the mechanism: $1.755 million raised in the first two weeks from named private donors, which means its outputs go to arXiv rather than to a DOPSR queue. Its director also co-authored a March 2023 draft paper with Sean Kirkpatrick, the sitting Director of the All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office - the only documented instance of a joint technical product between the Pentagon's UAP office and an independent academic programme. And its IM1 interstellar meteor work is the most instructive public failure mode on the board: a physical-recovery campaign that produced material, published it, and could not establish a chain from the material to the event, generating a citation war that maps precisely onto the epistemics of the whole subject.

### The longer account

Loeb's framing paper, "Overview of the Galileo Project," was posted to arXiv on 2 August 2022 as arXiv:2209.02479 and published in a special issue of the Journal of Astronomical Instrumentation in 2023. Its abstract describes the project as "the first systematic scientific research program in search for potential astro-archaeological artifacts or remnants of extraterrestrial technological civilizations (ETCs) or potentially active equipment near Earth," and characterises the approach as taking a path not taken and conceivably picking low-hanging fruit. That is a statement about method, and the method is the point: Galileo's premise is that the subject has been starved of instrumented, calibrated, continuous data, not of anecdotes.

The UAP branch built to that premise. Wesley Watters and colleagues published "The Scientific Investigation of Unidentified Aerial Phenomena (UAP) Using Multimodal Ground-Based Observatories" in the Journal of Astronomical Instrumentation, volume 12, article 2340006 (2023), laying out the sensor architecture: synchronised optical, infrared, radio, magnetometric and acoustic channels producing a continuous census of everything above an observation site, so that anomaly is defined by exclusion from a measured baseline rather than by the surprise of an observer. The first instrument came online at Harvard. Commissioning results for the all-sky infrared camera array were posted as arXiv:2411.07956, and a system architecture paper followed as arXiv:2506.00125. Loeb has publicly discussed commissioning data covering roughly half a million objects. No Galileo detection has been shown to be non-human technology, and the project has not claimed otherwise.

The interstellar meteor branch is where the project collided with the discipline. CNEOS 2014-01-08, designated IM1, is a bolide detected over the western Pacific whose inferred velocity implied a hyperbolic, interstellar trajectory. In summer 2023 Galileo mounted an ocean expedition off Papua New Guinea, dragging a magnetic sled across a roughly ten-kilometre region around the fireball localisation across twenty-six runs and recovering approximately 700 sub-millimetre spherules. Loeb and colleagues reported that a subset had a distinctive composition rich in beryllium, lanthanum and uranium - the 'BeLaU' type - which they argued was inconsistent with solar-system materials. The chemical classification work was eventually published in Chemical Geology (2024), with the preprint at arXiv:2401.09882.

The response was immediate and formal, and it is worth cataloguing precisely because it is the model of how a materials claim gets adjudicated in the open. Steve Desch and Alan Jackson published a direct critique, arXiv:2311.07699, arguing among other things that the spherules plot on or extremely near a terrestrial fractionation line and that a body entering at the assumed IM1 velocity would have been essentially vaporised, so that recovered iron spherules cannot be attributed to that event. A separate paper, arXiv:2403.05161, proposed the BeLaU signature as microtektites of terrestrial laterites. Most damaging to the localisation, Fernando and colleagues published "Seismic and acoustic signals from the 2014 'interstellar meteor'" in Geophysical Journal International, volume 238, issue 2 (2024), concluding that the seismic signal used to pin the impact site changed direction over time in a way that matched a road running past the seismometer - a truck, not a bolide. Galileo replied with "Peak-Brightness Localization of the CNEOS 2014-01-08 (IM1) Fireball," arXiv:2403.07696, arguing that the search region was set by the NASA/JPL CNEOS localisation box - 11.112 km on a side, centred at 1.3 degrees south, 147.6 degrees east - and not by the seismic data at all.

Whichever way that dispute resolves, its structure is the finding. A privately funded team recovered candidate material, published composition data, and could not establish provenance linking material to event; independent groups published falsifying analyses within months; the original team responded in the same channel. This is exactly the adjudication process that has never been available for any material alleged to be in government custody, and the contrast is the argument. The Nolan-Vallee 2022 paper faces the same problem from the other direction - a sample with a documented same-night recovery but a forty-four-year custody gap.

The most direct institutional connection on the board is the Loeb-Kirkpatrick paper. Dated 7 March 2023 and titled "Physical Constraints on Unidentified Aerial Phenomena," it derives physical constraints on interpretations of highly manoeuvrable UAP from standard physics and known forms of matter and radiation, and it is co-authored by the serving Director of AARO. The draft circulated publicly, drew criticism from multiple directions, and was never formally issued as an AARO product. It nonetheless establishes that the Pentagon's UAP office and Galileo were in direct technical collaboration during Kirkpatrick's tenure, which bears on how one reads AARO's later categorical statements and on what data, if any, moved in either direction.

For an analyst, Galileo's value is threefold. It produces a public, timestamped, instrumented baseline that any future disclosure has to be consistent with. Its funding graph - Eugene Jhong, Vinny Jain, Teddy Jones, Eric Keto, the Laukien Science Foundation, Joerg Laukien, William A. Linton, Adnan Sen, the Brinson Foundation - identifies the private capital willing to underwrite the question outside the appropriations system. And through Laukien and Bruker it sits one step from the mass-spectrometry instrument industry whose products define what any materials claim, government or otherwise, can actually be tested with.

### What it has denied

Galileo issues no denials; it is the party against which official denial is directed. Its own posture is one of null results honestly reported - no Galileo observation has been shown to be non-human technology, and the project has repeatedly declined to assert probabilities. That posture is itself evidentially useful, because it establishes that the project's later claims, if any, would not be the product of a group predisposed to affirm.

Roles the board assigns it: research, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether Galileo observatory data has ever been requested by, shared with, or offered to AARO under 50 U.S.C. 3373, and whether any such exchange is documented
- What became of the Loeb-Kirkpatrick draft paper - whether it was ever submitted, reviewed, or suppressed, and whether AARO reviewed it as an official product
- Whether the BeLaU spherules can be independently re-analysed by a laboratory with no stake in the outcome, and whether sample material has been distributed
- Whether any Galileo observatory site has been sited near locations with historical UAP incident density, and how site selection was made
- Whether the project has retained legal or classification advice regarding what would happen to its data if a detection were judged to implicate a classified US programme
- The extent of overlap between Galileo's donor network and the private funders of other UAP research vehicles

### Why it holds its tier
