## Sec. 6802, James M. Inhofe NDAA for FY2023 (Pub. L. 117-263) - AARO establishment, Historical Record Report and annual reports

Sec. 6802, James M. Inhofe NDAA for FY2023 (Pub. L. 117-263) - AARO establishment, Historical Record Report and annual reports is a legislation on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates no dates recorded; axes C0 A0 D0; 7 connections; in the research band.

In one line: statute.

### What it is

Section 6802 of the James M. Inhofe National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2023, Public Law 117-263, Division F (the Intelligence Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2023), Title LXVIII, enacted 23 December 2022, 136 Stat. 3586. Section 6802(a) amended 50 U.S.C. 3373 in its entirety, replacing the FY2022 Airborne Object Identification and Management Synchronization Group language with the statutory charter of the All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office. It is the provision that put a congressional demand for the concealment history into permanent federal law.

### Why it is implicated

Section 6802 is the moment Congress wrote its suspicion into the United States Code. The historical record report it mandates at 50 U.S.C. 3373(j) does not ask whether UAP are real; it orders the Director of AARO to compile and itemize 'any program or activity that was protected by restricted access that has not been explicitly and clearly reported to Congress' and 'any efforts to obfuscate, manipulate public opinion, hide, or otherwise provide incorrect unclassified or classified information about unidentified anomalous phenomena or related activities,' from 1 January 1945 forward. Those are not the words of a legislature seeking information about aerial objects. They are the words of a legislature accusing the executive branch of running unreported compartmented programs and conducting a disinformation effort, and then ordering the executive branch to write the indictment itself. Section 6802 also created the material-testing and propulsion-science machinery at 50 U.S.C. 3373(e) and (g), and Section 6803 attached a Comptroller General audit to the historical record report because Congress did not trust the self-investigation it had just commissioned.

### The longer account

Read Section 6802 the way a prosecutor reads a charging document and the picture is unmistakable.

The mechanics first, because the citation matters and is commonly muddled. Public Law 117-263 is the FY2023 NDAA. Division F of that act is the Intelligence Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2023. Section 6802(a) of Division F, Title LXVIII, amended 50 U.S.C. 3373 generally, at 136 Stat. 3586. That is the AARO charter. A separate provision, Section 1673 of Division A, Title XVI, at 136 Stat. 2959, created 50 U.S.C. 3373b, the authorized-disclosure mechanism, and it is Section 1673 rather than Section 6802 that carries the phrase 'reverse engineering.' An analyst tracing statutory authority needs both, and needs to keep them distinct: 6802 built the office, 1673 built the whistleblower channel, and Section 6803 built the audit.

Now the language. Subsection (j)(1)(A) requires the Director of AARO, within 540 days of 23 December 2022, to submit to the congressional defense committees, the congressional intelligence committees and congressional leadership 'a written report detailing the historical record of the United States Government relating to unidentified anomalous phenomena,' drawing on intelligence community records and documents, oral history interviews, open source analysis, interviews of current and former Government officials, and 'classified and unclassified national archives including any records any third party obtained pursuant to section 552 of title 5.' Subsection (j)(1)(B)(i) sets the period: 'beginning on January 1, 1945' and running to completion.

Then (j)(1)(B)(ii), the operative demand. The report shall 'include a compilation and itemization of the key historical record of the involvement of the intelligence community with unidentified anomalous phenomena, including, (I) any program or activity that was protected by restricted access that has not been explicitly and clearly reported to Congress; (II) successful or unsuccessful efforts to identify and track unidentified anomalous phenomena; and (III) any efforts to obfuscate, manipulate public opinion, hide, or otherwise provide incorrect unclassified or classified information about unidentified anomalous phenomena or related activities.'

Subparagraph (I) is a statutory recognition that unreported restricted-access programs may exist, which is precisely the waived-SAP and Restricted Data territory that the Information Security Oversight Office cannot independently inventory and that the 10 U.S.C. 119(e) waiver channel narrows to four members of Congress. Subparagraph (III) is a statutory recognition that a disinformation effort may exist, and it uses the word 'obfuscate' and the phrase 'manipulate public opinion' in the United States Code. Congress does not legislate against hypotheticals in these terms.

Subsection (j)(2) removed the obvious dodge in advance: 'The Archivist of the United States shall make available to the Office such information maintained by the National Archives and Records Administration, including classified information, as the Director of the Office considers necessary.' No classification barrier at the Archives.

Section 6802 also built the technical apparatus. Subsection (e)(1) directs the Secretary of Defense, coordinating with the DNI, to designate 'one or more line organizations' primarily responsible for scientific, technical and operational analysis of field-investigation data and data from other sources, 'including with respect to the testing of materials, medical studies, and development of theoretical models.' Subsection (e)(2) requires directives ensuring each designated line organization has authority 'to draw on the special expertise of persons outside the Federal Government with appropriate security clearances.' Subsection (g) orders a science plan to develop and test theories accounting for 'characteristics and performance of unidentified anomalous phenomena that exceed the known state of the art in science or technology, including in the areas of propulsion, aerodynamic control, signatures, structures, materials, sensors, countermeasures, weapons, electronics, and power generation,' and to 'provide the foundation for potential future investments to replicate or otherwise better understand any such advanced characteristics and performance.' Replicate. The statute contemplates replication of performance exceeding the known state of the art. Subsection (n)(6) then defines 'line organization' in a way that excludes any component of the Office of the Secretary of Defense, pushing the material testing out to service labs, national labs and agencies where the work would actually be done.

The reporting architecture around it is equally telling. Subsection (l)(4) requires the Director to give the Armed Services and Intelligence committee leadership, every briefing period, 'an enumeration of any instances in which data relating to unidentified anomalous phenomena was not provided to the Office because of classification restrictions on that data or for any other reason.' Congress built a running log of refusals into the statute. And Section 6803 of the same public law, at 136 Stat. 3594, required the Comptroller General within 90 days to identify cleared GAO personnel to audit the historical record report process, with on-site work as appropriate, quarterly findings to AARO, and semiannual verbal briefings to the intelligence committees, the defense committees and congressional leadership.

That is a statute that funds an office, orders it to investigate its own department's concealment history back to 1945, strips the classification defense at the Archives, requires it to log every time it is refused data, and puts an independent auditor over its shoulder. Later Congresses kept tightening: Section 1673(a) of Public Law 119-60, enacted 18 December 2025, ordered the AARO Director within 180 days to make 'an accounting of security classification guides that apply to information used for reports and investigations of unidentified anomalous phenomena,' and Section 1687(b) of Public Law 118-31 barred UAP-related independent research and development costs from being allowable as indirect contract expenses unless the material is made available to the defense and intelligence committees and congressional leadership, a direct strike at the mechanism by which contractor-held UAP work could be billed to the taxpayer without congressional visibility.

Section 6802 is the hook because everything downstream hangs from it: AARO's existence, the historical record report, the line-organization material testing, the classification-guide accounting, and the statutory vocabulary, 'restricted access,' 'not explicitly and clearly reported to Congress,' 'obfuscate', that the rest of the disclosure fight is conducted in.

### What it has denied

Section 6802 contains no denial; it is the instrument that compels one. Its significance for the concealment record is that any AARO historical record report issued under 50 U.S.C. 3373(j) that fails to itemize unreported restricted-access programs or obfuscation efforts is a dated, signed statutory filing by a named Director, made under a mandate that expressly contemplated finding both, which converts a null finding from an absence of evidence into an attributable representation to Congress.

Roles the board assigns it: oversight, disclosure, research.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether the historical record report required by 50 U.S.C. 3373(j) was delivered within the 540-day statutory deadline and in how many volumes; the delivery record was not examined for this dossier.
- Which 'line organizations' were designated under 50 U.S.C. 3373(e)(1) for material testing, medical studies and theoretical modelling, the names are required to be reported under 3373(k)(1)(B)(xvi) but have not been verified here.
- How many enumerations of withheld data have been provided to the committees under 50 U.S.C. 3373(l)(4), and what they contain.
- What the Comptroller General's Section 6803 audit found, and whether any of the semiannual verbal briefings produced a written product.
- Whether the nondisclosure order sweep required by 50 U.S.C. 3373b(b)(3)(A) was in fact conducted by all covered agencies and contractors, and how many instruments were produced to AARO.
- How many 72-hour congressional notifications under 50 U.S.C. 3373b(a)(4)(B) have been made, each one would be a formal statement that an unreported restricted-access program was implicated.
- Whether any contractor has had UAP-related IRAD costs disallowed under Section 1687(b) of Public Law 118-31, which would be a documented dollar figure attached to contractor UAP work.
- Whether the security classification guide accounting required by Section 1673(a) of Public Law 119-60 was completed by its June 2026 deadline and whether a consolidated classification matrix was issued.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 68 of 1057, score 70). 7 connections, 6 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A0/D0.

### Its strongest connections

- Sec. 6802, James M. Inhofe NDAA for FY2023 (Pub. L. 117-263) - AARO establishment, Historical Record Report and annual reports runs out to **All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office (AARO)** (institution, board tier A), a programmatic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: establishes the office. Sec. 6802 does not create AARO alongside anything: it amends section 1683 of the FY2022 NDAA (50 U.S.C. 3373) to read as a new section establishing the office. 'Not later than 120 days after the date of the enactment of the Intelligence Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2023, the Secretary of Defense, in coordination with the Director of National Intelligence, shall establish an office ... to carry out the duties of the Unidentified Aerial Phenomena Task Force, as in effect on December 26, 2021', and 'The office established under paragraph (1) shall be known as the "All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office"'. The reporting chain is set in the same subsection: the Director reports directly to the Deputy Secretary of Defense and the Principal Deputy Director of National Intelligence, and to USD(I&S) on administrative matters only. Read from the enacted text on govinfo, 22 August 2026. Sources: Pub. L. 117-263 (James M. Inhofe NDAA for FY2023) sec. 6802, enacted text read from govinfo on 22 August 2026, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-117publ263/html/PLAW-117publ263.htm, dated 2026-08-22.

## Wayne State University

Wayne State University is a institution on the board; board tier C, contextual, real and relevant, no direct programme linkage on the record yet; dates no dates recorded; axes C0 A0 D0; 7 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Academic.

### What it is

Wayne State University is a public research university in midtown Detroit whose School of Medicine operates jointly with the Detroit Medical Center, a large urban academic hospital system. Inside that medical school sat a small, defense- and intelligence-facing neuroscience unit called the Emergent Technology Research Division, built around functional neuroimaging and cognitive-workload measurement. That division, not the university's public research portfolio, is the reason Wayne State appears on this board.

### Why it is implicated

Four of the thirty-eight Defense Intelligence Reference Documents commissioned under the DIA's Advanced Aerospace Weapon System Applications program are attributed on the government-derived DIRD list to two Wayne State men: Christopher C. "Kit" Green and Richard J. Genik II. Green is a career CIA analyst (1969-1985) who came to Wayne State as executive director for emergent technology research for the School of Medicine and Detroit Medical Center, and then as assistant dean; Genik directed the Emergent Technology Research Division that Green's role had seeded, and the two had already co-authored on "cognitive avionics" for spaceflight crews in 2005. Green's DIRD is the human-effects paper, the single AAWSAP deliverable about injured bodies rather than propulsion, and it states outright that clinical diagnosis of injured witnesses can be used to reverse-engineer the craft that hurt them. That makes a named academic medical centre in Detroit a node in the custody chain for injury data on people harmed by anomalous craft, and it makes the university's silence about that work the relevant fact. Wayne State has never issued any public acknowledgement of the DIA contract deliverables written by its faculty, and Green himself has since recast the entire enterprise as private consulting done outside any institutional review structure.

### The longer account

The program reached into universities, and Wayne State is where it reached into a hospital.

The entry point is Christopher C. Green. His career is the point: senior division analyst and assistant national intelligence officer for science and technology at CIA from 1969 to 1985, then General Motors, then Wayne State. The National Research Council's own 2008 biography of him, published while he chaired a committee on military and intelligence methodology for emergent neurophysiological research, records that immediately prior to becoming assistant dean he was "executive director for emergent technology research for the SOM/DMC" and that he held clinical appointments in the Departments of Diagnostic Radiology and of Psychiatry and Behavioral Neurosciences of the School of Medicine and the Detroit Medical Center. Note what that means. A former CIA science-and-technology officer was running the emergent-technology research function of a large American medical school, with hospital-side appointments in the two departments that give access to brain imaging and to psychiatric case material.

By 2009 the unit had a name and a successor. Wayne State's own School of Medicine news office described Richard J. Genik II as "director of the Emergent Technology Research Division and assistant professor of the Department of Psychiatry and Behavioral Neurosciences," announcing that the U.S. Army had picked him as one of fifteen experts to direct two decades of Army neuroscience investment, and that he briefed the Pentagon, the Board of Army Science and Technology, the Army Research Lab and Training and Doctrine Command. The university publicised that. It has never publicised what came next.

What came next was AAWSAP. The DIRD list attributes to Genik three papers and to Green one. Genik's three are all verifiable in the released record: "Technological Approaches to Controlling External Devices in the Absence of Limb-Operated Interfaces" (DIA-08-1003-012, 23 March 2010), "Cognitive Limits on Simultaneous Control of Multiple Unmanned Spacecraft" (DIA-08-1101-001, 15 December 2010, ICOD 8 September 2010, issued in the "Defense Futures" series), and a quantum-computing paper dated 10 December 2010. Read together they are not miscellaneous: brain-machine control of external devices, cognitive ceilings on piloting multiple craft at once, and molecular computing. That is a specification sheet for operating something you do not fly with your hands.

Green's contribution is the one that matters most. "Anomalous Acute and Subacute Field Effects on Human Biological Tissues," dated 11 March 2010 with an information cut-off of 1 December 2009, produced under the FY 2009 AAWSA program in the "Acquisition Threat Support" series, opens with three antenna engineers who suffered erythema, fever, hair loss, blood dyscrasia and, in one case, "signs of malignant transformations" after a near-field exposure centred at about 785 MHz. Its summary is the load-bearing sentence in the whole DIRD corpus: the paper "relates, summarizes, and analyzes evidence of unintended injury to human observers by anomalous advanced aerospace systems," and argues "that the subsequent work can inform (e.g., reverse engineer), through clinical diagnoses, certain physical characteristics of possible future advanced aerospace systems from unknown provenance." Later it states: "It is our contention that characteristics of the fields or mechanisms associated with close encounters with anomalous craft can even sometimes carefully be gleaned from archival records where effects on human physiology have been carefully reported." Its appendices draw on the Schuessler catalog of UFO-related human physiological effects and on Vallee's close-encounter typology. The internal references cite "Green C. 2009 & 1983 (Unpublished Personal Archives)" and "Green, CC 2001, 2009 Unpublished HIPPA files", a defence intelligence reference document resting, in part, on one physician's private patient archive.

The custody problem is exactly there. The released PDFs redact every author name under FOIA exemption (b)(6); the attribution to Wayne State comes from the DIRD list, not from the documents. Green, interviewed in April 2022, said he was a consultant to AAWSAP facilitated by BAASS, that there were no control groups and no institutional review board because he was doing "case studies" and not research, that a client gave him a grant he would not name, and that DIA reviewers objected to his use of the word UFO and had him change the wording. So the human-effects paper commissioned by a defence intelligence agency was written by a Wayne State assistant dean, drew on patient material he holds personally, passed through no university IRB, and was scrubbed of the word that described what it was about. The institution's name is on the government's list; the institution's oversight machinery never touched the work; and the institution has said nothing since. That is not an accident of paperwork. It is how the program used a university: for the credential and the clinical access, not for the accountability.

### What it has denied

Wayne State University has issued no public acknowledgement of the DIA/AAWSAP deliverables written by its School of Medicine personnel; no press release, statement or research disclosure naming the contract has been located, in contrast to the university's 2009 publicity for Genik's Army neuroscience work. Green's own posture is displacement rather than denial: he characterises the human-effects paper as private consulting and personal case studies conducted outside any university review structure, and declines to name the funder. The DIA's standing line that DIRDs are contractor-produced theoretical papers that do not represent agency positions performs the same function for the government side.

Roles the board assigns it: research, custody, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- No evidence was found that Green ever held a named or endowed chair at Wayne State. The verifiable titles are assistant dean, adjunct/clinical professorial appointments in Diagnostic Radiology and Psychiatry and Behavioral Neurosciences, and earlier executive director for emergent technology research. Any claim of a 'chair' for anomalous-exposure research should be treated as unsupported until a Wayne State appointment letter or catalogue entry surfaces.
- The exact start and end dates of Green's Wayne State appointments are not established in the sourced record; the 2008 NRC biography is the firm anchor, and his status in 2009-2010 when the DIRD was written is inferred rather than documented.
- Whether any part of the AAWSAP work flowed through Wayne State accounts, contracts, facilities or the Emergent Technology Research Division's budget is unresolved. Green describes personal consulting; no subcontract, subaward or effort-reporting document has surfaced either way.
- Whether Genik's three DIRDs were written under university affiliation or, like Green's, as private consulting is not established. He is attributed to Wayne State on the DIRD list, but no contract instrument has been located.
- The document number on the human-effects DIRD is illegible in the released scan, so the paper is cited here by title and date only. The quantum-computing DIRD's number OCRs ambiguously and is likewise cited by date.
- Whether Wayne State's institutional review board ever reviewed, or was ever asked to review, any of the clinical or imaging work Green describes conducting on 'experiencers' in later years.
- The identity of 'Morris' in the DIRD's Appendix B 'Green/Morris' ICD-coding exercise, and whether that collaborator was a Wayne State colleague.
- Whether the Emergent Technology Research Division still exists at Wayne State, when it was wound down or renamed, and where its records went.
- Whether Wayne State has ever received a FOIA, records request or press inquiry about the DIA work, and what it said in response.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded C on a global pass (rank 909 of 1057, score 15). 7 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A0/D0.

## Senado Federal do Brasil

Senado Federal do Brasil is a institution on the board; board tier C, contextual, real and relevant, no direct programme linkage on the record yet; dates no dates recorded; axes C0 A0 D0; 6 connections; in the research band.

In one line: Foreign legislature.

### What it is

The Senado Federal is the upper chamber of Brazil's National Congress. On 16 March 2022 it received Requerimento no 193, de 2022 (RQS 193/2022), tabled by Senator Eduardo Girao (PODEMOS/CE) with seven co-signatories, and approved it the same day; the resulting special plenary session was held on 24 June 2022. The requerimento's stated purpose was to hold a special session commemorating the 75th anniversary of World Ufology Day, and its justificacao entered into the Senate's permanent record a detailed account of Brazil's official UFO programmes and archive holdings.

### Why it is implicated

The Senate node is where the correction lives, and correcting it makes the comparison stronger rather than weaker. RQS 193/2022 is not a records-production instrument. Its ementa is a request for a commemorative special session under Article 199 of the Senate's internal rules, and its disposition reads 'Aprovada pelo Plenario' followed by 'SESSAO REALIZADA'. What it did instead is arguably more useful to this board: it wrote into the permanent legislative record of a G20 state, over eight senators' names, that the Aeronautica ran Operacao Prato and SIOANI, that FAB fighters chased 21 objects in 1986, that from 2007 the air force but not the army and not the navy transferred its files, and that the archive reference code BR DFANBSB ARX returns 758 documents. No US congressional instrument has ever put an equivalent set of specifics into the record as settled institutional history. The contrast is not that Brazil compelled disclosure through legislation. It is that Brazil did not have to, because the executive had already published, and the legislature's role was to hold a session about it in the plenary chamber with foreign witnesses at the rostrum.

### The longer account

The premise that most needs correcting before this node is usable is the shape of the instrument. RQS 193/2022 is a requerimento, a procedural request, filed under Article 199 of the Regimento Interno do Senado Federal, asking for a special session at an appropriate date to commemorate 75 years of World Ufology Day. It carries the signatures of Eduardo Girao (PODEMOS/CE), Eliziane Gama (CIDADANIA/MA), Alessandro Vieira (CIDADANIA/SE), Izalci Lucas (PSDB/DF), Jorge Kajuru (PODEMOS/GO), Marcos do Val (PODEMOS/ES), Paulo Rocha (PT/PA) and Reguffe (PODEMOS/DF), which is a cross-party spread from left to right. It was read and approved on 16 March 2022, published in the Diario do Senado Federal no 32 of 17 March 2022 at pages 269 to 275 and page 62, the session was held on 24 June 2022 and published in DSF no 99 of 25 June 2022 at pages 7 to 67, and the matter was closed and sent to the archive coordination on 29 June 2022. It compelled nothing and produced no documents. Any claim on this board that the Senate passed an instrument seeking UAP records must be retired.

What survives, and what makes the node worth keeping, is the justificacao. Brazilian requerimentos carry a reasoning section that enters the permanent record with the instrument, and Girao's runs to several pages of institutional history. It states that Brazil was the first nation to officially admit that OVNIs exist and have extraterrestrial provenance, at a 1954 session at the Escola Superior de Guerra in Rio at which the then Aeronautica captain Joao Adil de Oliveira spoke to officers, civilians and press, and that France followed only in 1976. It states that since 1954 the Brazilian government has created several official UFO research programmes through the Aeronautica, naming Operacao Prato in 1977, SIOANI in 1969 at the IV Comando Aereo Regional in Sao Paulo, and the 1986 night in which 21 spherical objects, each given as 100 metres in diameter, were pursued for hours by Brazilian jet fighters. It states that the Aeronautica was always discreet about these facts but never denied them when asked. It states that from 2007 the Aeronautica, and expressly not the Exercito and not the Marinha, began sending its papers to the Arquivo Nacional, and that the total now exceeds 20,000 pages. It records that in August 2010 the then FAB commander determined by an instrument published in the Diario Oficial da Uniao that all OVNI cases across the force be concentrated at COMDABRA for onward transmission. It records the April 2013 meeting at the Ministry of Defence, convened at the invitation of then Foreign Minister Celso Amorim and held before the service commanders, at which researchers pressed for the Army and Navy files. And it gives the archive arithmetic directly: searching OVNI alone yields 137 registros, but the reference code BR DFANBSB ARX yields 758 documents, some of them more than 70 years old.

The justificacao also turns outward, and that is where the comparison it enables becomes explicit. It notes that at least 30 nations established research commissions, that the 2021 Pentagon UAP preliminary assessment examined 144 sighting cases of which only one was explained, and that on 15 March 2022 President Biden signed an omnibus funding bill including 782.5 billion dollars for defence with resources reserved for unexplained aerial phenomena research legislated within the FY2022 National Defense Authorization Act. Brazil, in other words, put the American record into its own legislative record while noting that its own files were already open.

The session itself, on 24 June 2022, was held in the Senate plenary and is documented in the Senate's own file with individual presentation documents lodged for each invited speaker: Rony Vernet, Ademar Jose, Gary Heseltine, Geraldo Lemos Neto, Inajar Antonio Kurowski, Jackson Luiz Camargo, Robert Lambert Salas and Thiago Ticchetti. Agencia Senado's report of the session records Gevaerd calling the opening incomplete but fruitful, Robert Salas, a former US Air Force officer, describing unidentified objects disabling ten nuclear weapons at a launch facility he commanded, and the delivery to Girao at the close of a document the participants called the Carta de Brasilia, recommending creation of a permanent joint civil and military commission to enable organised research on the subject.

One error must be named and buried. A previous pass on this board asserted that Senator Girao brought the 1996 Varginha case to Capitol Hill in 2026. Nothing in the reporting located supports that. Girao's verified, documented action is RQS 193/2022 and the 24 June 2022 special session, and the board should carry that and nothing beyond it.

### What it has denied

None on the part of the Senate. The chamber's posture is the inverse of denial: it approved a plenary session on the subject unanimously enough to be recorded simply as 'Aprovada pelo Plenario', gave the floor to foreign and domestic witnesses including a former US Air Force officer, and entered into its permanent Diario the specific archive reference code under which its own military's files can be retrieved.

Roles the board assigns it: oversight, disclosure, research.

### Open questions on this node

- A previous research pass asserted that Senator Girao brought the 1996 Varginha case to Capitol Hill in 2026. No source located supports this. It should be treated as retracted unless a primary record is produced.
- No Brazilian legislative instrument has been located that compels production of Army or Navy holdings, establishes a records review board, or creates the permanent joint commission proposed in the Carta de Brasilia.
- The text of the Carta de Brasilia itself has not been retrieved; its content is known only through Agencia Senado's summary.
- Whether any of the eight co-signatory senators pursued follow-on action after the session closed on 29 June 2022 is not established.
- The full stenographic record of the 24 June 2022 session is published in DSF no 99, pages 7 to 67, but has not been read in this pass; individual statements beyond those summarised by Agencia Senado are unverified.
- Several factual assertions inside the justificacao, notably the 100-metre diameter figure for the 1986 objects and the primacy claim for Brazil in 1954, are the author's assertions entered into the record, not independently verified findings.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded C on a global pass (rank 714 of 1057, score 22). 6 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A0/D0.

- Senado Federal do Brasil comes in from **Brasilia, Federal District, Brazil** (location, board tier C), a geographic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: The Senado Federal sits in Brasília. RQS 193/2022 also records the 2005 visit by ufologists to CINDACTA and COMDABRA 'em Brasília' to inspect the documents, the capital is where both the archive and the legislature that pressed on it are located. These edges assert what the document NAMES, which is verifiable in its text and unaffected by its procedural nature, so the tier is unchanged. - Senado Federal do Brasil runs out to **Hospital Regional de Varginha** (institution, board tier C), a investigative link at Tier 2 named person on the record. The link reads: Girao backed Varginha hospital witnesses in Washington. Sen. Eduardo Girao, who presided over the 2022 Brazilian Senate UAP session, travelled to Washington for the 20 January 2026 National Press Club event and praised the Varginha witnesses, and discussed a joint Brazil-US evidence effort with Rep. Eric Burlison. The Debrief is explicit that Girao acted as a private citizen and was not officially representing the Brazilian Senate, so this is a personal rather than institutional linkage.
